arXiv:2610.36078v1 [stat.ML] 26 Oct 2026
Learning on-the-fly quasi-stationarity diagnostics from low-dimensional surrogates
Noé Blassel
Abstract
Accelerated dynamics methods, such as parallel replica dynamics, require estimates of the decorrelation time, after which a process trapped in one of its metastable states is distributed according to the quasi-stationary distribution (QSD).
In a method proposed by Binder, Lelièvre and Simpson, decorrelation times are defined by coupling a particle approximation of the non-linear conditioned probability flow of the process with MCMC-inspired diagnostics.
We explore an alternative to MCMC diagnostics, based on coarse-graining of non-linear probability flows and simulation-based inference.
We first develop a coarse-graining framework for killed Markov jump processes conditioned on survival, proving uniform-in-time, non-asymptotic estimates approximating the non-linear probability flow of
the true process by the non-linear flow of a surrogate process, defined with a collective variable. The framework does not assume reversibility of the true process, and also gives bounds which scale explicitly in an adiabatic regime.
Motivated by these results, we then construct a quasi-stationarity diagnostic using simulation-based inference: a recurrent neural network (RNN), trained on particle approximations of non-linear effective flows, estimates posterior probabilities of decorrelation. The learned diagnostic is then transferred to collective-variable traces of high-dimensional particle approximations.
We investigate the behavior and robustness of this diagnostic on a set of numerical experiments, comparing it to the MCMC diagnostic of Binder et al. on both synthetic examples and a molecular system, in and out of its training prior distribution.
When its prior assumptions hold, the RNN diagnostic outperforms the MCMC diagnostic in ROC analysis, is less conservative, fails less often, and has an interpretable decision threshold.
Keywords: metastability, quasi-stationary distributions, parallel replica algorithm, convergence diagnostics, effective dynamics, simulation-based inference
1 Introduction
Metastability is a common feature in the stochastic dynamics of complex systems (in this work described by a Markov process XX), which has been observed and analyzed for dynamical models in a variety of fields: protein folding [36], defect propagation in materials science [55], turbulent flows [8], climate models [23], dynamics of ecosystems [44], and collective dynamics of neurons [47].
In its own right, it has become a rich topic in the analysis of stochastic processes [20, 9, 29].
This phenomenon can be broadly described as follows: due to the existence of nearly-invariant subsets of the configuration space, the dynamics XX remains trapped in these so-called metastable states, leading to a timescale separation between within-state fluctuations and between-states transitions.
As XX may also be stiff (as in molecular dynamics simulations [2]), the characteristic timescales of state-to-state transitions and the time step used to numerically integrate the dynamics are separated by many orders of magnitude.
Metastability therefore poses a fundamental computational challenge to the simulation of long trajectories needed to estimate dynamical properties, which are key in predicting emergent behavior. In molecular dynamics (MD), this challenge is known as the timescale problem.
Starting in 1997, Arthur Voter and his collaborators developed a family of numerical methods, so-called accelerated molecular dynamics (AMD) algorithms [57, 56, 58, 54, 51], aimed at the simulation of long trajectories for MD models of materials.
Rigorous justifications for all of these methods rely on a local description of metastability, organized around the notion of quasi-stationary distribution [42].
1.1 Local description of metastability
As XX remains trapped for progressively long times in a metastable subset 𝒳\mathcal{X} of the configuration space, its time-marginal distributions converge (under suitable assumptions) to a local equilibrium within 𝒳\mathcal{X}, which we denote νX∈𝒫⁡(𝒳)\nu^{X}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}).
This local equilibrium, which describes the long-time distribution of XX, conditioned on staying trapped in 𝒳\mathcal{X}, is known as the Yaglom limit.
Under general conditions on (X,𝒳)(X,\mathcal{X}) (see [12, 3]), this limit can be shown to be unique and independent of the initial distribution of XX. Moreover, whenever the Yaglom limit exists, it is a quasi-stationary distribution (QSD). Formally, these two properties can be written as
ηtX:=Law⁡(Xt|τX>t)→t→∞νX,∀A⊂𝒳,ℙνX​(Xt∈A|τX>t)=νX​(A),\eta_{t}^{X}:=\mathrm{Law}\left(X_{t}\,\middle|\,\tau^{X}>t\right)\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{}\nu^{X},\qquad\forall\,A\subset\mathcal{X},\,\quad\mathbb{P}_{\nu^{X}}\left(X_{t}\in A\,\middle|\,\tau^{X}>t\right)=\nu^{X}(A),
(1)
where τX\tau^{X} denotes the exit time of XX from 𝒳\mathcal{X}, η0X∈𝒫⁡(𝒳)\eta_{0}^{X}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}) is the initial distribution, and AA is supposed measurable. A fundamental property of the QSD concerns the exit distribution of XX from 𝒳\mathcal{X}, when X0X_{0} is distributed according to νX\nu^{X}:
∀A⊂∂𝒳,ℙνX​(τX>t,XτX∈A)=e−λX​t​ℙνX​(XτX∈A),\forall\,A\subset\partial\mathcal{X},\qquad\mathbb{P}_{\nu^{X}}\left(\tau^{X}>t,X_{\tau^{X}}\in\,A\right)=\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}t}\mathbb{P}_{\nu^{X}}\left(X_{\tau^{X}}\in\,A\right),
(2)
meaning that the exit time τX\tau^{X} is exponentially distributed (with rate λX>0\lambda^{X}>0) and independent of the exit point XτXX_{\tau^{X}}.
QSDs were introduced by Yaglom [59] to study the pre-extinction behavior of certain critical branching processes, but the notion has proved useful in population and molecular dynamics, among other fields.
We refer to [42, 14] for an introduction to QSDs and their main properties, as well as [12, 3] for existence results in a large class of Markov processes.
1.2 Accelerated simulation of metastable dynamics
AMD methods rely on the crucial property (2) to efficiently sample long, unbiased trajectories of the system, and on an informal level can be understood as the following simple three-step procedure: i) wait for XX to reach a local-equilibrium within some metastable state 𝒳\mathcal{X} (i.e. for the limit in (1) to be realized), ii) sample (τX,XτX)(\tau^{X},X_{\tau^{X}}) efficiently using the property (2), iii) continue the simulation from XτXX_{\tau^{X}}, incrementing the simulation clock by τX\tau^{X}, until another metastable state is reached, etc.
Unbiasedness of the sequence of visited metastable states is guaranteed by the independence property in (2), if samples in step ii) are exact. As a counterpart, details of the dynamics in the quasi-stationary portion of the trajectory, between convergence to the QSD and exit from the state, are sacrificed.
To efficiently sample the exit event, hyperdynamics [57] and temperature-accelerated dynamics [51] rely on physical approximations of the exit distribution (2), using transition state theory (TST) and harmonic transition state theory (HTST) respectively, see [26] and references therein. As such, they are only valid in specific physical regimes, which are relevant for solid-state materials applications.
The parallel replica algorithm [58], however, is more general, and relies only on the property (2) to accelerate step ii) in the following way. A number N≥1N\geq 1 of independent samples from νX\nu^{X} are drawn, and simulated in parallel until the first replica exits. The property (2) then implies the equality
(N​τminX,XτminX(min))​=in law​(τX,XτX),\left(N\tau^{X}_{\min},X_{\tau^{X}_{\min}}^{(\min)}\right)\overset{\text{in law}}{=}\left(\tau^{X},X_{\tau^{X}}\right),
(3)
where τminX\tau_{\min}^{X} denotes the minimum exit time across all replicas, and X(min)X^{(\min)} the first replica to exit. Provided the replicas can be simulated with perfect parallel efficiency, this procedure leads to an expected speedup of a factor NN in simulation time, for a given trajectory length.
The only approximation in the parallel replica algorithm is a time-truncation in the Yaglom limit, which makes it applicable in principle to a large class of metastable processes.
The numerical analysis of this algorithm was initiated in [37, 50]. For a perspective on implementation aspects, variants and challenges, we refer to [46].
1.3 Challenges in AMD methods
Despite their apparent simplicity (and generality, in the case of the parallel replica method), AMD methods have not been widely applied beyond materials science.
This can be explained, at least in part, by the fact that AMD users must provide two key parameters, which have natural and suitable definitions for materials, but few beyond this setting.
These are i) definitions of the metastable states 𝒳\mathcal{X} and ii) definitions of valid decorrelation times tcorr=tcorr​(𝒳)>0t_{\rm corr}=t_{\rm corr}(\mathcal{X})>0 within each state, to be used as truncation parameters in Yaglom’s limit (1).
Regarding problem i), natural definitions of 𝒳\mathcal{X}, when XX is a small-noise perturbation of a deterministic dynamical system, are available as positively invariant subsets of the deterministic process (e.g. basins of attraction of some fixed point).
Recent mathematical works [5, 6] have also proposed optimizing the shape of 𝒳\mathcal{X} to directly increase the efficiency of the parallel replica algorithm. Beyond these, many practical simulation methods still rely on hand-crafted, ad-hoc definitions of 𝒳\mathcal{X}.
Every definition of the decorrelation time to address problem ii) relies, implicitly or explicitly, on a quantitative version of Yaglom’s limit, typically in the form
∀t≥0,dTV​(ηtX,νX)≤C⁡(𝒳,η0X)​e−γX​t,\forall\,t\geq 0,\qquad\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}\left(\eta_{t}^{X},\nu^{X}\right)\leq C(\mathcal{X},\eta_{0}^{X})\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}t},
(4)
where C⁡(𝒳,η0X)>0C(\mathcal{X},\eta_{0}^{X})>0 depends on the definition of the metastable state and the initial distribution η0X\eta_{0}^{X} of XX within 𝒳\mathcal{X}, but is independent of time. The constant γX>0\gamma^{X}>0 is called a spectral gap, for reasons which will become clear in Section 2, and dTV\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV} is the total variation distance (also defined below, see Equation (23)).
We refer to [12] and [3, Theorem 1.8] for sufficient conditions on (X,𝒳)(X,\mathcal{X}) for (4) to hold.
Assuming this convergence holds, various solutions to problem ii) have been proposed.
∘\circ 
Physical heuristics. Many implementations of the parallel replica method rely on 𝒳\mathcal{X}-independent definitions of tcorrt_{\rm corr}, based on domain expertise and validated a posteriori by checking that the exit statistics satisfy (2). This includes the original implementations [58, 54] where 1≤tcorr≤2​ps1\leq t_{\rm corr}\leq 2\,{\rm ps}. Slightly more principled, but still heuristic, definitions rely, for small-noise diffusions, on linearizations [46] of the dynamics around some stable equilibrium x0∈𝒳x_{0}\in\mathcal{X}, whenever 𝒳\mathcal{X} is the basin of attraction of x0x_{0} for the zero-noise dynamics. The closed form of this linear process is then used to define tcorr​(x0)=1/γlinXt_{\rm corr}(x_{0})=1/\gamma^{X}_{\rm lin}, where γlinX\gamma^{X}_{\rm lin} is the spectral gap of this linearization.
∘\circ 
Quantitative spectral asymptotics. When XX is a reversible diffusion process and 𝒳\mathcal{X} is bounded, γX=λ2​(𝒳)−λ1​(𝒳)\gamma^{X}=\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{X})-\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{X}) is the spectral gap of the infinitesimal generator of XX, endowed with homogeneous Dirichlet boundary conditions on ∂𝒳\partial\mathcal{X} [37, Proposition 6]. In the low-temperature regime, it is possible to obtain precise, 𝒳\mathcal{X}-dependent estimates γX∼γscX​(𝒳)\gamma^{X}\sim\gamma^{X}_{\rm{sc}}(\mathcal{X}) (see [5, Corollary 6]), leading to a principled definition tcorr​(𝒳):=1/γscX​(𝒳)t_{\rm corr}(\mathcal{X}):=1/\gamma^{X}_{\rm sc}(\mathcal{X}). The subscript stands for “semiclassical” in allusion to the mathematical tools involved in the proofs of these results.
∘\circ 
Operator learning. These physically-inspired definitions are invalid in glassy landscapes encountered in biochemical systems or materials with defects, where metastable states often contain many energy minima separated by shallow barriers [46]. A recent data-driven alternative [41] relies on trajectory data to directly estimate γX=λ2​(𝒳)−λ1​(𝒳)\gamma^{X}=\lambda_{2}(\mathcal{X})-\lambda_{1}(\mathcal{X}) in a Koopman operator-learning framework, adapted to the case of Dirichlet boundary conditions. This again leads to the definition tcorr​(𝒳):=1/γ^Xt_{\rm corr}(\mathcal{X}):=1/\widehat{\gamma}^{X} for reversible processes, where γ^X\widehat{\gamma}^{X} is the spectral gap of the Koopman approximation.
∘\circ 
MCMC diagnostics. This approach, proposed in [4], relies on a somewhat different philosophy. It combines a particle approximation to the conditioned time-marginal ηtX\eta_{t}^{X} (in the form of a Fleming–Viot process [19], see Definition 9 below), and so-called convergence diagnostics from the Markov chain Monte Carlo literature (see [49] for an overview of this important topic) to detect stationarity in this particle approximation.
The first three approaches are offline definitions, where the decorrelation time is a deterministic parameter in the algorithm (albeit a learned parameter in the operator learning approach), and the fourth is an online or on-the-fly definition, in which tcorrt_{\rm corr} is a random variable whose value is determined dynamically from the simulation itself (in [4], tcorrt_{\rm corr} can be understood as a stopping time of the Fleming–Viot particle approximation).
On-the-fly definitions are a promising direction to free the parallel replica method from the user-defined parameter tcorrt_{\rm corr}, and therefore make it applicable in a wider class of metastable systems.
The first and so far only implementation of this online approach within the parallel replica method was proposed in [4] (giving the so-called generalized parallel replica method), and relies on a diagnostic inspired by the Gelman–Rubin convergence test [22] (see (137) below). The method was tested on several biochemical systems in [28].
Another attractive feature of on-the-fly definitions is their sensitivity to the initial condition η0X\eta_{0}^{X}, through the initial-condition-dependent prefactor C⁡(𝒳,η0X)C(\mathcal{X},\eta_{0}^{X}) in (4). Offline methods, by contrast, which rely on some estimate of the spectral gap γX\gamma^{X}, necessarily neglect this contribution.
It was observed in [28] that setting the decorrelation time uniformly in the initial condition was in many cases suboptimal.
The use of MCMC convergence diagnostics (which should really be understood as nonconvergence diagnostics) as a quasi-stationary convergence diagnostic (or more simply, quasi-stationarity diagnostic) is however heuristic, and just as in the case of physical heuristics, has to be validated a posteriori.
Moreover, it relies on a user-defined scalar decision threshold α\alpha. As this threshold implicitly defines tcorrt_{\rm corr}, the method is not strictly speaking tcorrt_{\rm corr}-free, but replaces one parameter by another. The question then becomes whether an appropriate value of α\alpha can be selected for many systems at once.
More critically, the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic proposed in [4] is prone to being conservative (we provide some concurring numerical evidence in Section 4), which poses a challenge for applications to AMD.
Indeed, overestimating tcorrt_{\rm corr} leads to wasted acceleration in the parallel replica method, by allocating unnecessary computation to simulating quasi-stationary portions of the trajectory.
On the other hand, a systematically lax diagnostic will introduce bias in the distribution of exit events (2), and consequently in the long-time kinetics of sampled trajectories.
This efficiency-bias tradeoff is clearly identified in the literature on parallel replica methods [37, 46, 4, 6].
1.4 Contributions of this work and outline
We explore alternatives to MCMC-inspired diagnostics to define on-the-fly decorrelation times. Our method uses simulation-based inference [15] of decorrelation times, over a prior ensemble of low-dimensional surrogate processes.
In the course of developing our method, we make the following contributions.
∘\circ 
We develop a coarse-graining framework for the non-linear probability flows ηtX\eta_{t}^{X} of killed Markov jump processes conditioned on non-absorption. For the case of finitely many states, we give the first estimates, non-asymptotic in time, on the evolution of ηtX\eta_{t}^{X} (the conditional law in (1) and (4)) in terms of a well-chosen effective flow ηtZ\eta_{t}^{Z} (Theorem 5).
The framework, importantly, makes no assumption on the reversibility of XX. This effective description then allows one to prove approximate convergence in the Yaglom limit (4) from convergence in the coarse-grained Yaglom limit of ηtZ\eta_{t}^{Z} (Corollary 6).
∘\circ 
We identify an adiabatic regime in which the estimates of Theorem 5 and Corollary 6 hold with explicit rates (Theorem 7 and Corollary 8).
This regime corresponds to a slow-fast decomposition of the infinitesimal generator of XX.
∘\circ 
We use these theoretical results to motivate using a surrogate process ZZ in a simulation-based Bayesian inference framework for quasi-stationarity diagnostics, in which the surrogate process ZZ is treated as a nuisance parameter (Section 3.2) in the sense of [21, Section 3.1]. This approach leads us to define tcorrt_{\rm corr} as a stopping time for a Fleming–Viot approximation of ηtX\eta_{t}^{X} (as in [4]), defined using a recurrent neural network (RNN) rather than an MCMC-inspired diagnostic.
The underlying assumptions are explicitly stated and discussed, for future extensions and mathematical analysis (Assumptions (B1)–(B4)).
∘\circ 
We prove that the decision threshold α\alpha in our RNN diagnostic has a probabilistic interpretation (Lemmas 13 and 14), and can therefore be tuned a priori under ideal assumptions, rather than validated a posteriori per-system as MCMC-inspired diagnostics require.
∘\circ 
We conduct numerical experiments of increasing complexity, including on a molecular system (Section 4). These experiments suggest that the RNN consistently outperforms the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic when its underlying assumptions hold. We also explore cases where these assumptions do not hold to identify plausible failure modes, as well as a simple mitigation strategy.
The work is organized as follows. In Section 2, we develop our theoretical framework, and present our main theoretical results. In Section 3, we develop our numerical method. In Section 4, we present the results of our experiments. We finally discuss our method and outline some perspectives in Section 5, and dedicate Appendices A and B to the proofs of some technical results and implementation details, respectively.
2 Coarse-graining of killed Markov jump processes
We develop a framework to obtain coarse-grained approximations of killed Markov processes XX, conditioned on non-absorption, at the level of probability flows.
Given a so-called collective variable ξ:𝒳→𝒵\xi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Z} of the full state, our coarse-graining procedure produces another Markov process, ZZ the surrogate process, which is in some sense simpler than XX, and whose conditioned time-marginals approximate those of the non-Markovian process ξ⁡(X)\xi(X).
The problem is closely related to the study of so-called effective dynamics, which are Markovian approximations to the generically non-Markovian processes ξ⁡(X)\xi(X).
The case without killing has been studied extensively in the literature, see for instance [39, 60, 38, 18] and references therein. Our problem is different, due to non-linearities induced by the conditioning at the level of time-marginals. By contrast, probability flows evolve linearly in the case without killing.
Due to this complexity of the non-linear case, we focus as a first step on the case of finite state space jump processes. We suspect our approach can be extended to more general dynamics, but this requires non-trivial technical adaptations for the case of diffusions.
An important feature of the framework is that the reversibility of XX is not assumed (the infinitesimal generator ℒX\mathcal{L}^{X} may be non-self-adjoint for every inner product on ℓ∞​(𝒳)\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}), i.e. detailed balance is not required). We therefore hope to extend in future work the framework to non-gradient diffusions, which are relevant to describe the quasi-stationary behavior of driven systems.
The ultimate goal of this framework is to obtain non-asymptotic estimates on the distance between the conditioned time-marginals of ZZ and those of XX, for the total variation metric. As explained in Section 3, this objective is well-motivated for algorithmic reasons.
We will proceed in two steps, first bounding the difference between the conditional marginals of ZZ and those of ξ⁡(X)\xi(X), then between those of ξ⁡(X)\xi(X) and XX.
Under a formal assumption encoding the intuition that ξ\xi is a slow variable of the system (but only mildly so), we will derive non-asymptotic in time error bounds. These bounds in particular show that the coarse-graining bias is controlled uniformly in time by an approximation parameter encoding a separation of timescales between slow and fast variables.
2.1 Killed Markov jump processes
We consider XX, ZZ two jump processes on finite state spaces 𝒳¯=𝒳∪∂𝒳\overline{\mathcal{X}}=\mathcal{X}\cup\partial^{\mathcal{X}} and 𝒵¯=𝒵∪∂𝒵\overline{\mathcal{Z}}=\mathcal{Z}\cup\partial^{\mathcal{Z}}, where ∂𝒳,∂𝒵\partial^{\mathcal{X}},\partial^{\mathcal{Z}} are so-called killing boundaries.
Conventions used throughout this work.
Given a set SS, we denote by 𝒫⁡(S)\mathcal{P}(S) the set of probability measures on SS. We work in ℓ∞​(𝒳),ℓ∞​(𝒵)\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}),\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}), with the standard norms. All vector and operator norms (be they on 𝒳\mathcal{X} or 𝒵\mathcal{Z}) will be denoted with ∥⋅∥\|\cdot\|, while the dual (ℓ1\ell^{1}) norm on covectors will be denoted by ∥⋅∥∗\|\cdot\|_{*}.
The killed semigroup.
In the following, (Y,𝒴)∈{(X,𝒳),(Z,𝒵)}(Y,\mathcal{Y})\in\left\{(X,\mathcal{X}),(Z,\mathcal{Z})\right\}. The killing time of the process YY is defined as the stopping time
τY=inf{t>0:Yt∈∂𝒴}.\tau^{Y}=\inf\,\left\{t>0:\,Y_{t}\in\partial^{\mathcal{Y}}\right\}.
(5)
We identify ∂𝒴\partial^{\mathcal{Y}} with a unique absorbing state, that is satisfying
ℙ(Yt∈∂𝒴|t≥τY)=1,\mathbb{P}\left(Y_{t}\in\partial^{\mathcal{Y}}\,\middle|\,t\geq\tau^{Y}\right)=1,
(6)
and assume that every non-absorbing state is connected to every other non-absorbing state, and to the boundary:
∀y,y′∈𝒴,y↔y′,y→∂𝒴,\forall\,y,y^{\prime}\in\mathcal{Y},\qquad y\leftrightarrow y^{\prime},\qquad y\rightarrow\partial^{\mathcal{Y}},
(7)
where y→y′y\rightarrow y^{\prime} means ℙy​(Yt=y′)>0\mathbb{P}_{y}\left(Y_{t}=y^{\prime}\right)>0 for some and therefore all t>0t>0, and y↔y′y\leftrightarrow y^{\prime} means y→y′y\rightarrow y^{\prime} and y′→yy^{\prime}\rightarrow y. This condition, which we call conditional irreducibility, ensures in particular that τY\tau^{Y} is almost-surely finite.
Absorption (or killing) at the boundary gives rise to a sub-Markovian evolution, encoded in the following linear semigroup,
∀f∈ℓ∞​(𝒴),QtY​f​(y)=𝔼⁡[f⁡(Yt)​𝟙τY>t|Y0=y],\forall\,f\in\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y}),\qquad Q_{t}^{Y}f(y)=\mathbb{E}\left[f(Y_{t})\mathbbm{1}_{\tau^{Y}>t}\,\middle|\,Y_{0}=y\right],
(8)
the so-called killed semigroup. This semigroup can be seen equivalently as acting on the full state space 𝒴¯\overline{\mathcal{Y}} with Dirichlet boundary conditions on ∂𝒴\partial^{\mathcal{Y}}.
That is, denoting ℒ¯Y\overline{\mathcal{L}}^{Y} the infinitesimal generator of the unconditioned process in 𝒴¯\overline{\mathcal{Y}}, the semigroup QYQ^{Y} is generated by its restriction ℒY\mathcal{L}^{Y} to the space
{f∈ℓ∞​(𝒴¯):f|∂𝒴=0}≅ℓ∞​(𝒴),\left\{f\in\ell^{\infty}(\overline{\mathcal{Y}}):\left.f\right|_{\partial^{\mathcal{Y}}}=0\right\}\cong\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y}),
(9)
which is invariant under the QYQ^{Y}-flow.
Using the notation
η⁡(f)=∫𝒴f⁡(y)​η​(𝑑y)\eta(f)=\int_{\mathcal{Y}}f(y)\,\eta(\mathrm{d}y)
(10)
for η∈𝒫⁡(𝒴)\eta\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}), the law of YtY_{t} conditioned on survival is given for any t≥0t\geq 0 by
ηtY​(f):=𝔼⁡[f⁡(Yt)|τY>t]=η0Y​(QtY​f)η0Y​(QtY​𝟙𝒴),\eta_{t}^{Y}(f):=\mathbb{E}\left[f(Y_{t})\,\middle|\,\tau^{Y}>t\right]=\frac{\eta_{0}^{Y}\left(Q_{t}^{Y}f\right)}{\eta_{0}^{Y}\left(Q_{t}^{Y}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Y}}\right)},
(11)
where Y0∼η0Y∈𝒫⁡(𝒴)Y_{0}\sim\eta_{0}^{Y}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}). The map t↦ηtYt\mapsto\eta_{t}^{Y} defines a non-linear, probability measure-valued flow in 𝒫⁡(𝒴)\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}).
The key tool is the following Feynman–Kac formula, which in our simplified setting is just a reformulation of the backward Kolmogorov equation for the killed process.
For any observable f∈ℓ∞​(𝒴)f\in\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y}), the trajectory u⁡(t):=QtY​f{u(t):=Q_{t}^{Y}f} solves the Cauchy problem
{∂tu⁡(t,y)=ℒY​u​(t,y),(t,y)∈(0,∞)×𝒴,u⁡(0,y)=f⁡(y),y∈𝒴.\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}u(t,y)&=\mathcal{L}^{Y}u(t,y),&(t,y)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathcal{Y},\\
u(0,y)&=f(y),&y\in\mathcal{Y}.\end{aligned}\right.
(12)
The following classical result (see [14, Section 3.1]), which is a consequence of the Perron–Frobenius theorem, will be used extensively in the following.
Proposition 1 (Spectral properties).
For each Y∈{X,Z}Y\in\{X,Z\}, the following holds.
i) 
There exists a unique principal eigentriplet
(λY,νY,hY)∈(0,∞)×𝒫⁡(𝒴)×ℓ∞​(𝒴)\left(\lambda^{Y},\nu^{Y},h^{Y}\right)\in(0,\infty)\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y})
(13)
for the semigroup QYQ^{Y}, i.e. for any η∈𝒫⁡(𝒴)\eta\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y}), f∈ℓ∞​(𝒴)f\in\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y}) and t>0t>0,
νY​(QtY​f)=e−λY​t​νY​(f),η⁡(QtY​hY)=e−λY​t​η​(hY),\nu^{Y}(Q_{t}^{Y}f)=\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{Y}t}\nu^{Y}(f),\qquad\eta(Q_{t}^{Y}h^{Y})=\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{Y}t}\eta(h^{Y}),
(14)
with exponential decay of the semigroup: there exists CλY>0C_{\lambda}^{Y}>0 such that
‖QtY‖≤CλY​e−λY​t.\|Q_{t}^{Y}\|\leq C_{\lambda}^{Y}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{Y}t}.
(15)
ii) 
The eigenvector hY>0h^{Y}>0 satisfies the normalization property
νY​(hY)=1.\nu^{Y}(h^{Y})=1.
(16)
iii) 
Denote by
PY​f=f−νY​(f)νY​(hY)​hYP^{Y}f=f-\frac{\nu^{Y}(f)}{\nu^{Y}(h^{Y})}h^{Y}
(17)
the νY\nu^{Y}-centering projector along the direction hYh^{Y}.
There exists a spectral gap, that is, constants γY,CγY>0\gamma^{Y},C_{\gamma}^{Y}>0 verifying the estimate
‖QtY​PY‖≤CγY​e−(λY+γY)​t.\|Q_{t}^{Y}P^{Y}\|\leq C_{\gamma}^{Y}\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{Y}+\gamma^{Y})t}.
(18)
Differentiating in time the eigenrelations (14) in the first item of Proposition 1, we also obtain, for any f∈ℓ∞​(𝒴)f\in\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{Y}), the generator-level eigenrelation
ℒY​hY=−λY​hY,νY​(ℒY​f)=−λY​νY​(f).\mathcal{L}^{Y}h^{Y}=-\lambda^{Y}h^{Y},\qquad\nu^{Y}(\mathcal{L}^{Y}f)=-\lambda^{Y}\nu^{Y}(f).
(19)
The probability measure νY\nu^{Y} is called the quasi-stationary distribution of the process YY in the metastable state 𝒴\mathcal{Y}.
2.2 Coarse-graining approximation and bias
Let ξ:𝒳→𝒵\xi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Z} be a surjective coarse-graining map.
Whenever f∈ℓ∞​(𝒵)f\in\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}), we denote the pullback through ξ\xi via
ξ⋆​f:=f∘ξ∈ℓ∞​(𝒳),\xi^{\star}f:=f\circ\xi\in\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}),
(20)
and its dual, the pushforward acting on measures η∈𝒫⁡(𝒳)\eta\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}) by
ξ⋆η=η(ξ⋆⋅).\xi_{\star}\eta=\eta(\xi^{\star}\cdot).
(21)
Since we have ξ⁡(𝒳)=𝒵\xi(\mathcal{X})=\mathcal{Z}, we may assume by a suitable extension of ξ\xi to 𝒳¯\overline{\mathcal{X}} that ξ(∂𝒳)=∂𝒵\xi(\partial^{\mathcal{X}})=\partial^{\mathcal{Z}}, so that τX\tau^{X} is a stopping time for ξ∘X\xi\circ X.
For some fixed observable f∈ℓ∞​(𝒵)f\in\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}), our first objective is to control the coarse-graining bias
ξ⋆​ηtX​(f)−ηtZ​(f)\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X}(f)-\eta_{t}^{Z}(f)
(22)
as a function of the time variable tt.
The flow ηtZ\eta_{t}^{Z} is the non-linear flow (11) associated with the Markovian dynamics ZZ. We say it is a non-linear Markovian flow. The flow ξ⋆​ηtX\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X} is the pushforward of the non-linear Markovian flow ηtX\eta_{t}^{X}, but is not itself Markovian.
We say it is a non-linear non-Markovian flow.
Given a measurable space (E,ℰ)(E,\mathcal{E}) and any two probability measures μ,ν∈𝒫⁡(E,ℰ)\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}(E,\mathcal{E}), we recall their distance in total variation is given by
dTV​(μ,ν)=2​supA∈ℰ​|ν⁡(A)−μ⁡(A)|.\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}(\mu,\nu)=2\,\underset{A\in\mathcal{E}}{\sup}\,\left|\nu(A)-\mu(A)\right|.
(23)
If μ\mu and ν\nu are dominated by a common σ\sigma-finite measure λ∈ℳ⁡(ℰ)\lambda\in\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{E}), the total variation distance is simply the L1​(λ)L^{1}(\lambda)-distance of their densities with respect to λ\lambda. Moreover, we recall the dual formula
dTV​(μ,ν)=sup‖f‖B∞​(E)≤1​μ​(f)−ν⁡(f),\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\mu,\nu)=\underset{\|f\|_{B^{\infty}(E)}\leq 1}{\sup}\,\mu(f)-\nu(f),
(24)
where B∞​(E)B^{\infty}(E) denotes the set of real-valued bounded (E,ℰ)(E,\mathcal{E})-measurable functions.
We estimate (22) using backward numerical analysis, which studies approximate solutions of exact problems by comparing them to exact solutions of approximate problems. The central idea is the definition of well-chosen probability flows (28) encoding the coarse-graining error in a convenient way.
These definitions are inspired by a recent construction in the literature on particle approximations of non-linear semigroups, the backward error process from [33].
We first introduce a family of probability measures on the level sets or fibers of ξ\xi
ℱ:=(πz)z∈𝒵,πz∈𝒫⁡(ξ−1​{z}),\mathcal{F}:=\left(\pi_{z}\right)_{z\in\mathcal{Z}},\qquad\pi_{z}\in\mathcal{P}\left(\xi^{-1}\{z\}\right),
(25)
and define associated operators
ΠZ​f​(z)=πz​(f),ΠX​f​(x)=πξ⁡(x)​(f)=ξ⋆​ΠZ​f​(x).\Pi^{Z}f(z)=\pi_{z}(f),\qquad\Pi^{X}f(x)=\pi_{\xi(x)}(f)=\xi^{\star}\Pi^{Z}f(x).
(26)
The operator ΠZ\Pi^{Z} sends the observable ff on 𝒳\mathcal{X} to a fiber-averaged observable on 𝒵\mathcal{Z}, which is then pulled back by ξ⋆\xi^{\star} to the space of fiber-wise constant observables on 𝒳\mathcal{X}, giving ΠX​f\Pi^{X}f.
The following relations are easy to check:
ΠZ​ξ⋆=id,ΠX​ξ⋆=ξ⋆,\Pi^{Z}\xi^{\star}=\mathrm{id},\qquad\Pi^{X}\xi^{\star}=\xi^{\star},
(27)
and in particular, ΠX​ΠX=ξ⋆​(ΠZ​ξ⋆)​ΠZ=ΠX\Pi^{X}\Pi^{X}=\xi^{\star}(\Pi^{Z}\xi^{\star})\Pi^{Z}=\Pi^{X} is a projector.
The central objects in our framework are the following time-dependent backward error measures.
For each Y∈{X,Z}Y\in\{X,Z\}, we set
ψt,TY​(g)=ηtY​(ΠY​QT−tX​g)ηtY​(ΠY​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳)=η0Y​(QtY​ΠY​QT−tX​g)η0Y​(QtY​ΠY​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳).\psi_{t,T}^{Y}(g)=\frac{\eta_{t}^{Y}\left(\Pi^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}g\right)}{\eta_{t}^{Y}\left(\Pi^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\right)}=\frac{\eta_{0}^{Y}\left(Q_{t}^{Y}\Pi^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}g\right)}{\eta_{0}^{Y}\left(Q_{t}^{Y}\Pi^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\right)}.
(28)
These define probability measure-valued flows in 𝒫⁡(𝒳)\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}): the exact QXQ^{X}-evolution is applied to gg on [t,T][t,T], preceded by a “coarse-grained evolution” QtY​ΠYQ_{t}^{Y}\Pi^{Y} on [0,t][0,t]. This linear evolution is normalized to ensure this approximate flow remains in 𝒫⁡(𝒳)\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}).
The usefulness of the error measures (28) is clarified by the following reformulation of the bias (22).
Proposition 2.
Fix T>0T>0, and assume that the initial conditions for XX and ZZ are compatible, in the sense that η0Z=ξ⋆​η0X{\eta_{0}^{Z}=\xi_{\star}\eta_{0}^{X}}.
Then,
ξ⋆​ηTX​(f)−ηTZ​(f)\displaystyle\xi_{\star}\eta_{T}^{X}(f)-\eta_{T}^{Z}(f)
=∫0T∂t[ψt,TX−ψt,TZ]​(ξ⋆​f)​𝑑t\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\partial_{t}\left[\psi^{X}_{t,T}-\psi^{Z}_{t,T}\right](\xi^{\star}f)\,\mathrm{d}t
(29)
=∫0TηtX​(AX​QT−tX​(ξ⋆​f−ψt,TX​(ξ⋆​f)​𝟙𝒳))ηtX​(ΠX​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳)​𝑑t\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\,\frac{\eta_{t}^{X}\left(A^{X}Q_{T-t}^{X}\left(\xi^{\star}f-\psi_{t,T}^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\right)\right)}{\eta_{t}^{X}\left(\Pi^{X}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\right)}\,\mathrm{d}t
−∫0TηtZ​(AZ​QT−tX​(ξ⋆​f−ψt,TZ​(ξ⋆​f)​𝟙𝒳))ηtZ​(ΠZ​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳)dt,\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\eta_{t}^{Z}\left(A^{Z}Q_{T-t}^{X}\left(\xi^{\star}f-\psi_{t,T}^{Z}(\xi^{\star}f)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\right)\right)}{\eta_{t}^{Z}\left(\Pi^{Z}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\right)}\mathrm{d}t,
where the operators AXA^{X} and AZA^{Z} are defined by
AX=ℒX​ΠX−ΠX​ℒX,AZ=ℒZ​ΠZ−ΠZ​ℒX.A^{X}=\mathcal{L}^{X}\Pi^{X}-\Pi^{X}\mathcal{L}^{X},\qquad A^{Z}=\mathcal{L}^{Z}\Pi^{Z}-\Pi^{Z}\mathcal{L}^{X}.
(30)
Proof.
First, setting t=Tt=T in (28) gives
ψT,TY​(ξ⋆​f)=ηTY​(ΠY​ξ⋆​f)ηTY​(ΠY​𝟙𝒳).\psi_{T,T}^{Y}(\xi^{\star}f)=\frac{\eta_{T}^{Y}(\Pi^{Y}\xi^{\star}f)}{\eta_{T}^{Y}(\Pi^{Y}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})}.
(31)
For Y=XY=X, this gives, using ΠX​ξ⋆​f=ξ⋆​f\Pi^{X}\xi^{\star}f=\xi^{\star}f and 𝟙𝒳=ξ⋆​𝟙𝒵\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}=\xi^{\star}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Z}}
ψT,TX​(ξ⋆​f)=ηTX​(ξ⋆​f)ηTX​(𝟙𝒳)=ξ⋆​ηTX​(f),\psi_{T,T}^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)=\frac{\eta_{T}^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)}{\eta_{T}^{X}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})}=\xi_{\star}\eta_{T}^{X}(f),
(32)
since ηTX∈𝒫⁡(𝒳)\eta_{T}^{X}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}). For Y=ZY=Z, using ΠZ​ξ⋆=id\Pi^{Z}\xi^{\star}=\mathrm{id}, we obtain
ψT,TZ​(ξ⋆​f)=ηTZ​(f)ηTZ​(𝟙𝒵)=ηTZ​(f).\psi_{T,T}^{Z}(\xi^{\star}f)=\frac{\eta_{T}^{Z}(f)}{\eta_{T}^{Z}(\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Z}})}=\eta_{T}^{Z}(f).
(33)
On the other hand, setting t=0t=0 in (28) gives
ψ0,TX​(ξ⋆​f)\displaystyle\psi_{0,T}^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)
=η0X​(ΠX​QTX​ξ⋆​f)η0X​(ΠX​QTX​ξ⋆​𝟙𝒵)\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{0}^{X}\left(\Pi^{X}Q_{T}^{X}\xi^{\star}f\right)}{\eta_{0}^{X}\left(\Pi^{X}Q_{T}^{X}\xi^{\star}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Z}}\right)}
(34)
=η0X​(ξ⋆​ΠZ​QTX​ξ⋆​f)η0X​(ξ⋆​ΠZ​QTX​ξ⋆​𝟙𝒵)\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{0}^{X}\left(\xi^{\star}\Pi^{Z}Q_{T}^{X}\xi^{\star}f\right)}{\eta_{0}^{X}\left(\xi^{\star}\Pi^{Z}Q_{T}^{X}\xi^{\star}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Z}}\right)}
=ξ⋆​η0X​(ΠZ​QTX​ξ⋆​f)ξ⋆​η0X​(ΠZ​QTX​ξ⋆​𝟙𝒵)\displaystyle=\frac{\xi_{\star}\eta_{0}^{X}\left(\Pi^{Z}Q_{T}^{X}\xi^{\star}f\right)}{\xi_{\star}\eta_{0}^{X}\left(\Pi^{Z}Q_{T}^{X}\xi^{\star}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{Z}}\right)}
=ψ0,TZ​(ξ⋆​f),\displaystyle=\psi_{0,T}^{Z}(\xi^{\star}f),
using the compatibility condition η0Z=ξ⋆​η0X\eta^{Z}_{0}=\xi_{\star}\eta^{X}_{0} in the last line.
The first equality in (29) then follows from (32), (33), (34) and the fundamental theorem of calculus.
To obtain the second equality, we use the Feynman–Kac formula (12) twice (once with YY and once with XX), to compute, for all t∈(0,T)t\in(0,T) and g∈ℓ∞​(𝒳)g\in\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}):
∂tη0Y​(QtY​ΠY​QT−tX​g)=η0Y​(QtY​[ℒY​ΠY−ΠY​ℒX]​QT−tX​g).\partial_{t}\eta_{0}^{Y}\left(Q_{t}^{Y}\Pi^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}g\right)=\eta_{0}^{Y}\left(Q_{t}^{Y}\left[\mathcal{L}^{Y}\Pi^{Y}-\Pi^{Y}\mathcal{L}^{X}\right]Q_{T-t}^{X}g\right).
(35)
By the product rule, this further gives, after a direct computation,
∂tψt,TY​(g)=ηtY​([ℒY​ΠY−ΠY​ℒX]​QT−tX​(g−ψt,TY​(g)​𝟙𝒳))ηtY​(ΠY​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳).\partial_{t}\psi_{t,T}^{Y}(g)=\frac{\eta_{t}^{Y}\left(\left[\mathcal{L}^{Y}\Pi^{Y}-\Pi^{Y}\mathcal{L}^{X}\right]Q_{T-t}^{X}\left(g-\psi_{t,T}^{Y}(g)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\right)\right)}{\eta_{t}^{Y}\left(\Pi^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\right)}.
(36)
Applying this identity to Y=X,ZY=X,Z and g=ξ⋆​fg=\xi^{\star}f, integrating over t∈(0,T)t\in(0,T), we obtain the second equality in (29).
∎
We will use this integral formulation to obtain bounds on the coarse-graining bias (22) in Section 2.5.
The commutator AX=[ℒX,ΠX]A^{X}=[\mathcal{L}^{X},\Pi^{X}] is a closure defect, measuring how the evolution of a fiber-wise constant observable deviates from being fiber-wise constant under the dynamics of XX.
Consequently, the contribution of the term involving AXA^{X} is related to the intrinsic non-Markovianity of ξ⁡(X)\xi(X), and indeed is independent of the definition of the surrogate process ZZ.
The operator AZ=ℒZ​ΠZ−ΠZ​ℒXA^{Z}=\mathcal{L}^{Z}\Pi^{Z}-\Pi^{Z}\mathcal{L}^{X} involves the quality of ZZ. Viewing ΠZ\Pi^{Z} as a Markov kernel 𝒵→𝒫⁡(𝒳)\mathcal{Z}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}) via the identification
ΠZ​(z,d​x)=πz​(d​x),\Pi^{Z}(z,\mathrm{d}x)=\pi_{z}(\mathrm{d}x),
(37)
and denoting the dual action of ℒY\mathcal{L}^{Y} on probability measures with a right-multiplication, the exact case AZ=0A^{Z}=0 corresponds to the algebraic identity
ℒZ​ΠZ=ΠZ​ℒX,\mathcal{L}^{Z}\Pi^{Z}=\Pi^{Z}\mathcal{L}^{X},
(38)
which is known in the probabilistic literature as an intertwining relation. Intertwining [48, 17] is a powerful tool in the analysis of complex Markov processes, allowing one to prove various properties via transfer from equivalent but simpler models.
We understand AZA^{Z} as measuring an intertwining defect.
We finally stress that, up to this point, the definition of the fiber measures ℱ\mathcal{F} (and therefore of ΠX\Pi^{X} and ΠZ\Pi^{Z}) has not been fixed. In fact, since the bias (22) we wish to control is independent of these objects, ℱ\mathcal{F} should be seen as a parameter in the proofs.
A specific choice, with several interesting properties, will be fixed in the remainder of this work, but one could possibly optimize our arguments with respect to ℱ\mathcal{F} in specific situations.
2.3 A canonical choice of ZZ
We now introduce a specific modeling choice, specifying both the choice of the fiber measures ℱ\mathcal{F} and of the surrogate process ZZ.
This choice has several interesting consequences, listed in Proposition 4 below.
Definition 3 (Canonical model).
i) 
The family ℱ\mathcal{F} of probability measures is defined as a disintegration of νX\nu^{X} with respect to ξ\xi, that is
∫𝒳f⁡(x)​νX​(𝑑x)=∫𝒵∫ξ−1​(z)f⁡(x)​πz​(𝑑x)​ξ⋆​νX​(𝑑z)\int_{\mathcal{X}}f(x)\nu^{X}(\mathrm{d}x)=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\int_{\xi^{-1}(z)}f(x)\pi_{z}(\mathrm{d}x)\xi_{\star}\nu^{X}(\mathrm{d}z)
(39)
for any f∈L1​(νX)f\in L^{1}(\nu^{X}).
ii) 
The surrogate process ZZ is the killed jump process generated by the coarse-grained generator
ℒZ=ΠZ​ℒX​ξ⋆.\mathcal{L}^{Z}=\Pi^{Z}\mathcal{L}^{X}\xi^{\star}.
(40)
One can show that the operator ℒZ\mathcal{L}^{Z} is the generator of a conditionally irreducible jump process, which can be understood as a fiber-to-fiber jump process, where rates are given by fiber-averages of the ℒX\mathcal{L}^{X}-rates under the QSD νX\nu^{X}.
This property relies only on the positivity of νX\nu^{X}, and would apply to any choice of ℱ\mathcal{F} defined by disintegration of a strictly positive measure. We refer to the proof in Appendix A where a very similar property is proven.
The definition (40) is closely related to classical definitions of effective dynamics (see [39, 60]) where Markovian approximations are defined in terms of conditional averages with respect to the global invariant measure.
Here, we perform an analogous construction with respect to the local equilibrium measure νX\nu^{X}.
Many simplifications come from this choice of ZZ, as the following proposition shows.
Proposition 4 (Consequences of Definition 3).
Suppose that ℱ\mathcal{F} is defined as in item i) from Definition 3. Then the following properties are satisfied.
i) 
The QSD is invariant under fiber-averaging:
∀f∈L1​(νX),νX​(ΠX​f)=νX​(f).\forall\,f\in L^{1}(\nu^{X}),\qquad\nu^{X}(\Pi^{X}f)=\nu^{X}(f).
(41)
ii) 
The closure defect is QSD-centered:
PX​AX=AX.P^{X}A^{X}=A^{X}.
(42)
Suppose furthermore that ℒZ\mathcal{L}^{Z} is defined as in item ii) from Definition 3.
iii) 
The ZZ-counterparts to (41) and (42) hold:
∀f∈L1​(νX),νZ​(ΠZ​f)=νX​(f),PZ​AZ=AZ.\forall\,f\in L^{1}(\nu^{X}),\quad\nu^{Z}(\Pi^{Z}f)=\nu^{X}(f),\qquad P^{Z}A^{Z}=A^{Z}.
(43)
iv) 
The QSD for ZZ is explicitly given by
νZ=ξ⋆​νX,λZ=λX.\nu^{Z}=\xi_{\star}\nu^{X},\qquad\lambda^{Z}=\lambda^{X}.
(44)
v) 
The intertwining defect is controlled by the closure defect:
AZ=ΠZ​AX.A^{Z}=\Pi^{Z}A^{X}.
(45)
Proof.
We first show (41), computing, since ΠX​f\Pi^{X}f is constant on fibers:
∫𝒳ΠX​f​(x)​νX​(dx)\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{X}}\Pi^{X}f(x)\nu^{X}(\mathrm{d}x)
=∫𝒵∫ξ−1​(z)ΠX​f​(x)​πz​(dx)​ξ⋆​νX​(dz)\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\int_{\xi^{-1}(z)}\Pi^{X}f(x)\,\pi_{z}(\mathrm{d}x)\,\xi_{\star}\nu^{X}(\mathrm{d}z)
(46)
=∫𝒵∫ξ−1​(z)πz​(f)​πz​(dx)​ξ⋆​νX​(dz)\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\int_{\xi^{-1}(z)}\pi_{z}(f)\,\pi_{z}(\mathrm{d}x)\,\xi_{\star}\nu^{X}(\mathrm{d}z)
=∫𝒵πz​(f)​ξ⋆​νX​(dz)\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\pi_{z}(f)\,\xi_{\star}\nu^{X}(\mathrm{d}z)
=∫𝒵∫ξ−1​(z)f⁡(x)​πz​(dx)​ξ⋆​νX​(dz)\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{Z}}\int_{\xi^{-1}(z)}f(x)\,\pi_{z}(\mathrm{d}x)\,\xi_{\star}\nu^{X}(\mathrm{d}z)
=νX​(f).\displaystyle=\nu^{X}(f).
We now show (42). For any Y∈{X,Z}Y\in\{X,Z\},
νY​AY=νY​[ℒY​ΠY−ΠY​ℒX]=−λY​νY​ΠY−νY​ΠY​ℒX=−λY​νX−νX​ℒX,\nu^{Y}A^{Y}=\nu^{Y}\left[\mathcal{L}^{Y}\Pi^{Y}-\Pi^{Y}\mathcal{L}^{X}\right]=-\lambda^{Y}\nu^{Y}\Pi^{Y}-\nu^{Y}\Pi^{Y}\mathcal{L}^{X}=-\lambda^{Y}\nu^{X}-\nu^{X}\mathcal{L}^{X},
(47)
from which (42) follows immediately, as well as the second identity in (43) once (44) is established.
We now assume that ZZ is defined as in (40), and proceed to show (44). Let f∈ℓ∞​(𝒵)f\in\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}). We compute
ξ⋆​νX​(ℒZ​f)\displaystyle\xi_{\star}\nu^{X}(\mathcal{L}^{Z}f)
=νX​(ξ⋆​ΠZ​ℒX​ξ⋆​f)\displaystyle=\nu^{X}(\xi^{\star}\Pi^{Z}\mathcal{L}^{X}\xi^{\star}f)
(48)
=νX​(ΠX​ℒX​ξ⋆​f)\displaystyle=\nu^{X}(\Pi^{X}\mathcal{L}^{X}\xi^{\star}f)
=νX​(ℒX​ξ⋆​f)\displaystyle=\nu^{X}(\mathcal{L}^{X}\xi^{\star}f)
=−λX​νX​(ξ⋆​f)\displaystyle=-\lambda^{X}\nu^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)
=−λX​ξ⋆​νX​(f)\displaystyle=-\lambda^{X}\xi_{\star}\nu^{X}(f)
using the definition of ΠX\Pi^{X} in the second line, (41) in the third line, and the eigenequation (19) in the fourth line.
This proves that ξ⋆​νX\xi_{\star}\nu^{X} is a positive eigenmeasure for ℒZ\mathcal{L}^{Z}, and is therefore equal to νZ\nu^{Z} by uniqueness in Proposition 1, with common negative eigenvalue −λX=−λZ-\lambda^{X}=-\lambda^{Z}.
The first identity in (43) then follows easily, since
νZ​(ΠZ​f)=ξ⋆​νX​(ΠZ​f)=νX​(ξ⋆​ΠZ​f)=νX​(ΠX​f)=νX​(f)\nu^{Z}(\Pi^{Z}f)=\xi_{\star}\nu^{X}(\Pi^{Z}f)=\nu^{X}(\xi^{\star}\Pi^{Z}f)=\nu^{X}(\Pi^{X}f)=\nu^{X}(f)
(49)
by (41) and (44).
Finally, (45) follows from a simple computation using the identities (26) and (27):
AZ\displaystyle A^{Z}
=ℒZ​ΠZ−ΠZ​ℒX\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}^{Z}\Pi^{Z}-\Pi^{Z}\mathcal{L}^{X}
(50)
=ΠZ​ℒX​ξ⋆​ΠZ−ΠZ​ℒX\displaystyle=\Pi^{Z}\mathcal{L}^{X}\xi^{\star}\Pi^{Z}-\Pi^{Z}\mathcal{L}^{X}
=ΠZ​ℒX​ΠX−ΠZ​ξ⋆​ΠZ​ℒX\displaystyle=\Pi^{Z}\mathcal{L}^{X}\Pi^{X}-\Pi^{Z}\xi^{\star}\Pi^{Z}\mathcal{L}^{X}
=ΠZ​ℒX​ΠX−ΠZ​ΠX​ℒX\displaystyle=\Pi^{Z}\mathcal{L}^{X}\Pi^{X}-\Pi^{Z}\Pi^{X}\mathcal{L}^{X}
=ΠZ​AX.\displaystyle=\Pi^{Z}A^{X}.
∎
2.4 Statement of main results
Our first main result is a control on the bias (22), giving an approximation of the non-linear non-Markovian flow ξ⋆​ηtX\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X} by the non-linear Markovian flow ηtZ\eta_{t}^{Z} associated with the canonical model ZZ of Definition 3.
Theorem 5.
Let ℱ\mathcal{F} and ZZ be as in Definition 3, and assume that a separation of timescales holds: there exist Cζ,ζ>0C_{\zeta},\zeta>0 such that
∀t≥0,‖et⁡(I−ΠX)​ℒX​(I−ΠX)​(I−ΠX)‖≤Cζ​e−ζ​t,\forall\,t\geq 0,\qquad\left\|\mathrm{e}^{t(I-\Pi^{X})\mathcal{L}^{X}(I-\Pi^{X})}(I-\Pi^{X})\right\|\leq C_{\zeta}\mathrm{e}^{-\zeta t},
(51)
with moreover ζ>2​(λX+γX)\zeta>2(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X}), and that the compatibility condition η0Z=ξ⋆​η0X\eta_{0}^{Z}=\xi_{\star}\eta_{0}^{X} holds.
Recall the definition of the closure defect AX=ℒX​ΠX−ΠX​ℒXA^{X}=\mathcal{L}^{X}\Pi^{X}-\Pi^{X}\mathcal{L}^{X}. It holds that
i) 
there exist explicit positive constants CC and C′C^{\prime} depending on η0X\eta_{0}^{X} but independent of TT such that
∀T>0,dTV​(ξ⋆​ηTX,ηTZ)≤‖AX‖ζ​(C​e−γX​T+C′​T​e−(γZ∧γX)​T),\forall\,T>0,\qquad\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}\left(\xi_{\star}\eta_{T}^{X},\eta_{T}^{Z}\right)\leq\frac{\|A^{X}\|}{\zeta}\left(C\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}T}+C^{\prime}T\mathrm{e}^{-(\gamma^{Z}\land\gamma^{X})T}\right),
(52)
ii) 
we have the quasimode estimates
‖hX−ΠX​hX‖≤CλX​Cζ​‖AX‖ζ−λX\|h^{X}-\Pi^{X}h^{X}\|\leq C_{\lambda}^{X}C_{\zeta}\frac{\|A^{X}\|}{\zeta-\lambda^{X}}
(53)
and
‖ΠZ​hX−hZ‖≤CλX​Cζ​CγZγZ​‖AX‖2ζ−λX,\|\Pi^{Z}h^{X}-h^{Z}\|\leq\frac{C_{\lambda}^{X}C_{\zeta}C_{\gamma}^{Z}}{\gamma^{Z}}\frac{\|A^{X}\|^{2}}{\zeta-\lambda^{X}},
(54)
iii) 
the following two-sided estimate holds for all T>0T>0,
|dTV​(ξ⋆​ηTX,ξ⋆​νX)−dTV​(ηTX,νX)|≤CλX​CγX​Cζη0X​(hX)​(e−(ζ−λX)​T+2​‖AX‖ζ​e−γX​T).\left|\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\xi_{\star}\eta_{T}^{X},\xi_{\star}\nu^{X})-\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\eta_{T}^{X},\nu^{X})\right|\leq\frac{C_{\lambda}^{X}C_{\gamma}^{X}C_{\zeta}}{\eta_{0}^{X}(h^{X})}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-(\zeta-\lambda^{X})T}+2\frac{\|A^{X}\|}{\zeta}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}T}\right).
(55)
Theorem 5 has the following straightforward consequence.
Corollary 6.
Under the Assumptions of Theorem 5, it holds, for all T>0T>0,
|dTV​(ηTZ,νZ)−dTV​(ηTX,νX)|\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\eta_{T}^{Z},\nu^{Z})-\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\eta_{T}^{X},\nu^{X})\right|
≤CλX​CγX​Cζη0X​(hX)​(e−(ζ−λX)​T+2​‖AX‖ζ​e−γX​T)\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\lambda}^{X}C_{\gamma}^{X}C_{\zeta}}{\eta_{0}^{X}(h^{X})}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-(\zeta-\lambda^{X})T}+2\frac{\|A^{X}\|}{\zeta}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}T}\right)
(56)
+‖AX‖ζ​(C​e−γX​T+C′​T​e−(γZ∧γX)​T),\displaystyle+\frac{\|A^{X}\|}{\zeta}\left(C\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}T}+C^{\prime}T\mathrm{e}^{-(\gamma^{Z}\land\gamma^{X})T}\right),
with the same constants C,C′C,C^{\prime} as in (52).
Proof.
Write
|dTV​(ηtZ,νZ)−dTV​(ηtX,νX)|\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\eta_{t}^{Z},\nu^{Z})-\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\eta_{t}^{X},\nu^{X})\right|
≤|dTV​(ηtZ,νZ)−dTV​(ξ⋆​ηtX,ξ⋆​νX)|\displaystyle\leq\left|\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\eta_{t}^{Z},\nu^{Z})-\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X},\xi_{\star}\nu^{X})\right|
(57)
+|dTV​(ξ⋆​ηtX,ξ⋆​νX)−dTV​(ηtX,νX)|,\displaystyle+\left|\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X},\xi_{\star}\nu^{X})-\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\eta_{t}^{X},\nu^{X})\right|,
use item iv) in Proposition 4 and a reverse triangle inequality to bound
|dTV​(ηtZ,νZ)−dTV​(ξ⋆​ηtX,ξ⋆​νX)|≤dTV​(ηtZ,ξ⋆​ηtX).\left|\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\eta_{t}^{Z},\nu^{Z})-\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X},\xi_{\star}\nu^{X})\right|\leq\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\eta_{t}^{Z},\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X}).
(58)
The proof then follows immediately from the estimates (52) and (55).
∎
Before proving Theorem 5, let us make a few remarks on the nature of these results.
∘\circ 
The estimate (56) is relevant to quasi-stationarity detection, as it ensures that, in the regime where ‖AX‖/ζ\|A^{X}\|/\zeta is much smaller than the prefactors, the convergence of ηtZ\eta_{t}^{Z} towards νZ\nu^{Z} provides an accurate diagnostic for the convergence of ηtX\eta_{t}^{X} towards the QSD.
∘\circ 
The formulas we obtain for the prefactors CC and C′C^{\prime} in (52) and (56) are rather complicated, which is why we provide simplified constants, given for convenience at the end of the proof of Theorem 5 (see Equation (99) below). Their dependence on η0X\eta_{0}^{X} is completely explicit in terms of hXh^{X} and hZh^{Z}, however, we have no a priori control on their relation to ζ\zeta. In the adiabatic regime considered in Section 2.6, we prove explicit scaling estimates for these constants and the parameter ‖AX‖/ζ\|A^{X}\|/\zeta. In Theorem 7, we show, in the limit of some small parameter ε→0\varepsilon\to 0, that ‖AX‖/ζ=𝒪⁡(ε)\|A^{X}\|/\zeta=\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon), while all the other constants remain bounded uniformly in ε\varepsilon. In this regime, we can therefore obtain convergence of the probability flow ηZ\eta^{Z} towards ξ⋆​ηX\xi_{\star}\eta^{X} uniformly in time, at rate 𝒪⁡(ε)\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon) in total-variation (see Corollary 8). A similar quantitative-in-ε\varepsilon result holds for the diagnostic bound of Corollary 6.
∘\circ 
Using a straightforward argument based on a spectral decomposition and a triangle inequality, we can, instead of (52), obtain an estimate of the form
dTV​(ξ⋆​ηTX,ηTZ)≤C′′​e−γX​T+C′′′​e−γZ​T.\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}\left(\xi_{\star}\eta_{T}^{X},\eta_{T}^{Z}\right)\leq C^{\prime\prime}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}T}+C^{\prime\prime\prime}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{Z}T}.
(59)
This is the sharper bound in the regime T≫ζ/‖AX‖T\gg\zeta/\|A^{X}\|. The difference is that the bound (59) is unaware of any separation of timescale for XX or of the magnitude of the closure defect AXA^{X}. We therefore understand (52) as sacrificing relative accuracy in the long-time limit for accuracy in the transient phase of decorrelation, in which quasi-stationarity diagnostics are most needed. The upper bound (52) still converges to 00 as T→+∞T\to+\infty, and therefore preserves absolute accuracy in the long-time limit.
Moreover, the trivial bound (59) gives no information in the adiabatic limit of Section 2.6, where orthogonal degrees of freedom quickly equilibrate at a fixed value of ξ\xi.
∘\circ 
The assumption ζ>2​(λX+γX)\zeta>2(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X}) is not crucial, and the proof shows that it could be relaxed to ζ>λX+γX\zeta>\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X}, which says roughly that the orthogonal fiber-wise dynamics relax strictly faster than the non-linear semigroup. We understand this condition as a mild adiabatic condition. We keep the more restrictive condition that the orthogonal dynamics relax twice as fast, in order to simplify the final estimates.
∘\circ 
Checking whether a given pair X,ξX,\xi of dynamics and collective variable satisfies the assumptions of Theorem 5 is typically infeasible for systems with many microstates (i.e. when |𝒳|≫1|\mathcal{X}|\gg 1), as these involve quantitative spectral properties of the generator. For the specific case of complex molecular systems, assuming that ‖AX‖/ζ\|A^{X}\|/\zeta is small will likely remain a working hypothesis in many cases for which a natural definition of ξ\xi is available, either through domain expertise or computational methods.
Practical choices of the collective variable ξ\xi can nevertheless be inspired by the adiabaticity condition, if sufficient knowledge of the system is at hand.
2.5 Proof of Theorem 5
We now prove Theorem 5.
Proof of Theorem 5.
For brevity, we will use the notations Π0:=ΠX\Pi_{0}:=\Pi^{X}, Π1:=I−Π0\Pi_{1}:=I-\Pi_{0}, and Aα​β:=Πα​A​ΠβA_{\alpha\beta}:=\Pi_{\alpha}A\Pi_{\beta} for any operator AA on ℓ∞​(𝒳)\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}).
We often use the convolution bound
∀a,b,T>0,a≠b∫0Te−a​t​e−b⁡(T−t)​𝑑t=e−b​T−e−a​Ta−b≤T​e−(a∧b)​T,\forall a,b,T>0,\,a\neq b\qquad\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{-at}\mathrm{e}^{-b(T-t)}\,\mathrm{d}t=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-bT}-\mathrm{e}^{-aT}}{a-b}\leq T\mathrm{e}^{-(a\land b)T},
(60)
where the upper bound follows from the mean-value theorem and the convexity of x↦e−x​Tx\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{-xT}. The same inequality obviously holds for a=ba=b.
We begin by showing the main estimate (52). This proceeds in two steps. We first establish rough estimates on the error process, using only the principal eigenprojections, and an interesting “self-correcting” property of the error measures ψt,TY​(ξ⋆​f)\psi_{t,T}^{Y}(\xi^{\star}f), which implies faster decay than generic spectral bounds.
The condition (51) is then used in a second step in combination with these rough estimates to establish (52), by splitting the semigroup into its action on slow and fast variables. The main tool (76) used in this step is based on a Duhamel–Dyson splitting identity (75).
2.5.1 Preliminary estimates and self-correcting property
The first step is to obtain a rough bound on the time derivatives
∂tψt,TY​(ξ⋆​f)=η0Y​QtY​AY​QT−tX​rt,TY​(f)η0Y​QtY​ΠY​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳,\partial_{t}\psi_{t,T}^{Y}(\xi^{\star}f)=\frac{\eta_{0}^{Y}Q_{t}^{Y}A^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}r_{t,T}^{Y}(f)}{\eta_{0}^{Y}Q_{t}^{Y}\Pi^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}},
(61)
where we denote the residual
rt,TY​(f):=ξ⋆​f−ψt,TY​(ξ⋆​f)​𝟙𝒳.r_{t,T}^{Y}(f):=\xi^{\star}f-\psi_{t,T}^{Y}(\xi^{\star}f)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}.
(62)
We will use the following lower estimate
η0Y​QtY​ΠY​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳\displaystyle\eta_{0}^{Y}Q_{t}^{Y}\Pi^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}
≥1‖hX‖​η0Y​QtY​ΠY​QT−tX​hX\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{\|h^{X}\|}\eta_{0}^{Y}Q_{t}^{Y}\Pi^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}h^{X}
(63)
≥ChY‖hX‖​e−λX​(T−t)​η0Y​(QtY​hY)\displaystyle\geq\frac{C_{h}^{Y}}{\|h^{X}\|}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}(T-t)}\eta_{0}^{Y}(Q_{t}^{Y}h^{Y})
=ChY‖hX‖​e−λX​(T−t)−λY​t​η0Y​(hY)\displaystyle=\frac{C_{h}^{Y}}{\|h^{X}\|}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}(T-t)-\lambda^{Y}t}\eta_{0}^{Y}(h^{Y})
:=C1Y​e−λX​(T−t)−λY​t\displaystyle:=C_{1}^{Y}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}(T-t)-\lambda^{Y}t}
which uses the positivity of the operators QtXQ_{t}^{X}, QtYQ_{t}^{Y} and ΠY\Pi^{Y}, as well as the pointwise comparison ΠY​hX≥ChY​hY\Pi^{Y}h^{X}\geq C_{h}^{Y}h^{Y}, defining
ChY:=min𝒴​ΠY​hXhY,C_{h}^{Y}:=\underset{\mathcal{Y}}{\min}\frac{\Pi^{Y}h^{X}}{h^{Y}},
(64)
which is well-defined since hYh^{Y} is positive on 𝒴\mathcal{Y}.
Because of item iv) in Proposition 4, we actually have λX=λY\lambda^{X}=\lambda^{Y}, which simplifies the estimate (63), but this does not turn out to be a crucial property.
We then decompose spectrally
η0Y​QtY​AY​QT−tX​rt,TY​(f)\displaystyle\eta_{0}^{Y}Q_{t}^{Y}A^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}r_{t,T}^{Y}(f)
=η0Y​QtY​PY​AY​QT−tX​rt,TY​(f)\displaystyle=\eta_{0}^{Y}Q_{t}^{Y}P^{Y}A^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}r_{t,T}^{Y}(f)
(65)
=η0Y​QtY​PY​AY​QT−tX​PX​rt,TY​(f)+νX​(rt,TY​(f))​η0Y​QtY​PY​AY​QT−tX​hX,\displaystyle=\eta_{0}^{Y}Q_{t}^{Y}P^{Y}A^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}P^{X}r_{t,T}^{Y}(f)+\nu^{X}(r_{t,T}^{Y}(f))\eta_{0}^{Y}Q_{t}^{Y}P^{Y}A^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}h^{X},
using the identity AY=PY​AYA^{Y}=P^{Y}A^{Y}, which is a consequence of the choice of canonical model, as shown in Proposition 4 (items ii and iii).
We estimate the terms in the decomposition as
|η0Y​QtY​PY​AY​QT−tX​PX​rt,TY​(f)|\displaystyle|\eta_{0}^{Y}Q_{t}^{Y}P^{Y}A^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}P^{X}r_{t,T}^{Y}(f)|
≤CγX​CγY​‖η0Y‖∗​e−(λY+γY)​t​‖AY​‖e−(λX+γX)​(T−t)‖​rt,TY​(f)‖\displaystyle\leq C_{\gamma}^{X}C_{\gamma}^{Y}\|\eta_{0}^{Y}\|_{*}\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{Y}+\gamma^{Y})t}\|A^{Y}\|\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})(T-t)}\|r_{t,T}^{Y}(f)\|
(66)
≤2​CγX​CγY​e−(λY+γY)​t​‖AY‖​e−(λX+γX)​(T−t)​‖f‖\displaystyle\leq 2C_{\gamma}^{X}C_{\gamma}^{Y}\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{Y}+\gamma^{Y})t}\|A^{Y}\|\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})(T-t)}\|f\|
:=C2Y​e−λX​(T−t)​e−λY​t​e−γY​t​e−γX​(T−t)​‖f‖\displaystyle:=C_{2}^{Y}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}(T-t)}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{Y}t}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{Y}t}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}(T-t)}\|f\|
|η0Y​QtY​PY​AY​QT−tX​hX|\displaystyle|\eta_{0}^{Y}Q_{t}^{Y}P^{Y}A^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}h^{X}|
≤CλX​CγY​e−(λY+γY)​t​‖AY‖​e−λX​(T−t)​‖hX‖\displaystyle\leq C_{\lambda}^{X}C_{\gamma}^{Y}\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{Y}+\gamma^{Y})t}\|A^{Y}\|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}(T-t)}\|h^{X}\|
:=C3Y​e−λX​(T−t)​e−λY​t​e−γY​t\displaystyle:=C_{3}^{Y}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}(T-t)}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{Y}t}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{Y}t}
using ‖η0Y‖∗=1\|\eta_{0}^{Y}\|_{*}=1 in the second and third line, and ‖rt,TY​(f)‖≤2​‖f‖\|r_{t,T}^{Y}(f)\|\leq 2\|f\| in the second line.
In fact, we can recover faster decay of the principal eigenspace contribution by estimating the coefficient νX​(rt,TY​(f))\nu^{X}(r_{t,T}^{Y}(f)) appearing in (65). We begin with a computation
νX​(rt,TY​(f))\displaystyle\nu^{X}(r_{t,T}^{Y}(f))
=νX​(ξ⋆​f−ψt,TY​(ξ⋆​f)​𝟙𝒳)\displaystyle=\nu^{X}(\xi^{\star}f-\psi_{t,T}^{Y}(\xi^{\star}f)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})
(67)
=νX​(ξ⋆​f)−ψt,TY​(ξ⋆​f)\displaystyle=\nu^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)-\psi_{t,T}^{Y}(\xi^{\star}f)
=−ψt,TY​(ξ⋆​f−νX​(ξ⋆​f)​𝟙𝒳)\displaystyle=-\psi_{t,T}^{Y}(\xi^{\star}f-\nu^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})
=−ψt,TY​(PX​[ξ⋆​f−νX​(ξ⋆​f)​𝟙𝒳])\displaystyle=-\psi_{t,T}^{Y}(P^{X}\left[\xi^{\star}f-\nu^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\right])
=−η0Y​QtY​ΠY​QT−tX​PX​[ξ⋆​f−νX​(ξ⋆​f)​𝟙𝒳]η0Y​QtY​ΠY​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳\displaystyle=-\frac{\eta_{0}^{Y}Q_{t}^{Y}\Pi^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}P^{X}\left[\xi^{\star}f-\nu^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\right]}{\eta_{0}^{Y}Q_{t}^{Y}\Pi^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}
=−η0Y​QtY​PY​ΠY​QT−tX​PX​[ξ⋆​f−νX​(ξ⋆​f)​𝟙𝒳]η0Y​QtY​ΠY​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳,\displaystyle=-\frac{\eta_{0}^{Y}Q_{t}^{Y}P^{Y}\Pi^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}P^{X}\left[\xi^{\star}f-\nu^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\right]}{\eta_{0}^{Y}Q_{t}^{Y}\Pi^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}},
using the fact that ψt,TY\psi_{t,T}^{Y} is a probability measure, and that νX​(ξ⋆​f−νX​(ξ⋆​f)​𝟙𝒳)=0\nu^{X}(\xi^{\star}f-\nu^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})=0 to insert PXP^{X} in the fourth line, together with the computation
νY​ΠY​QT−tX​PX=νY​ΠY​PX​QT−tX=νX​PX​QT−tX=0\nu^{Y}\Pi^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}P^{X}=\nu^{Y}\Pi^{Y}P^{X}Q_{T-t}^{X}=\nu^{X}P^{X}Q_{T-t}^{X}=0
(68)
to insert PYP^{Y} in the last line. We used items i and iii from Proposition 4 in the second-to-last equality.
Taking operator norms gives the estimate
|η0Y​QtY​PY​ΠY​QT−tX​PX​[ξ⋆​f−νX​(ξ⋆​f)​𝟙𝒳]|\displaystyle|\eta_{0}^{Y}Q_{t}^{Y}P^{Y}\Pi^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}P^{X}\left[\xi^{\star}f-\nu^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\right]|
(69)
≤CγX​CγY​‖η0Y‖∗​‖ΠY‖​e−(λY+γY)​t​e−(λX+γX)​(T−t)​‖ξ⋆​f−νX​(ξ⋆​f)​𝟙𝒳‖\displaystyle\leq C_{\gamma}^{X}C_{\gamma}^{Y}\|\eta_{0}^{Y}\|_{*}\|\Pi^{Y}\|\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{Y}+\gamma^{Y})t}\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})(T-t)}\|\xi^{\star}f-\nu^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\|
which combined with the lower bound (63) and the fact that ‖η0Y‖∗=1\|\eta_{0}^{Y}\|_{*}=1 gives
|νX​(rt,TY​(f))|\displaystyle|\nu^{X}(r_{t,T}^{Y}(f))|
≤CγX​CγY​‖η0Y‖∗​‖ΠY‖C1Y​e−γY​t​e−γX​(T−t)​‖ξ⋆​f−νX​(ξ⋆​f)​𝟙𝒳‖\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\gamma}^{X}C_{\gamma}^{Y}\|\eta_{0}^{Y}\|_{*}\|\Pi^{Y}\|}{C_{1}^{Y}}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{Y}t}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}(T-t)}\|\xi^{\star}f-\nu^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\|
(70)
≤2​CγX​CγY​‖ΠY‖C1Y​e−γY​t​e−γX​(T−t)​‖f‖\displaystyle\leq 2\frac{C_{\gamma}^{X}C_{\gamma}^{Y}\|\Pi^{Y}\|}{C_{1}^{Y}}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{Y}t}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}(T-t)}\|f\|
:=C4Y​e−γY​t​e−γX​(T−t)​‖f‖\displaystyle:=C_{4}^{Y}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{Y}t}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}(T-t)}\|f\|
where we used ‖ξ⋆​f−νX​(ξ⋆​f)​𝟙𝒳‖≤2​‖f‖\|\xi^{\star}f-\nu^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\|\leq 2\|f\|, since νX\nu^{X} is a probability measure and ‖ξ⋆​f‖=‖f‖\|\xi^{\star}f\|=\|f\|.
Combining (70) and (66), using a triangle inequality, and dividing by the lower bound (63), we arrive at the pointwise estimate in time on (61):
|∂tψt,TY​(ξ⋆​f)|≤(C2YC1Y​e−γY​t+C3Y​C4YC1Y​e−2​γY​t)​e−γX​(T−t)​‖f‖.\displaystyle|\partial_{t}\psi_{t,T}^{Y}(\xi^{\star}f)|\leq\left(\frac{C_{2}^{Y}}{C_{1}^{Y}}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{Y}t}+\frac{C_{3}^{Y}C_{4}^{Y}}{C_{1}^{Y}}\mathrm{e}^{-2\gamma^{Y}t}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}(T-t)}\|f\|.
(71)
It is notable that the contribution from the hXh^{X}-component of rt,TY​(f)r_{t,T}^{Y}(f) actually decays at a faster rate than that of the PXP^{X}-invariant component, which is contrary to what occurs for generic time-independent observables. This reflects a self-correcting property of the error measure ψt,TY\psi_{t,T}^{Y}.
We also record for later use the following estimate, which is obtained along similar lines
|∂tlog⁡(η0Y​QtY​ΠY​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳)|\displaystyle\left|\partial_{t}\log\left(\eta_{0}^{Y}Q_{t}^{Y}\Pi^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\right)\right|
=|η0Y​QtY​PY​AY​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳||η0Y​QtY​ΠY​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳|\displaystyle=\frac{|\eta_{0}^{Y}Q_{t}^{Y}P^{Y}A^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}|}{|\eta_{0}^{Y}Q_{t}^{Y}\Pi^{Y}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}|}
(72)
≤1C1Y​CλX​CγY​‖AY‖​‖𝟙𝒳‖​e−γY​t\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{C_{1}^{Y}}C_{\lambda}^{X}C_{\gamma}^{Y}\|A^{Y}\|\|\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\|\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{Y}t}
=1C1Y​CλX​CγY​‖AY‖​e−γY​t\displaystyle=\frac{1}{C_{1}^{Y}}C_{\lambda}^{X}C_{\gamma}^{Y}\|A^{Y}\|\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{Y}t}
:=C5Y​e−γY​t\displaystyle:=C_{5}^{Y}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{Y}t}
without resorting to a spectral decomposition (since the constant-in-time observable 𝟙𝒳\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}} has a non-zero hXh^{X}-component).
2.5.2 Slow-fast splitting and proof of (52)
We now use the timescale separation condition (51) together with the previous bounds to estimate the bias as in (29), by considering the decomposition
ℓ∞​(𝒳)=Π0​ℓ∞​(𝒳)⊕Π1​ℓ∞​(𝒳)\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})=\Pi_{0}\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})\oplus\Pi_{1}\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})
(73)
onto the subspaces Π0​ℓ∞​(𝒳)\Pi_{0}\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}) and Π1​ℓ∞​(𝒳)\Pi_{1}\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}) of slow and fast variables, respectively.
The basic idea is to use the splitting
∂t(Π1​QtX​v)=ℒ11X​QtX​v+ℒ10X​QtX​v=ℒ11X​Π1​QtX​v+ℒ10X​QtX​v,\partial_{t}\left(\Pi_{1}Q_{t}^{X}v\right)=\mathcal{L}^{X}_{11}Q_{t}^{X}v+\mathcal{L}^{X}_{10}Q_{t}^{X}v=\mathcal{L}^{X}_{11}\Pi_{1}Q_{t}^{X}v+\mathcal{L}^{X}_{10}Q_{t}^{X}v,
(74)
which gives the Duhamel–Dyson formula
∀v∈ℓ∞​(𝒳),Π1​QtX​v=et​ℒ11X​Π1​v+∫0te(t−s)​ℒ11X​ℒ10X​QsX​v​𝑑s.\forall\,v\in\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}),\qquad\Pi_{1}Q_{t}^{X}v=\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}^{X}_{11}}\Pi_{1}v+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{(t-s)\mathcal{L}^{X}_{11}}\mathcal{L}^{X}_{10}Q_{s}^{X}v\,\mathrm{d}s.
(75)
Such formulas are fundamental in the theory of coarse-grained dynamical systems, in particular for the Mori–Zwanzig formalism.
When it happens that Π1​v=0\Pi_{1}v=0, we can estimate, with the spectral decomposition v=PX​v+νX​(v)​hXv=P^{X}v+\nu^{X}(v)h^{X},
‖Π1​QtX​v‖\displaystyle\|\Pi_{1}Q_{t}^{X}v\|
≤CγX​Cζ​∫0te−ζ⁡(t−s)​‖ℒ10X‖​e−(λX+γX)​s​‖PX​v‖​𝑑s\displaystyle\leq C_{\gamma}^{X}C_{\zeta}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-\zeta(t-s)}\|\mathcal{L}^{X}_{10}\|\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})s}\|P^{X}v\|\,\mathrm{d}s
(76)
+CλXCζ∫0te−ζ⁡(t−s)∥ℒX10∥e−λX​s|νX(v)|∥hX∥ds\displaystyle+C_{\lambda}^{X}C_{\zeta}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{-\zeta(t-s)}\|\mathcal{L}^{X}_{10}\|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}s}|\nu^{X}(v)|\|h^{X}\|\,\mathrm{d}s
=CγX​Cζ​‖ℒ10X‖​e−(λX+γX)​t−e−ζ​tζ−λX−γX​‖PX​v‖+CλX​Cζ​‖ℒ10X‖​‖hX‖​e−λX​t−e−ζ​tζ−λX​|νX​(v)|\displaystyle=C_{\gamma}^{X}C_{\zeta}\|\mathcal{L}_{10}^{X}\|\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})t}-\mathrm{e}^{-\zeta t}}{\zeta-\lambda^{X}-\gamma^{X}}\|P^{X}v\|+C_{\lambda}^{X}C_{\zeta}\|\mathcal{L}_{10}^{X}\|\|h^{X}\|\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}t}-\mathrm{e}^{-\zeta t}}{\zeta-\lambda^{X}}|\nu^{X}(v)|
≤2​CγX​Cζ​‖ℒ10X‖​e−(λX+γX)​tζ​‖PX​v‖+2​CλX​Cζ​‖ℒ10X‖​‖hX‖​e−λX​tζ​|νX​(v)|\displaystyle\leq 2C_{\gamma}^{X}C_{\zeta}\|\mathcal{L}_{10}^{X}\|\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})t}}{\zeta}\|P^{X}v\|+2C_{\lambda}^{X}C_{\zeta}\|\mathcal{L}^{X}_{10}\|\|h^{X}\|\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}t}}{\zeta}|\nu^{X}(v)|
:=C6X​e−(λX+γX)​tζ​‖PX​v‖+C6X′​e−λX​tζ​|νX​(v)|,\displaystyle:=C_{6}^{X}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})t}}{\zeta}\|P^{X}v\|+C_{6}^{{}^{\prime}X}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}t}}{\zeta}|\nu^{X}(v)|,
where we use ζ>2​(λX+γX)\zeta>2(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X}) (note here that a similar estimate would hold assuming only ζ>(1+α)​(λX+γX)\zeta>(1+\alpha)(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X}) for some α>0\alpha>0, so the assumption ζ>2​(λX+γX)\zeta>2(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X}) could be relaxed to ζ>(λX+γX)\zeta>(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})).
To obtain pointwise bounds on ∂tψt,T\partial_{t}\psi_{t,T}, we start by noticing an algebraic consequence of Definition 3,
AZ​Π0\displaystyle A^{Z}\Pi_{0}
=(ℒZ​ΠZ−ΠZ​ℒX)​Π0\displaystyle=(\mathcal{L}^{Z}\Pi^{Z}-\Pi^{Z}\mathcal{L}^{X})\Pi_{0}
(77)
=ℒZ​(ΠZ​ξ⋆)​ΠZ−(ΠZ​ℒX​ξ⋆)​ΠZ\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}^{Z}\left(\Pi^{Z}\xi^{\star}\right)\Pi^{Z}-\left(\Pi^{Z}\mathcal{L}^{X}\xi^{\star}\right)\Pi^{Z}
=ℒZ​ΠZ−ℒZ​ΠZ=0,\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}^{Z}\Pi^{Z}-\mathcal{L}^{Z}\Pi^{Z}=0,
which allows us to write
∂tψt,TZ​(ξ⋆​f)=η0Z​QtZ​PZ​AZ​Π1​QT−tX​rt,TZ​(f)η0Z​QtZ​ΠZ​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳.\partial_{t}\psi_{t,T}^{Z}(\xi^{\star}f)=\frac{\eta_{0}^{Z}Q_{t}^{Z}P^{Z}A^{Z}\Pi_{1}Q_{T-t}^{X}r_{t,T}^{Z}(f)}{\eta_{0}^{Z}Q_{t}^{Z}\Pi^{Z}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}.
(78)
We estimate, using (76) and (70), and noting that Π1​rt,TY​(f)=0\Pi_{1}r_{t,T}^{Y}(f)=0 (since 𝟙𝒳\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}} and ξ⋆​f\xi^{\star}f are both constant on fibers),
‖Π1​QT−tX​rt,TZ​(f)‖≤C6X​e−(λX+γX)​(T−t)ζ​‖PX​rt,TZ​(f)‖+C6X′​e−λX​(T−t)ζ​|νX​(rt,TZ​(f))|\displaystyle\|\Pi_{1}Q_{T-t}^{X}r_{t,T}^{Z}(f)\|\leq C_{6}^{X}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})(T-t)}}{\zeta}\|P^{X}r_{t,T}^{Z}(f)\|+C_{6}^{{}^{\prime}X}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}(T-t)}}{\zeta}|\nu^{X}(r_{t,T}^{Z}(f))|
(79)
≤2​C6X​e−(λX+γX)​(T−t)ζ​‖PX‖​‖f‖+C6X′​C4Z​e−λX​(T−t)ζ​e−γZ​t​e−γX​(T−t)​‖f‖\displaystyle\leq 2C_{6}^{X}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})(T-t)}}{\zeta}\|P^{X}\|\|f\|+C_{6}^{{}^{\prime}X}C_{4}^{Z}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}(T-t)}}{\zeta}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{Z}t}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}(T-t)}\|f\|
:=(C7Z​e−(λX+γX)​(T−t)ζ+C7Z′​e−(λX+γX)​(T−t)−γZ​tζ)​‖f‖.\displaystyle:=\left(C_{7}^{Z}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})(T-t)}}{\zeta}+C_{7}^{{}^{\prime}Z}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})(T-t)-\gamma^{Z}t}}{\zeta}\right)\|f\|.
This gives
|η0Z​QtZ​PZ​AZ​Π1​QT−tX​rt,TZ​(f)|≤‖QtZ​PZ‖​‖AZ‖​‖Π1​QT−tX​rt,TZ​(f)‖\displaystyle|\eta_{0}^{Z}Q_{t}^{Z}P^{Z}A^{Z}\Pi_{1}Q_{T-t}^{X}r_{t,T}^{Z}(f)|\leq\|Q_{t}^{Z}P^{Z}\|\|A^{Z}\|\|\Pi_{1}Q_{T-t}^{X}r_{t,T}^{Z}(f)\|
(80)
≤CγZ​e−(λZ+γZ)​t​‖AZ‖​(C7Z​e−(λX+γX)​(T−t)ζ+C7Z′​e−(λX+γX)​(T−t)−γZ​tζ)​‖f‖\displaystyle\leq C_{\gamma}^{Z}\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{Z}+\gamma^{Z})t}\|A^{Z}\|\left(C_{7}^{Z}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})(T-t)}}{\zeta}+C_{7}^{{}^{\prime}Z}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})(T-t)-\gamma^{Z}t}}{\zeta}\right)\|f\|
:=e−λZ​t−λX​(T−t)​(C8Z​e−γX​(T−t)ζ+C8Z′​e−γX​(T−t)−γZ​tζ)​e−γZ​t​‖f‖.\displaystyle:=\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{Z}t-\lambda^{X}(T-t)}\left(C_{8}^{Z}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}(T-t)}}{\zeta}+C_{8}^{{}^{\prime}Z}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}(T-t)-\gamma^{Z}t}}{\zeta}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{Z}t}\|f\|.
Integrating on [0,T][0,T], we find, after dividing the estimate (80) by (63),
|∫0T∂tψt,TZ​(ξ⋆​f)​𝑑t|≤∫0T|∂tψt,TZ​(ξ⋆​f)|​𝑑t\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{T}\partial_{t}\psi_{t,T}^{Z}(\xi^{\star}f)\,\mathrm{d}t\right|\leq\int_{0}^{T}|\partial_{t}\psi_{t,T}^{Z}(\xi^{\star}f)|\,\mathrm{d}t
(81)
≤1C1Z​∫0T(C8Z​e−γX​(T−t)ζ+C8Z′​e−γX​(T−t)−γZ​tζ)​e−γZ​t​dt​‖f‖\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{C_{1}^{Z}}\int_{0}^{T}\left(C_{8}^{Z}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}(T-t)}}{\zeta}+C_{8}^{{}^{\prime}Z}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}(T-t)-\gamma^{Z}t}}{\zeta}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{Z}t}\,\mathrm{d}t\|f\|
≤C8Z+C8Z′ζ​C1Z​∫0Te−γX​(T−t)−γZ​t​dt​‖f‖\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{8}^{Z}+C_{8}^{{}^{\prime}Z}}{\zeta C_{1}^{Z}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}(T-t)-\gamma^{Z}t}\,\mathrm{d}t\|f\|
≤C8Z+C8Z′ζ​C1Z​T​e−(γX∧γZ)​T​‖f‖,\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{8}^{Z}+C_{8}^{{}^{\prime}Z}}{\zeta C_{1}^{Z}}T\mathrm{e}^{-(\gamma^{X}\land\gamma^{Z})T}\|f\|,
using (60) in the final line.
Controlling the contribution of the error measure ψt,TX\psi_{t,T}^{X} requires more care. We first decompose
∂tψt,TX​(ξ⋆​f)=η0X​QtX​PX​AX​(Π1+Π0)​QT−tX​rt,TX​(f)η0X​QtX​Π0​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳.\partial_{t}\psi_{t,T}^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)=\frac{\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}P^{X}A^{X}(\Pi_{1}+\Pi_{0})Q_{T-t}^{X}r_{t,T}^{X}(f)}{\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}.
(82)
To exploit the separation of timescales property, we first eliminate the contribution of Π0\Pi_{0} in the numerator.
To this end, we introduce the linear functional
Φ⁡(t,v)=η0X​QtX​Π1​QT−tX​v,∀(t,v)∈[0,T]×ℓ∞​(𝒳),\Phi(t,v)=\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}\Pi_{1}Q_{T-t}^{X}v,\qquad\forall\,(t,v)\in[0,T]\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}),
(83)
which is the analog of the unnormalized flow η0X​QtX​Π0​QT−tX​v\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T-t}^{X}v defining ψt,TX​(v)\psi_{t,T}^{X}(v), with the substitution Π0←Π1\Pi_{0}\leftarrow\Pi_{1}.
Differentiating with respect to the time variable, we get
∂tΦ⁡(t,v)=η0X​QtX​[ℒX,Π1]​QT−tX​v=−η0X​QtX​AX​QT−tX​v\partial_{t}\Phi(t,v)=\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}\left[\mathcal{L}^{X},\Pi_{1}\right]Q_{T-t}^{X}v=-\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}A^{X}Q_{T-t}^{X}v
(84)
Splitting AX=AX​Π0+AX​Π1A^{X}=A^{X}\Pi_{0}+A^{X}\Pi_{1}, it follows that
η0X​QtX​AX​Π0​QT−tX​rt,TX​(f)\displaystyle\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}A^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T-t}^{X}r_{t,T}^{X}(f)
=−η0X​QtX​AX​Π1​QT−tX​rt,TX​(f)−∂tΦ⁡(t,rt,TX​(f))\displaystyle=-\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}A^{X}\Pi_{1}Q_{T-t}^{X}r_{t,T}^{X}(f)-\partial_{t}\Phi(t,r_{t,T}^{X}(f))
(85)
=−η0X​QtX​AX​Π1​QT−tX​rt,TX​(f)−dd​t​Φ​(t,rt,TX​(f))\displaystyle=-\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}A^{X}\Pi_{1}Q_{T-t}^{X}r_{t,T}^{X}(f)-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Phi(t,r_{t,T}^{X}(f))
+Dv​Φ​(t,rt,TX​(f))​(dd​t​rt,TX​(f))\displaystyle+D_{v}\Phi(t,r_{t,T}^{X}(f))\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}r_{t,T}^{X}(f)\right)
=−η0X​QtX​AX​Π1​QT−tX​rt,TX​(f)−dd​t​Φ​(t,rt,TX​(f))\displaystyle=-\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}A^{X}\Pi_{1}Q_{T-t}^{X}r_{t,T}^{X}(f)-\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Phi(t,r_{t,T}^{X}(f))
−Φ(t,𝟙𝒳)∂tψt,TX(ξ⋆f),\displaystyle-\Phi(t,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})\partial_{t}\psi_{t,T}^{X}(\xi^{\star}f),
where ∂tΦ⁡(⋅,⋅)\partial_{t}\Phi(\cdot,\cdot) denotes the partial time derivative and dd​t​Φ​(⋅,⋅)\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Phi(\cdot,\cdot) denotes the total time derivative.
Therefore, the term involving Π0\Pi_{0} can be eliminated from the numerator in (82), leaving terms containing Π1\Pi_{1} through the definition of Φ\Phi.
Substituting (85) into (82) yields
∂tψt,TX​(ξ⋆​f)=−(dd​t​Φ​(t,rt,TX​(f))+Φ⁡(t,𝟙𝒳)​∂tψt,TX​(ξ⋆​f)η0X​QtX​Π0​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳).\partial_{t}\psi_{t,T}^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)=-\left(\frac{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Phi(t,r_{t,T}^{X}(f))+\Phi(t,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})\partial_{t}\psi_{t,T}^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)}{\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}\right).
(86)
The first term is handled using integration by parts
∫0T1η0X​QtX​Π0​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳​(dd​t​Φ​(t,rt,TX​(f)))​𝑑t\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\Phi(t,r_{t,T}^{X}(f))\right)\,\mathrm{d}t
(87)
=Φ⁡(T,rT,TX​(f))η0X​QTX​Π0​𝟙𝒳−Φ⁡(0,r0,TX​(f))η0X​Π0​QTX​𝟙𝒳+∫0TΦ⁡(t,rt,TX​(f))η0X​QtX​Π0​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳​∂tlog⁡(η0X​QtX​Π0​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳)​𝑑t\displaystyle=\frac{\Phi(T,r_{T,T}^{X}(f))}{\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{T}^{X}\Pi_{0}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}-\frac{\Phi(0,r_{0,T}^{X}(f))}{\eta_{0}^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}+\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\Phi(t,r_{t,T}^{X}(f))}{\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}\partial_{t}\log(\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})\,\mathrm{d}t
=η0X​QTX​Π1​rT,TX​(f)η0X​QTX​Π0​𝟙𝒳−η0X​Π1​QTX​r0,TX​(f)η0X​Π0​QTX​𝟙𝒳+∫0TΦ⁡(t,rt,TX​(f))η0X​QtX​Π0​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳​∂tlog⁡(η0X​QtX​Π0​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳)​𝑑t\displaystyle=\frac{\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{T}^{X}\Pi_{1}r_{T,T}^{X}(f)}{\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{T}^{X}\Pi_{0}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}-\frac{\eta_{0}^{X}\Pi_{1}Q_{T}^{X}r_{0,T}^{X}(f)}{\eta_{0}^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}+\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\Phi(t,r_{t,T}^{X}(f))}{\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}\partial_{t}\log(\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})\,\mathrm{d}t
=−η0X​Π1​QTX​r0,TX​(f)η0X​Π0​QTX​𝟙𝒳+∫0TΦ⁡(t,rt,TX​(f))η0X​QtX​Π0​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳​∂tlog⁡(η0X​QtX​Π0​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳)​dt,\displaystyle=-\frac{\eta_{0}^{X}\Pi_{1}Q_{T}^{X}r_{0,T}^{X}(f)}{\eta_{0}^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}+\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\Phi(t,r_{t,T}^{X}(f))}{\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}\partial_{t}\log(\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})\,\mathrm{d}t,
using rt,TX​(f)∈Π0​ℓ∞​(𝒳)r^{X}_{t,T}(f)\in\Pi_{0}\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}) for any t∈[0,T]t\in[0,T] to cancel the first boundary term.
Using a bound identical to (79) on the numerator of the remaining boundary term, we get
|η0X​Π1​QTX​r0,TX​(f)|≤‖η0X‖∗​‖Π1​QTX​r0,TX​(f)‖\displaystyle|\eta_{0}^{X}\Pi_{1}Q_{T}^{X}r_{0,T}^{X}(f)|\leq\|\eta_{0}^{X}\|_{*}\|\Pi_{1}Q_{T}^{X}r_{0,T}^{X}(f)\|
(88)
≤(C7X​e−(λX+γX)​Tζ+C7X′​e−(λX+γX)​Tζ)​‖f‖,\displaystyle\leq\left(C_{7}^{X}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})T}}{\zeta}+C_{7}^{{}^{\prime}X}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})T}}{\zeta}\right)\|f\|,
where, by convention, each constant CjXC_{j}^{X} is defined by substituting Z←XZ\leftarrow X in the definition of CjZC_{j}^{Z}. The boundary term is then controlled using the lower bound (63) by
|η0X​Π1​QTX​r0,TX​(f)η0X​Π0​QTX​𝟙𝒳|≤e−γX​T​C7X+C7X′C1X​ζ​‖f‖.\displaystyle\left|\frac{\eta_{0}^{X}\Pi_{1}Q_{T}^{X}r_{0,T}^{X}(f)}{\eta_{0}^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}\right|\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}T}\frac{C_{7}^{X}+C_{7}^{{}^{\prime}X}}{C_{1}^{X}\zeta}\|f\|.
(89)
We now control the integrand in (87) pointwise. Using the canonical modeling identity νX​Π0=νX\nu^{X}\Pi_{0}=\nu^{X}, which implies Π1=PX​Π1\Pi_{1}=P^{X}\Pi_{1}, as well as an identical estimate to (76), we have
|Φ⁡(t,rt,TX​(f))|\displaystyle|\Phi(t,r_{t,T}^{X}(f))|
=|η0X​QtX​PX​Π1​QT−tX​rt,TX​(f)|\displaystyle=|\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}P^{X}\Pi_{1}Q_{T-t}^{X}r_{t,T}^{X}(f)|
(90)
≤CγX​e−(λX+γX)​t​‖Π1​QT−tX​rt,TX​(f)‖\displaystyle\leq C_{\gamma}^{X}\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})t}\|\Pi_{1}Q_{T-t}^{X}r_{t,T}^{X}(f)\|
≤CγX​e−(λX+γX)​t​(C7X​e−(λX+γX)​(T−t)ζ+C7X′​e−(λX+γX)​(T−t)−γX​tζ)​‖f‖\displaystyle\leq C_{\gamma}^{X}\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})t}\left(C_{7}^{X}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})(T-t)}}{\zeta}+C_{7}^{{}^{\prime}X}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})(T-t)-\gamma^{X}t}}{\zeta}\right)\|f\|
:=e−λX​T​(C9X​e−γX​t​e−γX​(T−t)ζ+C9X′​e−2​γX​t​e−γX​(T−t)ζ)​‖f‖.\displaystyle:=\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}T}\left(C_{9}^{X}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}t}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}(T-t)}}{\zeta}+C_{9}^{{}^{\prime}X}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-2\gamma^{X}t}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}(T-t)}}{\zeta}\right)\|f\|.
Dividing by (63), combining with the pointwise bound on the logarithmic derivative (72) and integrating for t∈(0,T)t\in(0,T), we get
|∫0TΦ⁡(t,rt,TX​(f))η0X​QtX​Π0​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳​∂tlog⁡(η0X​QtX​Π0​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳)​dt|\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\Phi(t,r_{t,T}^{X}(f))}{\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}\partial_{t}\log(\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})\,\mathrm{d}t\right|
(91)
≤∫0TC5XC1X​e−γX​t​(C9X​e−γX​t​e−γX​(T−t)ζ+C9X′​e−2​γX​t​e−γX​(T−t)ζ)​‖f‖​𝑑t\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\frac{C_{5}^{X}}{C_{1}^{X}}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}t}\left(C_{9}^{X}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}t}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}(T-t)}}{\zeta}+C_{9}^{{}^{\prime}X}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-2\gamma^{X}t}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}(T-t)}}{\zeta}\right)\|f\|\,\mathrm{d}t
≤(C5X​C9XC1X​ζ​γX​e−γX​T+C5X​C9X′C1X​ζ​2​γX​e−γX​T)​‖f‖=C5X​(C9X+C9X′/2)C1X​ζ​γX​e−γX​T​‖f‖,\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{C_{5}^{X}C_{9}^{X}}{C_{1}^{X}\zeta\gamma^{X}}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}T}+\frac{C_{5}^{X}C_{9}^{{}^{\prime}X}}{C_{1}^{X}\zeta 2\gamma^{X}}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}T}\right)\|f\|=\frac{C_{5}^{X}(C_{9}^{X}+C_{9}^{{}^{\prime}X}/2)}{C_{1}^{X}\zeta\gamma^{X}}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}T}\|f\|,
using (60) to estimate ∫0Te−K​γX​t​e−γX​(T−t)​𝑑t≤1(K−1)​γX​e−γX​T\int_{0}^{T}\mathrm{e}^{-K\gamma^{X}t}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}(T-t)}\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\frac{1}{(K-1)\gamma^{X}}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}T} in the last line.
It remains to treat the integral term
∫0TΦ⁡(t,𝟙𝒳)​∂tψt,TX​(ξ⋆​f)η0X​QtX​Π0​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳​𝑑t.\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\Phi(t,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})\partial_{t}\psi_{t,T}^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)}{\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}\,\mathrm{d}t.
(92)
The contribution of |∂tψt,TX​(ξ⋆​f)||\partial_{t}\psi_{t,T}^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)| is controlled using the coarse estimate (71).
For Φ⁡(t,𝟙𝒳)\Phi(t,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}), we use the Duhamel–Dyson formula (75) with no spectral decomposition of 𝟙𝒳\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}, noting Π1​𝟙𝒳=0\Pi_{1}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}=0 and inserting Π1=PX​Π1\Pi_{1}=P^{X}\Pi_{1}, and estimate
|Φ⁡(t,𝟙𝒳)|\displaystyle|\Phi(t,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})|
=|η0X​QtX​PX​Π1​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳|\displaystyle=|\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}P^{X}\Pi_{1}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}|
(93)
≤CγX​e−(λX+γX)​t​‖Π1​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳‖\displaystyle\leq C_{\gamma}^{X}\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})t}\|\Pi_{1}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\|
≤CγX​e−(λX+γX)​t​Cζ​CλX​∫0T−te−ζ⁡(T−t−u)​‖ℒ10X‖​e−λX​u​‖𝟙𝒳‖​𝑑u\displaystyle\leq C_{\gamma}^{X}\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})t}C_{\zeta}C_{\lambda}^{X}\int_{0}^{T-t}\mathrm{e}^{-\zeta(T-t-u)}\|\mathcal{L}^{X}_{10}\|\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}u}\|\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}\|\,\mathrm{d}u
=CγX​e−(λX+γX)​t​Cζ​CλX​‖ℒ10X‖​e−λX​(T−t)−e−ζ⁡(T−t)ζ−λX\displaystyle=C_{\gamma}^{X}\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})t}C_{\zeta}C_{\lambda}^{X}\|\mathcal{L}^{X}_{10}\|\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}(T-t)}-\mathrm{e}^{-\zeta(T-t)}}{\zeta-\lambda^{X}}
≤2​CγX​Cζ​CλX​‖ℒ10X‖ζ​e−λX​T​e−γX​t\displaystyle\leq\frac{2C_{\gamma}^{X}C_{\zeta}C_{\lambda}^{X}\|\mathcal{L}^{X}_{10}\|}{\zeta}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}T}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}t}
:=C10Xζ​e−λX​T​e−γX​t.\displaystyle:=\frac{C_{10}^{X}}{\zeta}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}T}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}t}.
Therefore, substituting the pointwise estimate (71) for the time derivative ∂tψt,TX​(ξ⋆​f)\partial_{t}\psi_{t,T}^{X}(\xi^{\star}f), the final integral is bounded by
|∫0TΦ⁡(t,𝟙𝒳)​∂tψt,TX​(ξ⋆​f)η0X​QtX​Π0​QT−tX​𝟙𝒳​dt|\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{T}\frac{\Phi(t,\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})\partial_{t}\psi_{t,T}^{X}(\xi^{\star}f)}{\eta_{0}^{X}Q_{t}^{X}\Pi_{0}Q_{T-t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}}}\,\mathrm{d}t\right|
(94)
≤∫0TC10XC1X​ζ​e−γX​t​(C2XC1X​e−γX​t+C3X​C4XC1X​e−2​γX​t)​e−γX​(T−t)​‖f‖​𝑑t\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\frac{C_{10}^{X}}{C_{1}^{X}\zeta}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}t}\left(\frac{C_{2}^{X}}{C_{1}^{X}}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}t}+\frac{C_{3}^{X}C_{4}^{X}}{C_{1}^{X}}\mathrm{e}^{-2\gamma^{X}t}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}(T-t)}\|f\|\mathrm{d}t
≤e−γX​T​C10XC1X​ζ​(C2XγX​C1X+C3X​C4X2​γX​C1X)​‖f‖.\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}T}\frac{C_{10}^{X}}{C_{1}^{X}\zeta}\left(\frac{C_{2}^{X}}{\gamma^{X}C_{1}^{X}}+\frac{C_{3}^{X}C_{4}^{X}}{2\gamma^{X}C_{1}^{X}}\right)\|f\|.
Summing the estimates (81), (89), (91) and (94), we arrive at
|ξ⋆​ηTX​(f)−ηTZ​(f)|≤ζ−1​(C~​e−γX​T+C~′​T​e−(γZ∧γX)​T)​‖f‖,|\xi_{\star}\eta_{T}^{X}(f)-\eta_{T}^{Z}(f)|\leq\zeta^{-1}\left(\widetilde{C}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}T}+\widetilde{C}^{\prime}T\mathrm{e}^{-(\gamma^{Z}\land\gamma^{X})T}\right)\|f\|,
(95)
with explicit constants
C~\displaystyle\widetilde{C}
=C7X+C7X′C1X+C5X​(C9X+C9X′/2)γX​C1X+C10XγX​C1X​(C2XC1X+C3X​C4X2​C1X),\displaystyle=\frac{C_{7}^{X}+C_{7}^{{}^{\prime}X}}{C_{1}^{X}}+\frac{C_{5}^{X}\left(C_{9}^{X}+C_{9}^{{}^{\prime}X}/2\right)}{\gamma^{X}C_{1}^{X}}+\frac{C_{10}^{X}}{\gamma^{X}C_{1}^{X}}\left(\frac{C_{2}^{X}}{C_{1}^{X}}+\frac{C_{3}^{X}C_{4}^{X}}{2C_{1}^{X}}\right),
(96)
C~′\displaystyle\widetilde{C}^{\prime}
=C8Z+C8Z′C1Z.\displaystyle=\frac{C_{8}^{Z}+C_{8}^{{}^{\prime}Z}}{C_{1}^{Z}}.
To make the constants more manageable, we can bound them further.
Since ‖ΠZ‖=‖ΠX‖=1\|\Pi^{Z}\|=\|\Pi^{X}\|=1, we also have ‖AZ‖=‖ΠZ​AX‖≤‖AX‖\|A^{Z}\|=\|\Pi^{Z}A^{X}\|\leq\|A^{X}\| by (45). Similarly, a direct computation gives
ℒ10X=AX​ΠX\mathcal{L}_{10}^{X}=A^{X}\Pi^{X} and ℒ01X=−ΠX​AX\mathcal{L}_{01}^{X}=-\Pi^{X}A^{X}, therefore ‖ℒ10X‖,‖ℒ01X‖≤‖AX‖\|\mathcal{L}_{10}^{X}\|,\|\mathcal{L}_{01}^{X}\|\leq\|A^{X}\|. Finally we note the estimates following from Proposition 1:
‖PX‖=‖Q0X​PX‖≤supt≥0​‖QtX​PX‖​e(λX+γX)​t≤CγX,\|P^{X}\|=\|Q_{0}^{X}P^{X}\|\leq\underset{t\geq 0}{\sup}\,\|Q_{t}^{X}P^{X}\|\mathrm{e}^{(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})t}\leq C_{\gamma}^{X},
(97)
‖hX‖=‖hX‖​‖νX‖∗=‖hX​νX‖=‖limt→∞​eλX​t​QtX‖=limt→∞|eλX​t​QtX|≤CλX,\|h^{X}\|=\|h^{X}\|\|\nu^{X}\|_{*}=\|h^{X}\nu^{X}\|=\|\underset{t\to\infty}{\lim}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{X}t}Q_{t}^{X}\|=\underset{t\to\infty}{\lim}\|\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{X}t}Q_{t}^{X}\|\leq C_{\lambda}^{X},
(98)
where we view hX​νXh^{X}\nu^{X} as a rank-one operator.
Substituting the definitions of the constants in (96), estimating using (97) and (98) and simplifying leads, with constants C=C~/‖AX‖C=\widetilde{C}/\|A^{X}\| and C′=C~′/‖AX‖C^{\prime}=\widetilde{C}^{\prime}/\|A^{X}\|, to the slightly more manageable form
C′\displaystyle C^{\prime}
≤4​CλX​CγX​CγZ​Cζ​‖AX‖CZ​(η0)​(CγX+(CλX)3​CγZCZ​(η0)),\displaystyle\leq\frac{4C_{\lambda}^{X}C_{\gamma}^{X}C_{\gamma}^{Z}C_{\zeta}\|A^{X}\|}{C^{Z}(\eta_{0})}\left(C_{\gamma}^{X}+\frac{(C_{\lambda}^{X})^{3}C_{\gamma}^{Z}}{C^{Z}(\eta_{0})}\right),
(99)
C\displaystyle C
