≤4​CλX​(CγX)2​CζCX​(η0)​(1+(CλX)3CX​(η0))\displaystyle\leq\frac{4C_{\lambda}^{X}(C_{\gamma}^{X})^{2}C_{\zeta}}{C^{X}(\eta_{0})}\left(1+\frac{(C_{\lambda}^{X})^{3}}{C^{X}(\eta_{0})}\right)
+4​(CλX)3​(CγX)4​Cζ​‖AX‖γX​CX​(η0)2​(1+(CλX)32​CX​(η0))\displaystyle+\frac{4(C_{\lambda}^{X})^{3}(C_{\gamma}^{X})^{4}C_{\zeta}\|A^{X}\|}{\gamma^{X}C^{X}(\eta_{0})^{2}}\left(1+\frac{(C_{\lambda}^{X})^{3}}{2C^{X}(\eta_{0})}\right)
+4​(CλX)3​(CγX)3​Cζ​‖AX‖γX​CX​(η0)2​(1+(CλX)3​CγX2​CX​(η0))\displaystyle+\frac{4(C_{\lambda}^{X})^{3}(C_{\gamma}^{X})^{3}C_{\zeta}\|A^{X}\|}{\gamma^{X}C^{X}(\eta_{0})^{2}}\left(1+\frac{(C_{\lambda}^{X})^{3}C_{\gamma}^{X}}{2C^{X}(\eta_{0})}\right)
where the constants appearing in the denominators are
CZ​(η0)=η0Z​(hZ)​min𝒵​ΠZ​hXhZ,CX​(η0)=η0X​(hX)​min𝒳​ΠX​hXhX.C^{Z}(\eta_{0})=\eta_{0}^{Z}(h^{Z})\underset{\mathcal{Z}}{\min}\frac{\Pi^{Z}h^{X}}{h^{Z}},\qquad C^{X}(\eta_{0})=\eta_{0}^{X}(h^{X})\underset{\mathcal{X}}{\min}\frac{\Pi^{X}h^{X}}{h^{X}}.
(100)
Note that the ℓ∞\ell^{\infty} quasimode estimates (53) and (54) do not provide further control of these constants. Doing so would require instead hYh^{Y}-norm estimates.
The total variation bound (52) then follows from (95) by the duality formula (24) taking the supremum over ff such that ‖f‖≤1\|f\|\leq 1 in (95).
2.5.3 Proof of the quasimode estimates (53) and (54)
We now show the second item in Theorem 5. Using the formula (75) on v=hXv=h^{X}, we get, for any t≥0t\geq 0,
Π1​hX=eλX​t​(et​ℒ11X​Π1​hX+∫0te(t−s)​ℒ11X​ℒ10X​e−λX​s​hX​𝑑s).\Pi_{1}h^{X}=\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{X}t}\left(\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}^{X}_{11}}\Pi_{1}h^{X}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{e}^{(t-s)\mathcal{L}^{X}_{11}}\mathcal{L}^{X}_{10}\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}s}h^{X}\,\mathrm{d}s\right).
(101)
Estimating, this gives
‖Π1​hX‖\displaystyle\|\Pi_{1}h^{X}\|
≤Cζ​e−(ζ−λX)​t​‖Π1​hX‖+Cζ​1−e−(ζ−λX)​tζ−λX​‖ℒ10X‖​‖hX‖,\displaystyle\leq C_{\zeta}\mathrm{e}^{-(\zeta-\lambda^{X})t}\|\Pi_{1}h^{X}\|+C_{\zeta}\frac{1-\mathrm{e}^{-(\zeta-\lambda^{X})t}}{\zeta-\lambda^{X}}\|\mathcal{L}_{10}^{X}\|\|h^{X}\|,
(102)
and the bound (53) follows by letting t→∞t\to\infty, using ‖ℒ10X‖≤‖AX‖\|\mathcal{L}_{10}^{X}\|\leq\|A^{X}\| and the estimate (98).
To obtain the second estimate, we write
(ℒZ+λZ)​(ΠZ​hX−hZ)\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}^{Z}+\lambda^{Z})(\Pi^{Z}h^{X}-h^{Z})
=ℒZ​ΠZ​hX+λX​ΠZ​hX\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}^{Z}\Pi^{Z}h^{X}+\lambda^{X}\Pi^{Z}h^{X}
(103)
=[ℒZ​ΠZ−ΠZ​ℒX]​hX\displaystyle=\left[\mathcal{L}^{Z}\Pi^{Z}-\Pi^{Z}\mathcal{L}^{X}\right]h^{X}
=AZ​hX.\displaystyle=A^{Z}h^{X}.
Noting that νZ​(ΠZ​hX)=νX​(hX)=νZ​(hZ)=1\nu^{Z}(\Pi^{Z}h^{X})=\nu^{X}(h^{X})=\nu^{Z}(h^{Z})=1 and using νZ​AZ=0\nu^{Z}A^{Z}=0, we may insert PZP^{Z} on both sides, giving
(ℒZ+λZ)​PZ​(ΠZ​hX−hZ)=PZ​AZ​hX,(\mathcal{L}^{Z}+\lambda^{Z})P^{Z}(\Pi^{Z}h^{X}-h^{Z})=P^{Z}A^{Z}h^{X},
(104)
which is a Poisson equation on PZ​ℓ∞​(𝒵)P^{Z}\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}). Since λZ\lambda^{Z} is a simple eigenvalue by Perron–Frobenius, this equation is well-posed on PZ​ℓ∞​(𝒵)P^{Z}\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z}).
Applying
(ℒZ+λZ)|PZ​ℓ∞​(𝒵)−1=−∫0∞eλZ​tQtZdt|PZ​ℓ∞​(𝒵),\left.\left(\mathcal{L}^{Z}+\lambda^{Z}\right)\right|_{P^{Z}\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z})}^{-1}=-\left.\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{Z}t}Q_{t}^{Z}\,\mathrm{d}t\right|_{P^{Z}\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{Z})},
(105)
we obtain
ΠZhX−hZ=PZ(ΠZhX−hZ)=−∫0∞eλZ​tQtZPZAZhXdt,\Pi^{Z}h^{X}-h^{Z}=P^{Z}(\Pi^{Z}h^{X}-h^{Z})=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\lambda^{Z}t}Q_{t}^{Z}P^{Z}A^{Z}h^{X}\,\mathrm{d}t,
(106)
which gives the estimate
‖ΠZ​hX−hZ‖≤CγZγZ​‖AZ​hX‖.\|\Pi^{Z}h^{X}-h^{Z}\|\leq\frac{C_{\gamma}^{Z}}{\gamma^{Z}}\|A^{Z}h^{X}\|.
(107)
At this point, we use (77) to insert AZ​hX=AZ​Π1​hXA^{Z}h^{X}=A^{Z}\Pi_{1}h^{X} to estimate
‖ΠZ​hX−hZ‖≤CγZγZ​‖AZ‖​‖Π1​hX‖≤CγZγZ​‖AX‖​‖Π1​hX‖,\|\Pi^{Z}h^{X}-h^{Z}\|\leq\frac{C_{\gamma}^{Z}}{\gamma^{Z}}\|A^{Z}\|\|\Pi_{1}h^{X}\|\leq\frac{C_{\gamma}^{Z}}{\gamma^{Z}}\|A^{X}\|\|\Pi_{1}h^{X}\|,
(108)
which gives (54) upon substituting the estimate (53) on the right-hand side of this inequality.
2.5.4 Proof of the two-sided estimate (55)
We finally show the third item in Theorem 5. By the duality formula (24), we have
dTV​(ξ⋆​ηtX,ξ⋆​νX)≤dTV​(ηtX,νX).\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X},\xi_{\star}\nu^{X})\leq\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\eta_{t}^{X},\nu^{X}).
(109)
Now, for any f∈ℓ∞​(𝒳)f\in\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}), we may write
ηtX​(f)−νX​(f)\displaystyle\eta_{t}^{X}(f)-\nu^{X}(f)
=ηtX​(Π0​f)−νX​(Π0​f)+ηtX​(Π1​f)−νX​(Π1​f)\displaystyle=\eta_{t}^{X}(\Pi_{0}f)-\nu^{X}(\Pi_{0}f)+\eta_{t}^{X}(\Pi_{1}f)-\nu^{X}(\Pi_{1}f)
(110)
=ξ⋆​ηtX​(ΠZ​f)−ξ⋆​νX​(ΠZ​f)+ηtX​(Π1​f),\displaystyle=\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X}(\Pi^{Z}f)-\xi_{\star}\nu^{X}(\Pi^{Z}f)+\eta_{t}^{X}(\Pi_{1}f),
where we used (41) to deduce νX​Π1=0\nu^{X}\Pi_{1}=0.
Since ‖ΠZ​f‖≤‖f‖\|\Pi^{Z}f\|\leq\|f\|, we have
sup‖f‖≤1​[ξ⋆​ηtX​(ΠZ​f)−ξ⋆​νX​(ΠZ​f)]\displaystyle\underset{\|f\|\leq 1}{\sup}\,\left[\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X}(\Pi^{Z}f)-\xi_{\star}\nu^{X}(\Pi^{Z}f)\right]
≤sup‖g‖≤1​ξ⋆​ηtX​(g)−ξ⋆​νX​(g)\displaystyle\leq\underset{\|g\|\leq 1}{\sup}\,\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X}(g)-\xi_{\star}\nu^{X}(g)
(111)
=dTV​(ξ⋆​ηtX,ξ⋆​νX),\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X},\xi_{\star}\nu^{X}),
which leaves a term
ηtX​(Π1​f)=η0X​(QtX​Π1​f)η0X​(QtX​𝟙𝒳)=η0X​(QtX​PX​Π1​f)η0X​(QtX​𝟙𝒳)\eta_{t}^{X}(\Pi_{1}f)=\frac{\eta_{0}^{X}(Q_{t}^{X}\Pi_{1}f)}{\eta_{0}^{X}(Q_{t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})}=\frac{\eta_{0}^{X}(Q_{t}^{X}P^{X}\Pi_{1}f)}{\eta_{0}^{X}(Q_{t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})}
(112)
to estimate. Using the commutation ℒX​PX=PX​ℒX\mathcal{L}^{X}P^{X}=P^{X}\mathcal{L}^{X} and the Feynman–Kac formula (12), we split
∂tη0X​(QtX​PX​Π1)=η0X​(QtX​PX​ℒX​Π1)=η0X​(QtX​PX​Π1​ℒ11X)+η0X​(QtX​PX​ℒ01X),\partial_{t}\eta_{0}^{X}(Q_{t}^{X}P^{X}\Pi_{1})=\eta_{0}^{X}(Q_{t}^{X}P^{X}\mathcal{L}^{X}\Pi_{1})=\eta_{0}^{X}(Q_{t}^{X}P^{X}\Pi_{1}\mathcal{L}_{11}^{X})+\eta_{0}^{X}(Q_{t}^{X}P^{X}\mathcal{L}_{01}^{X}),
(113)
which by Duhamel–Dyson’s formula gives, after application to ff,
η0X​(QtX​PX​Π1​f)=η0X​(PX​et​ℒ11X​Π1​f)+∫0tη0X​(QsX​PX​ℒ01X​e(t−s)​ℒ11X​Π1​f)​𝑑s,\eta_{0}^{X}(Q_{t}^{X}P^{X}\Pi_{1}f)=\eta_{0}^{X}(P^{X}\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}_{11}^{X}}\Pi_{1}f)+\int_{0}^{t}\eta_{0}^{X}(Q_{s}^{X}P^{X}\mathcal{L}_{01}^{X}\mathrm{e}^{(t-s)\mathcal{L}_{11}^{X}}\Pi_{1}f)\,\mathrm{d}s,
(114)
using Π1​et​ℒ11X=et​ℒ11X​Π1\Pi_{1}\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}_{11}^{X}}=\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}_{11}^{X}}\Pi_{1}. Estimating, we find
|η0X​(QtX​PX​Π1​f)|\displaystyle|\eta_{0}^{X}(Q_{t}^{X}P^{X}\Pi_{1}f)|
≤‖η0X‖∗​‖PX‖​Cζ​e−ζ​t​‖f‖+∫0t‖η0X‖∗​CγX​e−(λX+γX)​s​‖ℒ01X‖​Cζ​e−ζ⁡(t−s)​‖f‖​𝑑s\displaystyle\leq\|\eta_{0}^{X}\|_{*}\|P^{X}\|C_{\zeta}\mathrm{e}^{-\zeta t}\|f\|+\int_{0}^{t}\|\eta_{0}^{X}\|_{*}C_{\gamma}^{X}\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})s}\|\mathcal{L}_{01}^{X}\|C_{\zeta}\mathrm{e}^{-\zeta(t-s)}\|f\|\,\mathrm{d}s
(115)
≤‖PX‖​Cζ​e−ζ​t​‖f​‖+CγX‖​ℒ01X‖​Cζ​e−(λX+γX)​t−e−ζ​tζ−λX−γX​‖f‖\displaystyle\leq\|P^{X}\|C_{\zeta}\mathrm{e}^{-\zeta t}\|f\|+C_{\gamma}^{X}\|\mathcal{L}_{01}^{X}\|C_{\zeta}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})t}-\mathrm{e}^{-\zeta t}}{\zeta-\lambda^{X}-\gamma^{X}}\|f\|
≤CγX​Cζ​(e−ζ​t+2​‖AX‖​e−(λX+γX)​tζ)​‖f‖,\displaystyle\leq C_{\gamma}^{X}C_{\zeta}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\zeta t}+2\|A^{X}\|\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(\lambda^{X}+\gamma^{X})t}}{\zeta}\right)\|f\|,
where we used (97) and ‖ℒ01X‖≤‖AX‖\|\mathcal{L}_{01}^{X}\|\leq\|A^{X}\| in the last line.
On the other hand, we have the lower bound
η0X​(QtX​𝟙𝒳)≥1‖hX‖​η0X​(QtX​hX)=e−λX​t‖hX‖​η0X​(hX),\eta_{0}^{X}(Q_{t}^{X}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}})\geq\frac{1}{\|h^{X}\|}\eta_{0}^{X}(Q_{t}^{X}h^{X})=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\lambda^{X}t}}{\|h^{X}\|}\eta_{0}^{X}(h^{X}),\,
(116)
which gives
sup‖f‖≤1​ηtX​(Π1​f)\displaystyle\underset{\|f\|\leq 1}{\sup}\,\eta_{t}^{X}(\Pi_{1}f)
≤‖hX‖η0X​(hX)​CγX​Cζ​(e−(ζ−λX)​t+2​‖AX‖​e−γX​tζ)\displaystyle\leq\frac{\|h^{X}\|}{\eta_{0}^{X}(h^{X})}C_{\gamma}^{X}C_{\zeta}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-(\zeta-\lambda^{X})t}+2\|A^{X}\|\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}t}}{\zeta}\right)
(117)
≤CλX​CγX​Cζη0X​(hX)​(e−(ζ−λX)​t+2​‖AX‖​e−γX​tζ).\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{\lambda}^{X}C_{\gamma}^{X}C_{\zeta}}{\eta_{0}^{X}(h^{X})}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-(\zeta-\lambda^{X})t}+2\|A^{X}\|\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}t}}{\zeta}\right).
Taking the supremum with respect to ‖f‖≤1\|f\|\leq 1 in (110) and using the estimates (111) and (117), we get estimate (55) when combined with (109).
∎
2.6 Uniform coarse-graining estimates in the adiabatic limit
We describe the behavior of the estimate (52) provided by Theorem 5 on a more specific class of models, in which the timescale separation ζ\zeta is tuned by a small adiabatic parameter ε\varepsilon.
Let us fix a partition of the state space
𝒳=⨆1≤m≤M​𝒳m,\mathcal{X}=\underset{1\leq m\leq M}{\bigsqcup}\mathcal{X}_{m},
(118)
where each 𝒳m\mathcal{X}_{m} is a fiber, that is a level set of the collective variable
ξ⁡(x)=∑m=1Mm​𝟙𝒳m​(x)\xi(x)=\sum_{m=1}^{M}m\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}_{m}}(x)
(119)
from 𝒳\mathcal{X} to 𝒵={1,…,M}\mathcal{Z}=\{1,\dots,M\}.
We consider the case where the killed semigroup is generated by the sum of three operators
ℒεX=ℒmac−K+ε−1​ℒmic,\mathcal{L}^{X}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{L}_{\rm mac}-K+\varepsilon^{-1}\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{mic}},
(120)
where ℒmac\mathcal{L}_{\rm mac} is the generator of an irreducible mass-conserving jump process on 𝒳\mathcal{X}, KK is a nonnegative, nonzero diagonal operator encoding the jump rates to the boundary (i.e. killing rates),
and ℒmic\mathcal{L}_{\rm mic} is the generator of a fiber-reducible jump process, that is, a direct sum
ℒmic=⨁m=1MAm,\mathcal{L}_{\rm mic}=\bigoplus_{m=1}^{M}A_{m},
(121)
where AmA_{m} generates an irreducible mass-conserving jump process on 𝒳m\mathcal{X}_{m}.
We call the limit ε→0\varepsilon\to 0 the adiabatic regime, and indicate the ε\varepsilon-dependence of various quantities with subscripts.
The initial distributions η0X\eta_{0}^{X} and η0Z\eta_{0}^{Z} are fixed independently from ε\varepsilon.
We denote by μm∈𝒫⁡(𝒳m)\mu_{m}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}_{m}) the unique AmA_{m}-invariant probability measure. Define the operators
P0s=∑m=1M𝟙𝒳m​μm,ℒavg=P0s​(ℒmac−K)​P0s.P_{0}^{\rm s}=\sum_{m=1}^{M}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{X}_{m}}\mu_{m},\qquad\mathcal{L}_{\rm avg}=P_{0}^{\rm s}(\mathcal{L}_{\rm mac}-K)P_{0}^{\rm s}.
(122)
The operator P0sP_{0}^{\rm s} is a rank-MM projector onto ΠεX​ℓ∞​(𝒳)\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{X}\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}). In general ΠεX≠P0s\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{X}\neq P_{0}^{\rm s}, since νεX\nu^{X}_{\varepsilon}-conditional averages over 𝒳m\mathcal{X}_{m} may not agree with averages under μm\mu_{m}.
Denote by (λ0s,h0s,ν0s)∈(0,∞)×ℓ∞​(𝒳)×𝒫⁡(𝒳)(\lambda_{0}^{\rm s},h_{0}^{\rm s},\nu_{0}^{\rm s})\in(0,\infty)\times\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{X})\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}) the principal eigentriplet of ℒavg\mathcal{L}_{\rm avg} as an operator on P0s​ℓ∞​(𝒳)P_{0}^{\rm s}\ell^{\infty}(\mathcal{X}), normalized so that ν0s​(h0s)=1\nu_{0}^{\rm s}(h_{0}^{\rm s})=1.
Its uniqueness and the strict positivity of h0sh_{0}^{\rm s} and ν0s\nu_{0}^{\rm s} will be checked in the proof of Theorem 7.
Using spectral perturbation theory [34, Chapter 2], we show the following result.
Theorem 7.
Assume that the compatibility condition ξ⋆​η0X=η0Z\xi_{\star}\eta_{0}^{X}=\eta_{0}^{Z} holds. Then, there exist C,ε0>0C,\varepsilon_{0}>0 such that, for any ε∈(0,ε0)\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}),
‖ΠεX−P0s‖,|λεX−λ0s|,|min𝒳​ΠεX​hεXhεX−1|,|min𝒵​ΠεZ​hεXhεZ−1|≤C​ε,\left\|\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{X}-P_{0}^{\rm s}\right\|,\,\left|\lambda_{\varepsilon}^{X}-\lambda_{0}^{\rm s}\right|,\,\left|\underset{\mathcal{X}}{\min}\,\frac{\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{X}h_{\varepsilon}^{X}}{h_{\varepsilon}^{X}}-1\right|,\,\left|\underset{\mathcal{Z}}{\min}\frac{\Pi_{\varepsilon}^{Z}h_{\varepsilon}^{X}}{h_{\varepsilon}^{Z}}-1\right|\leq C\varepsilon,
(123)
and the Assumptions of Theorem 5 are verified, with constants
γεX,γεZ≥1/C,Cλ,εX,Cλ,εZ,Cγ,εX,Cγ,εZ,Cζ,ε,‖AεX‖≤C,ζε≥1/(C​ε).\gamma_{\varepsilon}^{X},\gamma_{\varepsilon}^{Z}\geq 1/C,\qquad C_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{X},C_{\lambda,\varepsilon}^{Z},C_{\gamma,\varepsilon}^{X},C_{\gamma,\varepsilon}^{Z},C_{\zeta,\varepsilon},\|A_{\varepsilon}^{X}\|\leq C,\qquad\zeta_{\varepsilon}\geq 1/(C\varepsilon).
(124)
Moreover,
|η0X​(hεX)−η0X​(h0s)|,|η0Z​(hεZ)−η0X​(h0s)|≤C​ε,η0X​(h0s)≥1/C.\left|\eta_{0}^{X}(h_{\varepsilon}^{X})-\eta_{0}^{X}(h_{0}^{\rm s})\right|,\,\left|\eta_{0}^{Z}(h_{\varepsilon}^{Z})-\eta_{0}^{X}(h_{0}^{\rm s})\right|\leq C\varepsilon,\qquad\eta_{0}^{X}(h_{0}^{\rm s})\geq 1/C.
(125)
A self-contained proof is given in Appendix A.
Theorem 7 then implies uniform-in-ε\varepsilon versions of Theorem 5 and Corollary 6.
Corollary 8.
Assume that the compatibility condition ξ⋆​η0X=η0Z\xi_{\star}\eta_{0}^{X}=\eta_{0}^{Z} holds. There exist C,ε0>0C,\varepsilon_{0}>0 such that for all ε∈(0,ε0)\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}), it holds
supt≥0​dTV​(ηt,εZ,ξ⋆​ηt,εX)≤C​ε,\underset{t\geq 0}{\sup}\,\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}\left(\eta_{t,\varepsilon}^{Z},\xi_{\star}\eta_{t,\varepsilon}^{X}\right)\leq C\varepsilon,
(126)
and for t∗​(ε)=2​C​ε​|log⁡ε|t_{*}(\varepsilon)=2C\varepsilon|\log\varepsilon|,
supt≥t∗​(ε)​|dTV​(ηt,εZ,νεZ)−dTV​(ηt,εX,νεX)|≤C​ε.\underset{t\geq t_{*}(\varepsilon)}{\sup}\,\left|\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}\left(\eta_{t,\varepsilon}^{Z},\nu_{\varepsilon}^{Z}\right)-\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}\left(\eta_{t,\varepsilon}^{X},\nu_{\varepsilon}^{X}\right)\right|\leq C\varepsilon.
(127)
Proof.
We begin by proving (126). From (52) and Theorem 7, there exist C,ε0>0C,\varepsilon_{0}>0 such that for all ε∈(0,ε0)\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}), we have, for any T≥0T\geq 0, the bound
dTV(ξ⋆ηT,εX,ηT,εZ)≤Cε(1+T)e−T/C≤C2e−(C−1)/Cε,\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}(\xi_{\star}\eta_{T,\varepsilon}^{X},\eta_{T,\varepsilon}^{Z})\leq C\varepsilon(1+T)\mathrm{e}^{-T/C}\leq C^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-(C-1)/C}\varepsilon,
(128)
maximizing the middle expression with respect to TT to obtain the final bound. This proves (126) upon increasing CC. The uniform control on the constants appearing in (52) can be verified using their explicit form (see Equations (99) and (100)).
The estimate (127) requires the burn-in time t∗​(ε)>0t_{*}(\varepsilon)>0 to control a boundary layer term e−(ζε−λεX)​t\mathrm{e}^{-(\zeta_{\varepsilon}-\lambda_{\varepsilon}^{X})t} appearing in (56).
By (56) and Theorem 7, there exist C,ε0C,\varepsilon_{0} such that, for all ε∈(0,ε0)\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0}), ζε≥1/(C​ε)\zeta_{\varepsilon}\geq 1/(C\varepsilon), λεX≤C\lambda_{\varepsilon}^{X}\leq C, and that for all T≥0T\geq 0,
|dTV(ηT,εZ,νεZ)−dTV(ηT,εX,νεX)|≤C(e−(1/(C​ε)−C)​T+εe−T/C)+εC(1+T)e−T/C.\left|\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}(\eta_{T,\varepsilon}^{Z},\nu_{\varepsilon}^{Z})-\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}(\eta_{T,\varepsilon}^{X},\nu_{\varepsilon}^{X})\right|\leq C\left(\mathrm{e}^{-(1/(C\varepsilon)-C)T}+\varepsilon\mathrm{e}^{-T/C}\right)+\varepsilon C(1+T)\mathrm{e}^{-T/C}.
(129)
For ε<1/(2​C2)\varepsilon<1/(2C^{2}), 1/(C​ε)−C>1/(2​C​ε)1/(C\varepsilon)-C>1/(2C\varepsilon), which implies that for T≥2​C​ε​|log⁡ε|T\geq 2C\varepsilon|\log\varepsilon|,
e−(1/(C​ε)−C)​T≤e−T/(2Cε)≤e−|log⁡ε|=ε,\mathrm{e}^{-(1/(C\varepsilon)-C)T}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-T/(2C\varepsilon)}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-|\log\varepsilon|}=\varepsilon,
(130)
provided ε≤1\varepsilon\leq 1. This leaves us, for T≥t∗​(ε)T\geq t_{*}(\varepsilon), with
|dTV(ηT,εZ,νεZ)−dTV(ηT,εX,νεX)|≤εC(1+(2+T)e−T/C)\displaystyle\left|\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}(\eta_{T,\varepsilon}^{Z},\nu_{\varepsilon}^{Z})-\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}(\eta_{T,\varepsilon}^{X},\nu_{\varepsilon}^{X})\right|\leq\varepsilon C\left(1+(2+T)\mathrm{e}^{-T/C}\right)
(131)
≤εC(1+Ce−(C−2)/C),\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon C(1+C\mathrm{e}^{-(C-2)/C}),
which concludes the proof upon increasing CC and decreasing ε0\varepsilon_{0}.
∎
We illustrate the adiabatic limit numerically in Section 4.1, on a simple killed jump process with four states.
2.7 Particle approximations of non-linear semigroups
Online definitions of the decorrelation time in the parallel replica method require access to numerical approximations of the non-linear semigroup ηtX\eta^{X}_{t}.
For many systems of practical interest, approximations based on the formula (11) are intractable, as they require direct propagation of the linear semigroup QtXQ_{t}^{X}. This may not be possible when 𝒳\mathcal{X} is large and ℒX\mathcal{L}^{X} does not have a convenient sparse representation.
For such cases, which appear for instance in molecular dynamics, one can still approximate ηtX\eta^{X}_{t} using interacting particle systems, which is the choice made in the generalized parallel replica method of [4].
The simplest approximation is given by the Fleming–Viot process, a type of branching process defined recursively as follows.
Definition 9 (Fleming–Viot process).
Fix an integer N≥1N\geq 1. The NN-particle Fleming–Viot process 𝐗=(X(n))1≤n≤N{\bf X}=(X^{(n)})_{1\leq n\leq N} is an 𝒳N\mathcal{X}^{N}-valued stochastic process, whose paths are constructed as follows.
A: 
Initialization. Fix the initial time T0=0T_{0}=0, branching count k=0k=0 and initial point 𝐗0=(X0(j),0)1≤j≤N{\bf X}_{0}=\left(X^{(j),0}_{0}\right)_{1\leq j\leq N}.
B: 
Evolution. For any k≥0k\geq 0, sample on the time interval [Tk,Tk+1)[T_{k},T_{k+1}) (with Tk+1T_{k+1} defined in (133)), independent Markovian trajectories (X(j),k)1≤j≤N(X^{(j),k})_{1\leq j\leq N} on 𝒳¯\overline{\mathcal{X}} with infinitesimal generator ℒX\mathcal{L}^{X}, starting from XTk(j),kX^{(j),k}_{T_{k}}.
Here we define
Tk+1j=inf{t>Tk:Xt(j),k=∂𝒳},Jk=argmin1≤j≤NTk+1j.T_{k+1}^{j}=\inf\,\left\{t>T_{k}:\,X_{t}^{(j),k}=\partial^{\mathcal{X}}\right\},\qquad J_{k}=\underset{1\leq j\leq N}{\rm argmin}\,T_{k+1}^{j}.
(132)
Note that the JkJ_{k} are almost-surely uniquely defined.
C: 
Branching and iteration. Define the branching time
Tk+1:=Tk+1Jk,T_{k+1}:=T^{J_{k}}_{k+1},
(133)
and set Xt(j)=Xt(j),kX_{t}^{(j)}=X^{(j),k}_{t} for t∈(Tk,Tk+1)t\in(T_{k},T_{k+1}).
Then, set
∀j≠Jk,XTk+1(j),k+1=XTk+1−(j),k∈𝒳​ almost surely,\forall\,j\neq J_{k},\qquad X_{T_{k+1}}^{(j),k+1}=X_{T_{k+1}^{-}}^{(j),k}\in\mathcal{X}\,\text{ almost surely},
(134)
and sample independently
XTk+1(Jk),k+1∼1N−1​∑j≠JkδXTk+1−(j),k,X_{T_{k+1}}^{(J_{k}),k+1}\mathrel{\overset{}{\scalebox{1.5}[1]{$\sim$}}}\frac{1}{N-1}\sum_{j\neq J_{k}}\delta_{X^{(j),k}_{T_{k+1}^{-}}},
(135)
where Yt−=lims↑t​YsY_{t^{-}}=\underset{s\uparrow t}{\lim}\,Y_{s} is well-defined whenever YY is càdlàg.
Set XTk+1(j)=XTk+1(j),k+1X^{(j)}_{T_{k+1}}=X_{T_{k+1}}^{(j),k+1} for all 1≤j≤N1\leq j\leq N, and proceed from step B. with k←k+1{k\leftarrow k+1}.
In plainer terms, trajectories of the original jump process XX are simulated independently, and each time a replica reaches the boundary ∂𝒳\partial^{\mathcal{X}}, its state is branched from one of the surviving replicas, sampled independently and uniformly at random.
We stress that a killed replica only inherits the state of a surviving replica during a branching event, but retains its past trajectory.
The same construction may be attempted for more general choices of one-particle càdlàg dynamics (i.e. 𝒳\mathcal{X} need not be finite nor ℒX\mathcal{L}^{X} generate a jump process).
By viewing the Fleming–Viot process 𝐗\bf X as a 𝒫⁡(𝒳)\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X})-valued process via the dynamics of the empirical measure
η^t,NX=1N​∑n=1NδXt(n)∈𝒫⁡(𝒳),\widehat{\eta}_{t,N}^{X}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\delta_{X_{t}^{(n)}}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}),
(136)
we obtain a particle approximation of the non-linear flow ηtX\eta_{t}^{X}. It is indeed expected that η^t,NX\widehat{\eta}_{t,N}^{X} converge to the non-linear flow ηtX\eta_{t}^{X} in the limit N→∞N\to\infty for many choices of ℒX\mathcal{L}^{X} (and some appropriate choice of topology).
When such a property is verified, one says that propagation of chaos holds.
In [53], such approximation results have been obtained for general choices of the one-particle Markovian dynamics in Definition 9, but these only prove propagation of chaos at fixed times t>0t>0.
For uniform-in-time results, we refer to [13] in the case of jump processes. The case of killed diffusion processes is still open in generality, but partial results have been obtained, see for instance [31, 33, 32, 11].
The Fleming–Viot process is a component of the generalized parallel replica algorithm proposed in [4, 28], where it is used to generate approximately independent samples from the QSD, as well as to define the decorrelation time tcorrt_{\rm corr} as a stopping time for the natural filtration of 𝐗\mathbf{X}.
3 On-the-fly quasi-stationarity diagnostics
In this section, we present the main methodological contribution of this paper, which is a simulation-based inference [15, 16] method to define on-the-fly decorrelation times for Fleming–Viot processes.
The method combines the theoretical results of Section 2 with a Bayesian inference approach to the estimation of reference decorrelation times for the surrogate processes ZZ identified in the previous section (for some notion of reference decorrelation times to be defined below, see Equation (140)).
These two components play complementary roles: i) the results of Section 2 suggest that a good choice of collective variable allows to deduce Yaglom convergence (4) from the quasi-stationarity of a simpler process (the canonical model ZZ on the smaller space 𝒵\mathcal{Z}),
and ii) the Bayesian framework places a prior on the set of possible ZZ, and the decorrelation time is defined using posterior probabilities of decorrelation, given observations of Fleming–Viot approximations of ηtZ≈ξ⋆​ηtX\eta_{t}^{Z}\approx\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X}.
The only on-the-fly quasi-stationarity diagnostic proposed in the literature is, to the best of our knowledge, the MCMC-inspired diagnostic of [4].
As such, it will be our main point of comparison in our numerical experiments.
We first recall how this diagnostic is defined in Section 3.1, and then present our simulation-based inference method in Section 3.2. We finally discuss our methodological assumptions in Section 3.3.
3.1 Gelman–Rubin diagnostic
Given a real-valued collective variable ξ:𝒳→𝒵⊂ℝ{\xi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathbb{R}} and a Fleming–Viot approximation 𝐗=(X(n))1≤n≤N{\bf X}=(X^{(n)})_{1\leq n\leq N} of ηtX\eta_{t}^{X}, the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic is defined using the variance ratio
RT,ξX=Var⁡(ξ⋆​1T​∫0Tη^t,NX​𝑑t)1N​∑n=1NVar⁡(ξ⋆​1T​∫0TδXt(n)​𝑑t),R_{T,\xi}^{X}=\frac{\mathrm{Var}\left(\xi_{\star}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\widehat{\eta}_{t,N}^{X}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)}{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\mathrm{Var}\left(\xi_{\star}\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\delta_{X_{t}^{(n)}}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)},
(137)
denoting by Var⁡(π){\rm Var}(\pi) the variance of X∼πX\sim\pi for any univariate distribution π∈𝒫⁡(ℝ)\pi\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}), and where we recall η^t,NX\widehat{\eta}_{t,N}^{X} is the empirical measure (136).
The ratio (137) contains the variance of ξ\xi under the ergodic average of the empirical measure (therefore the total occupation measure in ξ\xi) in the numerator, and the mean variance over one-replica occupation measures (within-trajectory occupation measures) in the denominator.
It is therefore simple to verify that RT,ξX≥1R_{T,\xi}^{X}\geq 1, as the mean-of-variances in the denominator is necessarily smaller than the pooled variance in the numerator, by the law of total variance.
A closely related statistic was proposed by [22] to diagnose the convergence of Markov chain Monte Carlo algorithms in Bayesian inference, using multiple independent copies of the Markov chain instead of the Fleming–Viot process.
To define the Gelman–Rubin decorrelation time, we choose a finite set 𝒪\mathcal{O} of collective variables, and for ξ∈𝒪\xi\in\mathcal{O}, we define the stopping time
tcorr,ξGR​(α)=inf{t≥τ:Rt,ξX−1<α},t^{\rm GR}_{{\rm corr},\xi}(\alpha)=\inf\{t\geq\tau:\,R_{t,\xi}^{X}-1<\alpha\},
(138)
where α,τ>0\alpha,\tau>0 are user-provided parameters.
The Gelman–Rubin decorrelation time is finally defined as the maximum of these stopping times:
tcorrGR​(α)=maxξ∈𝒪​tcorr,ξGR​(α).t^{\rm GR}_{\rm corr}(\alpha)=\underset{\xi\in\mathcal{O}}{\max}\,t^{\rm GR}_{\rm corr,\xi}(\alpha).
(139)
The parameter α\alpha is a tolerance threshold, which [4, 28] suggest should be taken such that α∈[0.01,0.1]\alpha\in[0.01,0.1] for most systems, based on the results of some numerical experiments.
The parameter τ>0\tau>0 is a burn-in time, which is necessary to avoid spurious convergence: when the Fleming–Viot process starts from a deterministic state (η^0,NX=δX0\widehat{\eta}_{0,N}^{X}=\delta_{X_{0}}), this forces Rt,ξX≈1R_{t,\xi}^{X}\approx 1 for small times t>0t>0.
Turning (139) into a principled decorrelation time would rely on the following assumptions (which are adapted from those outlined in [4, Section 3.1]).
(A1) 
For large NN and ξ∈𝒪\xi\in\mathcal{O}, the empirical measure ξ⋆​η^t,NX\xi_{\star}\widehat{\eta}_{t,N}^{X} is, uniformly in time, a good approximation of ξ⋆​ηtX\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X}. This assumption is actually shared with our method (see Assumption (B2) for a more formal discussion).
(A2) 
The trajectories ξ⁡(Xt(n))\xi(X^{(n)}_{t}) for ξ∈𝒪\xi\in\mathcal{O} can be effectively treated as independent Markovian trajectories. This is the property which would justify the use of any Markov chain diagnostic.
(A3) 
There exists a slow ξ∈𝒪\xi\in\mathcal{O}, meaning that convergence of ξ⋆​ηtX\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X} towards its quasi-stationary states implies convergence of ηtX\eta_{t}^{X}. This assumption is also shared by our method (and stated more precisely in Assumption (B1)).
The main assumption we avoid is (A2), which will be replaced by an assumption about prior knowledge of a set of plausible surrogate processes ZZ (this will be made precise in Assumptions (B3), (B4) below).
Even in the ideal situation where Assumptions (A1)–(A3) hold, the stopping rule (139) is known to have the wrong qualitative behavior. Whereas ηTX\eta_{T}^{X} is generally known to converge to νX\nu^{X} asymptotically exponentially fast as T→∞T\to\infty (when a quantitative Yaglom limit such as (4) holds), the variance ratio (137) converges algebraically to 11 as T→∞T\to\infty (with a rate proportional to 1/T1/T).
Therefore, when the tolerance threshold α\alpha is small, we expect the stopping rule (139) to be overly conservative. This is broadly in line with what we observe numerically in Section 4, and the experiments of [4, 28].
3.2 Simulation-based inference of decorrelation times
In Section 2, we identify a regime in which a coarse-grained Markov process, the canonical model ZZ from Definition 3, provides an accurate description of the convergence of XX to its QSD within 𝒳\mathcal{X}.
It is therefore tempting to use ZZ to define a quasi-stationarity diagnostic. This is not a practical idea, as the very definition of ZZ requires knowledge of νX\nu^{X} through the definition of the fiber measures ℱ\mathcal{F}. As the purpose of a quasi-stationary diagnostic is ultimately to sample from νX\nu^{X}, we face an apparently circular problem.
We argue that this circularity can be avoided by combining the approximation results of Section 2 with simulation-based Bayesian inference.
Our proposed method can be described loosely as follows. The parameter of interest we would like to infer is the “honest” decorrelation time t0​(ℒZ,Z0)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) of ZZ (to be defined precisely in Proposition 11 below).
∘\circ 
We place a prior distribution ρsynth\rho_{\rm synth} on the dynamics of the surrogate process ZZ, specified by an infinitesimal generator and an initial point (ℒZ,Z0)(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}), and estimate posterior probabilities ht​(FtZ):=ℙρ​(t>t0​(ℒZ,Z0)|FtZ)h_{t}(F_{t}^{Z}):=\mathbb{P}_{\rho}\left(t>t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0})\,\middle|\,F_{t}^{Z}\right) for t≥0t\geq 0, where FtZF_{t}^{Z} are featurized observations of the paths (η^s,NZ)0≤s≤t(\widehat{\eta}_{s,N}^{Z})_{0\leq s\leq t} of the particle-approximated probability flow of ηtZ\eta_{t}^{Z}.
∘\circ 
Since (ℒZ,Z0)(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) is marginalized over rather than inferred directly, it is a nuisance parameter in the sense of [21, Section 3.1], rather than the parameter of interest, which is limited to t0​(ℒZ,Z0)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}).
∘\circ 
Because the conditional likelihood of FtZF_{t}^{Z} given (ℒZ,Z0)(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) is intractable, we train a machine learning model to approximate the posterior probabilities with a parametric model hθ,th_{\theta,t}, following the standard simulation-based inference methodology [15].
∘\circ 
In deployment, we evaluate the trained model on Ftξ,XF_{t}^{\xi,X}, a featurized observation of (ξ⋆​η^s,NX)0≤s≤t(\xi_{\star}\widehat{\eta}_{s,N}^{X})_{0\leq s\leq t}. That is, we use hθ,t​(Ftξ,X)h_{\theta,t}(F_{t}^{\xi,X}) as if Ftξ,XF_{t}^{\xi,X} had been generated from the synthetic prior. This substitution is motivated by the analysis of Section 2, and the underlying assumptions are made explicit in Section 3.3.
In particular, paths of XX are never simulated during training, and paths of ZZ are never simulated during deployment.
3.2.1 Honest decorrelation times
The parameter of interest is the honest decorrelation time
t0​(ℒX,η0X)=inf{t>0:dTV​(ηtX,νX)<εcorr},t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{X},\eta_{0}^{X})=\inf\{t>0:\,\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}(\eta_{t}^{X},\nu^{X})<\varepsilon_{\rm corr}\},
(140)
where εcorr>0\varepsilon_{\rm corr}>0 is a tolerance threshold, which will be fixed throughout this work. Note this is a function of the dynamical rule and initial distribution, not of the sample paths.
A direct estimator of t0​(ℒX,η0X)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{X},\eta_{0}^{X}) would be very valuable, as the knowledge of this time gives direct error control on sampled exit events starting from t0​(ℒX,η0X)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{X},\eta_{0}^{X}). We have the following result.
Proposition 10.
Writing t0X:=t0​(ℒX,η0X)t_{0}^{X}:=t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{X},\eta_{0}^{X}), we have, for any t≥0t\geq 0 and A⊆∂𝒳A\subseteq\partial\mathcal{X},
|ℙηt0XX​(XτX∈A,τX>t)−ℙνX​(XτX∈A,τX>t)|≤εcorr/2.\left|\mathbb{P}_{\eta_{t_{0}^{X}}^{X}}\left(X_{\tau^{X}}\in A,\tau^{X}>t\right)-\mathbb{P}_{\nu^{X}}\left(X_{\tau^{X}}\in A,\tau^{X}>t\right)\right|\leq\varepsilon_{\rm corr}/2.
(141)
We omit the proof, as it is similar to the proof of Proposition 11 below.
This quantity is however intractable for most systems of interest, as it requires knowledge of ηtX\eta_{t}^{X} and νX\nu^{X}.
We therefore introduce a first layer of approximation, consisting in fixing a collective variable ξ:𝒳→𝒵\xi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Z} and considering instead the coarse-grained decorrelation time t0​(ℒZ,η0Z)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},\eta_{0}^{Z}).
The analysis performed in Section 2 allows us to estimate the bias on the exit distribution (2) resulting from this choice.
Proposition 11.
Under the Assumptions and notations of Theorem 5, writing t0Z=t0​(ℒZ,η0Z)t_{0}^{Z}=t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},\eta_{0}^{Z}), we have, for any t≥0t\geq 0 and A⊆∂𝒳A\subseteq\partial\mathcal{X},
|ℙηt0ZX​(XτX∈A,τX>t)−ℙνX​(XτX∈A,τX>t)|≤εcorr2\displaystyle\left|\mathbb{P}_{\eta_{t_{0}^{Z}}^{X}}\left(X_{\tau^{X}}\in A,\tau^{X}>t\right)-\mathbb{P}_{\nu^{X}}\left(X_{\tau^{X}}\in A,\tau^{X}>t\right)\right|\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{\rm corr}}{2}
(142)
+‖AX‖2​ζ​(C​e−γX​t0Z+C′​t0Z​e−(γZ∧γX)​t0Z)\displaystyle+\frac{\|A^{X}\|}{2\zeta}\left(C\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}t_{0}^{Z}}+C^{\prime}t_{0}^{Z}\mathrm{e}^{-(\gamma^{Z}\land\gamma^{X})t_{0}^{Z}}\right)
+CλX​CγX​Cζ2​η0X​(hX)​(e−(ζ−λX)​t0Z+2​‖AX‖ζ​e−γX​t0Z).\displaystyle+\frac{C_{\lambda}^{X}C_{\gamma}^{X}C_{\zeta}}{2\eta_{0}^{X}(h^{X})}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-(\zeta-\lambda^{X})t_{0}^{Z}}+2\frac{\|A^{X}\|}{\zeta}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}t_{0}^{Z}}\right).
In the above, t0Z=t0​(ℒZ,η0Z)t_{0}^{Z}=t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},\eta_{0}^{Z}) is of course the honest decorrelation time for the canonical model ZZ associated to the choice of XX and ξ\xi, given in Definition 3.
Proof of Proposition 11.
The function f⁡(x)=ℙx​(XτX∈A,τX>t)f(x)=\mathbb{P}_{x}\left(X_{\tau^{X}}\in A,\tau^{X}>t\right) takes values in [0,1][0,1], which allows us to write
|ηt0ZX​(f)−νX​(f)|\displaystyle|\eta^{X}_{t_{0}^{Z}}(f)-\nu^{X}(f)|
=|∫01[ηt0ZX​(f>s)−νX​(f>s)]​ds|\displaystyle=\left|\int_{0}^{1}\left[\eta^{X}_{t_{0}^{Z}}(f>s)-\nu^{X}(f>s)\right]\,\mathrm{d}s\right|
(143)
≤∫01supA⊆𝒳​|ηt0ZX​(A)−νX​(A)|​𝑑s\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{1}\underset{A\subseteq\mathcal{X}}{\sup}\,|\eta^{X}_{t_{0}^{Z}}(A)-\nu^{X}(A)|\,\mathrm{d}s
=12​dTV​(ηt0ZX,νX).\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}(\eta_{t_{0}^{Z}}^{X},\nu^{X}).
From here, the estimate (56) from Corollary 6 gives
dTV​(ηt0ZX,νX)≤dTV​(ηt0ZZ,νZ)+CλX​CγX​Cζη0X​(hX)​(e−(ζ−λX)​t0Z+2​‖AX‖ζ​e−γX​t0Z)\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}(\eta_{t_{0}^{Z}}^{X},\nu^{X})\leq\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}(\eta_{t_{0}^{Z}}^{Z},\nu^{Z})+\frac{C_{\lambda}^{X}C_{\gamma}^{X}C_{\zeta}}{\eta_{0}^{X}(h^{X})}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-(\zeta-\lambda^{X})t_{0}^{Z}}+2\frac{\|A^{X}\|}{\zeta}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}t_{0}^{Z}}\right)
(144)
+‖AX‖ζ​(C​e−γX​t0Z+C′​t0Z​e−(γZ∧γX)​t0Z)\displaystyle+\frac{\|A^{X}\|}{\zeta}\left(C\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}t_{0}^{Z}}+C^{\prime}t_{0}^{Z}\mathrm{e}^{-(\gamma^{Z}\land\gamma^{X})t_{0}^{Z}}\right)
≤εcorr+CλX​CγX​Cζη0X​(hX)​(e−(ζ−λX)​t0Z+2​‖AX‖ζ​e−γX​t0Z)\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon_{\rm corr}+\frac{C_{\lambda}^{X}C_{\gamma}^{X}C_{\zeta}}{\eta_{0}^{X}(h^{X})}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-(\zeta-\lambda^{X})t_{0}^{Z}}+2\frac{\|A^{X}\|}{\zeta}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}t_{0}^{Z}}\right)
+‖AX‖ζ​(C​e−γX​t0Z+C′​t0Z​e−(γZ∧γX)​t0Z),\displaystyle+\frac{\|A^{X}\|}{\zeta}\left(C\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma^{X}t_{0}^{Z}}+C^{\prime}t_{0}^{Z}\mathrm{e}^{-(\gamma^{Z}\land\gamma^{X})t_{0}^{Z}}\right),
by definition of t0Zt_{0}^{Z} for the last inequality.
∎
This surrogate criterion is still impractical, since we do not know how to compute ℒZ\mathcal{L}^{Z} given ℒX\mathcal{L}^{X} and ξ\xi. Besides, we are not overly interested in measuring ℒZ\mathcal{L}^{Z} directly. The key idea in this section is to treat ℒZ\mathcal{L}^{Z} as a nuisance parameter in a probabilistic model.
3.2.2 Synthetic decorrelation ensembles
Our goal is to infer t0​(ℒZ,η0Z)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},\eta_{0}^{Z}), which is equivalent to inferring the values of the variables
Dt(ℒZ,η0Z)={t>t0(ℒZ,η0Z)},t≥0.D_{t}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},\eta_{0}^{Z})=\{t>t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},\eta_{0}^{Z})\},\qquad t\geq 0.
(145)
We define a probabilistic model, specified by the following.
∘\circ 
A prior ρdyn∈𝒫⁡(Gen⁡(𝒵))\rho_{\rm dyn}\in\mathcal{P}({\rm Gen}(\mathcal{Z})) over generators ℒZ\mathcal{L}^{Z} of the coarse-grained dynamics ZZ, where Gen⁡(𝒵){\rm Gen}(\mathcal{Z}) denotes the set of generators of killed, conditionally irreducible continuous-time Markov jump processes on 𝒵\mathcal{Z}.
∘\circ 
A prior ρ0\rho_{0} over an additional nuisance parameter Z0∈𝒵Z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}, whose role will be discussed below.
∘\circ 
A choice of design variables, in this work an integer N≥1N\geq 1 and a lag time τ>0\tau>0.
∘\circ 
An observation process, carrying information about the non-linear flow ηtZ\eta_{t}^{Z}, in the form of a sequence of parametrized random variables
(F⁡(ℒZ,Z0,N,k​τ))1≤k≤ℓ.(F(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0},N,k\tau))_{1\leq k\leq\ell}.
(146)
This observation provides the data in our Bayesian framework. The nature of this data is made precise below.
Given these definitions, our goal is to estimate the posterior probabilities
hk∗​(ℱ,N,τ):=ℙρsynth​(Dk​τ​(ℒZ,η0Z)|(F⁡(ℒZ,Z0,N,j​τ))1≤j≤k=ℱ),h_{k}^{*}(\mathscr{F};N,\tau):=\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{\rm synth}}\left(D_{k\tau}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},\eta_{0}^{Z})\,\middle|\,\left(F(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0},N,j\tau)\right)_{1\leq j\leq k}=\mathscr{F}\right),
(147)
where ρsynth\rho_{\rm synth} is the joint prior over the nuisance parameters (ℒZ,Z0)(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}). Let us be more specific about the role played by these parameters and the observation process FF.
∘\circ 
The parameter ℒZ\mathcal{L}^{Z} is the generator of the canonical model from Definition 3, and the role of the prior ρsynth\rho_{\rm synth} is to model our uncertainty about which ZZ adequately approximates the decorrelation behavior of ξ⁡(X)\xi(X). The synthetic ensemble ρdyn\rho_{\rm dyn} should cover the set of plausible surrogate processes, and this is the main modeling input in our framework. It should ideally be calibrated with domain-specific knowledge to avoid overly broad priors.
∘\circ 
The additional nuisance parameter is limited in this work to Z0∈𝒵Z_{0}\in\mathcal{Z}, corresponding to the initial state of the surrogate process. The prior ρ0\rho_{0} is therefore an element of 𝒫⁡(𝒵)\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z}), and in this work we take a uniform prior ρ0=𝒰⁡(𝒵)\rho_{0}=\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{Z}), with ρsynth=ρdyn​ρ0\rho_{\rm synth}=\rho_{\rm dyn}\rho_{0}, i.e. the dynamics and initial point are sampled independently. One could instead treat the initial distribution η0Z∈𝒫⁡(𝒵)\eta_{0}^{Z}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z}) as a nuisance parameter, in which case ρ0∈𝒫⁡(𝒫⁡(𝒵))\rho_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})), with the goal of making the model’s predictions robust to changes of the initial distribution. Here we restrict ourselves to the special case η0Z=δZ0\eta_{0}^{Z}=\delta_{Z_{0}} for simplicity.
∘\circ 
The variable FF is a sequence of indirect and noisy observations related to the non-linear flow ηtZ\eta_{t}^{Z}. The design parameter NN counts the number of replicas in a Fleming–Viot process (see Definition 9) on 𝒵\mathcal{Z}, killed at the boundary ∂𝒵\partial^{\mathcal{Z}}, with one-particle generator ℒZ\mathcal{L}^{Z}. The Fleming–Viot process is initialized at time t=0t=0 from the state 𝐙0=(Z0,…,Z0){\mathbf{Z}}_{0}=(Z_{0},\dots,Z_{0}). The sample path of this particle process is then featurized into a sequence of probability distributions
F⁡(ℒZ,Z0,N,k​τ)=Hist⁡(1τ​∫(k−1)​τk​τη^t,NZ​𝑑t),F(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0},N,k\tau)={\rm Hist}\left(\frac{1}{\tau}\int_{(k-1)\tau}^{k\tau}\widehat{\eta}_{t,N}^{Z}\,\mathrm{d}t\right),
(148)
where Hist:𝒫⁡(𝒵)→𝒫⁡(𝒵){\rm Hist}:\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})\to\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z}) is a histogram map associated to a fixed partition of 𝒵\mathcal{Z}. While other featurizations are possible, particle permutation-invariant features preserve the exchangeability symmetry of Fleming–Viot trajectories, which is likely important for parameter efficiency. The total observed data is therefore the sequence
(F⁡(ℒZ,Z0,N,k​τ))1≤k≤ℓ∈⋃ℓ≥1𝒫​(𝒵)ℓ.\left(F(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0},N,k\tau)\right)_{1\leq k\leq\ell}\in\bigcup_{\ell\geq 1}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z})^{\ell}.
(149)
Note that each observation sequence is finite, corresponding to the fact that Fleming–Viot simulations can only be performed over finite time horizons. The length ℓ\ell of each observation is determined by the learning objective, which will be discussed later.
In practice, trajectories and time integrals are discretized consistently with a numerical scheme, and τ=Nlag​Δ​t\tau=N_{\rm lag}\Delta t is an integer multiple of the timestep Δ​t\Delta t, so that each histogram in (148) aggregates N​NlagNN_{\rm lag} sample points in total.
Let us make some remarks on this model.
∘\circ 
An alternative (which we leave to future work) to the hand-calibration of ρsynth\rho_{\rm synth} is to learn a generative model ρsynth\rho_{\rm synth} for (ℒZ,Z0)(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) from real simulation data. More precisely, one would tune ρsynth\rho_{\rm synth} to make the paths of generated features FF indistinguishable from those obtained from high-dimensional Fleming–Viot processes (i.e. derived from low-dimensional traces (ξ⋆​η^t,NX)t≥0(\xi_{\star}\widehat{\eta}_{t,N}^{X})_{t\geq 0}), for practical choices of the collective variable ξ\xi. Classification-based approaches for calibrating parameters in synthetic-to-real distribution-matching problems are available in the literature, see [25] and references therein.
Doing so with gradient-based methods requires computing derivatives of Fleming–Viot trajectories with respect to ℒZ\mathcal{L}^{Z}.
∘\circ 
The total observed sequence FF is a noisy and biased observation of the non-linear flow ηtZ\eta_{t}^{Z}, polluted by several sources of error, first and foremost stochastic error terms and biases related to the finite particle number NN in the Fleming–Viot process. Training the inference model using imperfect features is consistent with the operational setting of molecular simulation, where these error sources are also present.
∘\circ 
The likelihood of FF under ρsynth\rho_{\rm synth} is analytically intractable, and therefore we rely on simulation-based (also known as likelihood-free) estimators of the posterior probabilities (147) as will be explained in Section 3.2.4, which require access to the ground-truth labels Dk​τ​(ℒZ,η0Z)D_{k\tau}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},\eta_{0}^{Z}). This implies that the distribution of synthetic dynamics ρdyn\rho_{\rm dyn} should be supported on generators ℒZ\mathcal{L}^{Z} for which computing these labels is tractable, at least as an offline data-generation cost.
∘\circ 
Instead of fixing the tolerance parameter εcorr>0\varepsilon_{\rm corr}>0, it could be treated as a design parameter in future work, which could be useful to tune the error level with no need for retraining.
3.2.3 Recurrent model
To approximate the posterior probabilities (147), we propose a simple class of parametric models acting on the space of sequences (148), which will be used to detect quasi-stationarity on-the-fly, as the Fleming–Viot simulation proceeds.
The simulation produces a sequence of histograms in ξ⁡(𝒳)=𝒵\xi(\mathcal{X})=\mathcal{Z} (the collective variable space), defined in (148). Denoting the observation (149)
F=(Hk)1≤k≤ℓ,Hk=F⁡(ℒZ,Z0,N,k​τ),F=(H_{k})_{1\leq k\leq\ell},\qquad H_{k}=F(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0},N,k\tau),
(150)
this sequence has important properties informing our choice of architecture.
First, it is not known in full at deployment time, but streamed as the simulation proceeds and samples are aggregated. For this reason, the final length ℓ\ell is not known a priori, and moreover may vary over several orders of magnitude throughout the synthetic ensemble (in accordance with the fact that real systems do decorrelate on a range of different timescales).
A naive approach consists in padding the sequence (say with zeros) into a fixed-size ℓmax\ell_{\max}-length buffer, and feeding a flattened ℓmax​dhist\ell_{\max}d_{\rm hist}-dimensional feature to a parametric model operating on fixed-size inputs (where dhistd_{\rm hist} gives the dimension of a single histogram), for instance
F⏟ℓ×dhist→Fpadded⏟ℓmax×dhist→hθ​(Fpadded,N,τ)⏟∈[0,1]ℓmax,\underbrace{F}_{\ell\times d_{\rm hist}}\to\underbrace{F_{\rm padded}}_{\ell_{\max}\times d_{\rm hist}}\to\underbrace{h_{\theta}(F_{\rm padded};N,\tau)}_{\in[0,1]^{\ell_{\rm max}}},
(151)
where hθh_{\theta} is a dense neural network parametrized by some θ∈ℝp\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}. The first ℓ\ell elements of the output can be taken as predictors of the posterior probabilities (147). This approach has several drawbacks: since inference cost for dense neural networks is quadratic in the dimensionality of input features, and inference has to be run every timestep (or every few timesteps), this strategy leads in total to a 𝒪⁡(ℓmax​dhist)\mathcal{O}(\ell_{\rm max}d_{\rm hist}) memory cost and 𝒪⁡(ℓ​ℓmax2​dhist2)\mathcal{O}(\ell\ell_{\rm max}^{2}d_{\rm hist}^{2}) total inference cost for a length-ℓ\ell sequence.
This may become very costly for long Fleming–Viot traces, and besides requires fixing ℓmax\ell_{\max} a priori (and pessimistically), which leads to wasted computation on short traces.
Moreover, we would like our model for h∗h^{*} to be causal, meaning that future observations may not influence past predictions. Indeed, it is clear from the definition of FF and the posterior probabilities (147) that
∀1≤k≤ℓ,hk∗​(F)=gk​(H1,…,Hk)\forall 1\leq k\leq\ell,\qquad h^{*}_{k}(F)=g_{k}(H_{1},\dots,H_{k})
(152)
for some measurable functions gk:ℝdhist×k→[0,1]g_{k}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm hist}\times k}\to[0,1]. This property has to be enforced manually in training this type of model. While more advanced architectures (such as autoregressive Transformers) naturally address some of these issues, these are still limited by memory constraints to relatively short sequences.
Such models are besides poorly suited to the operational constraints of quasi-stationarity diagnostics, which should remain lightweight and deployable on standard CPU computing clusters, with negligible memory cost.
We propose using recurrent neural networks (RNNs), a classical approach in dealing with sequence-to-sequence learning tasks, and which address these shortcomings by construction. These allow for 𝒪⁡(dhist)\mathcal{O}(d_{\rm hist}) memory cost and 𝒪⁡(ℓ​dhist2)\mathcal{O}(\ell d_{\rm hist}^{2}) total inference cost. Since the inference cost per simulation step is constant, they can feasibly be used on arbitrarily long sequences, and besides satisfy the causality property (152) automatically. Recurrent neural networks process the sequence element-by-element, building a sequence of latent states (Lk​(θ,N,τ))0≤k≤ℓ(L_{k}(\theta;N,\tau))_{0\leq k\leq\ell} recursively from (Hk)1≤k≤ℓ(H_{k})_{1\leq k\leq\ell} as follows:
∀ 1≤k≤ℓ,Lk​(θ,N,τ)=rθ​(Hk,Lk−1,N,τ),\forall\,1\leq k\leq\ell,\qquad L_{k}(\theta;N,\tau)=r_{\theta}(H_{k},L_{k-1};N,\tau),
(153)
where the recurrent layer is a parametrized map rθ:ℝdin×ℝdrnn→ℝdrnnr_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm in}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm rnn}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm rnn}}, and L0∈ℝdrnnL_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm rnn}} is an arbitrary latent initial state, which we take to be L0=0L_{0}=0 (L0L_{0} could also be treated as a trainable function of Z0Z_{0}). Our recurrent model for h∗h^{*} is finally defined as
∀ 1≤k≤ℓ,hk,θ​(F)=cθ​(Lk​(θ,N,τ)),\forall\,1\leq k\leq\ell,\qquad h_{k,\theta}(F)=c_{\theta}(L_{k}(\theta;N,\tau)),
(154)
where cθ:ℝdrnn→[0,1]c_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm rnn}}\to[0,1] is a classifier acting on RNN latent states. In this work, cθc_{\theta} is a shallow, dense neural network.
We define the recurrent component of the network by
rθ​(H,L,N,τ)=LSTMθ​(fθ​(H)⋆m⁡(N,τ),L),r_{\theta}(H,L;N,\tau)={\rm LSTM}_{\theta}(f_{\theta}(H)\star m(N,\tau),L),
(155)
where fθ:ℝdhist→ℝdcnnf_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm hist}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm cnn}} is a (N,τ)(N,\tau)-independent featurizer, m⁡(N,τ):ℝ2→ℝdmetam(N,\tau):\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm meta}} is a hand-defined feature encoding design information, LSTMθ:ℝdcnn+dmeta×ℝdrnn→ℝdrnn{\rm LSTM}_{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm cnn}+d_{\rm meta}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm rnn}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm rnn}} is a stack of one or several long short-term memory (LSTM) layers, and ⋆:ℝdcnn×ℝdmeta→ℝdcnn+dmeta\star:\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm cnn}}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm meta}}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm cnn}+d_{\rm meta}} is the concatenation operator.
LSTMs are a classical class of recurrent models introduced for processing sequential data, and are an available option in most neural network packages today. Although modern alternatives to LSTMs exist, we found these to be sufficient for the purposes of this work.
We choose fθf_{\theta} to be a convolutional neural network (CNN) operating on histograms, which is a parameter-efficient architecture suitable for feature-extraction in spatially structured low-dimensional signals.
The fact that our histogram features are spatially structured is not yet obvious: this will become apparent when we consider choices of ξ\xi related to molecular collective variables (see Section 4 below).
Finally, the recurrent layer is informed of the design variables only through the scalar feature m⁡(N,τ)=N​τm(N,\tau)=\sqrt{N\tau}, so that dmeta=1d_{\rm meta}=1. This choice is physically motivated by the fact that N​τ\sqrt{N\tau} is a natural precision scaling for the histogram features (148). We therefore expect that supplying this quantity to the recurrent model will make it more effective at filtering the finite-sample statistical noise out of its latent state trajectories.
One could also supply m⁡(N,τ)=(N,τ)m(N,\tau)=(N,\tau) directly, but we deliberately chose not to do this in order to avoid overfitting to the elapsed time of the simulation. The influence of this choice has not been tested systematically.
One could also ask whether it is possible to directly approximate the posterior probability ℙρsynth​(Dk​τ​(ℒZ,η0Z)|Hk)\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{\rm synth}}\left(D_{k\tau}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},\eta_{0}^{Z})\,\middle|\,H_{k}\right) rather than (147). This target, however, discards our knowledge of (H1,…,Hk−1)(H_{1},\dots,H_{k-1}) when we have observed HkH_{k}. In Section 4.3, we test a diagnostic based on the joint observation of (Hk,νZ)(H_{k},\nu^{Z}), and find that it performs poorly when compared to the recurrent model. Although HkH_{k} does depend implicitly on (H1,…,Hk−1)(H_{1},\dots,H_{k-1}), it may be too noisy in practice to be a useful predictor.
3.2.4 Training objective
For any set EE, we denote 𝒮⁡(E)=Eℕ\mathcal{S}(E)=E^{\mathbb{N}} the set of EE-valued sequences. The posterior probabilities (147) we wish to estimate define a causal, measurable function h∗​(⋅,N,τ):𝒮⁡(ℝdhist)→𝒮⁡([0,1])h^{*}(\cdot\,;N,\tau):\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm hist}})\to\mathcal{S}([0,1]) mapping sequences to sequences.
For any summable weighting function w∈ℓ1​(ℕ,ℝ+)w\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R}_{+}), we introduce a weighted cross-entropy functional
ℒw​[h;N,τ]=𝔼ρsynth​[𝔼𝐙​[∑k≥1w⁡(k)​ℍ​{Dk​τ​(ℒZ,Z0),hk​(F)}]],\mathscr{L}_{w}[h;N,\tau]=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\rm synth}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{{\bf Z}}\left[\sum_{k\geq 1}w(k)\mathbb{H}\left\{D_{k\tau}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}),h_{k}(F)\right\}\right]\right],
(156)
where the outer expectation is with respect to the prior ρsynth\rho_{\rm synth} over (ℒZ,Z0)(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}), while the inner expectation is over the distribution of features FF induced by the Fleming–Viot process 𝐙\mathbf{Z}.
We recall that the integer ℓ\ell is the length of the feature sequence FF, and that the variables Dk​τD_{k\tau} defined in (145) are {0,1}\{0,1\}-valued. We write
ℍ⁡{p,q}=−p​log⁡q−(1−p)​log⁡(1−q)\mathbb{H}\left\{p,q\right\}=-p\log\,q-(1-p)\,\log(1-q)
(157)
for the cross-entropy of a Bernoulli distribution with respect to another, with parameters pp and qq.
This class of objectives is naturally motivated by the following lemma.
Lemma 12.
For any choice of weight w∈ℓ1​(ℕ,ℝ+)w\in\ell^{1}(\mathbb{N},\mathbb{R}_{+}), design parameters N≥1N\geq 1, τ>0\tau>0, and any causal h:𝒮⁡(ℝdhist)→𝒮⁡([0,1])h:\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm hist}})\to\mathcal{S}([0,1]) (that is, verifying (152)), it holds that
ℒw​[h;N,τ]≥ℒw​[h∗;N,τ]\mathscr{L}_{w}[h;N,\tau]\geq\mathscr{L}_{w}[h^{*};N,\tau]
(158)
with equality if and only if hk=hk∗h_{k}=h_{k}^{*} almost surely for every kk such that w⁡(k)>0w(k)>0.
Proof.
Denote 𝔼=𝔼ρsynth​𝔼𝐙\mathbb{E}=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\rm synth}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{Z}}, and ℱk=σ(Hj, 1≤j≤k)\mathcal{F}_{k}=\sigma(H_{j},\,1\leq j\leq k) be the natural filtration for the observed data. Let h:𝒮⁡(ℝdhist)→𝒮⁡([0,1])h:\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm hist}})\to\mathcal{S}([0,1]) be a causal measurable function. Then,
ℒw​[h;N,τ]\displaystyle\mathscr{L}_{w}[h;N,\tau]
=𝔼⁡[∑k≥1w⁡(k)​ℍ​{Dk​τ​(ℒZ,Z0),hk​(F)}]\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{k\geq 1}w(k)\mathbb{H}\left\{D_{k\tau}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}),h_{k}(F)\right\}\right]
(159)
=∑k≥1w⁡(k)​𝔼​[𝔼⁡[ℍ⁡{Dk​τ​(ℒZ,Z0),hk​(F)}|ℱk]]\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}w(k)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{H}\left\{D_{k\tau}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}),h_{k}(F)\right\}\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right]\right]
=∑k≥1w⁡(k)​𝔼​[ℍ⁡{𝔼⁡[Dk​τ​(ℒZ,Z0)|ℱk],hk​(F)}]\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}w(k)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{H}\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[D_{k\tau}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0})\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right],h_{k}(F)\right\}\right]
=∑k≥1w⁡(k)​𝔼​[ℍ⁡{hk∗​(F),hk​(F)}]\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}w(k)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{H}\left\{h_{k}^{*}(F),h_{k}(F)\right\}\right]
≥∑k≥1w⁡(k)​𝔼​[ℍ⁡{hk∗​(F),hk∗​(F)}]\displaystyle\geq\sum_{k\geq 1}w(k)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{H}\left\{h_{k}^{*}(F),h_{k}^{*}(F)\right\}\right]
=ℒw​[h∗;N,τ],\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}_{w}[h^{*};N,\tau],
where we used the causality property (152) and the fact that ℍ\mathbb{H} is affine in its first argument for the third equality, the definition of h∗h^{*} for the fourth equality, and Gibbs’ cross-entropy inequality in the fifth line. This inequality saturates if and only if hk=hk∗h_{k}=h_{k}^{*} whenever w⁡(k)>0w(k)>0, which is our claimed equality condition.
∎
When ww has infinite support, evaluating this functional requires simulating infinitely long Fleming–Viot trajectories. A simple idea to avoid this consists in truncating the sum using a deterministic weight w⁡(k)=𝟙k≤ℓmaxw(k)=\mathbbm{1}_{k\leq\ell_{\max}} for some large ℓmax≫1\ell_{\max}\gg 1.
This requires setting a time-horizon a priori for the diagnostic, which we wished to avoid by using a recurrent architecture.
We propose another approach using a randomized time-horizon. Given (ℒZ,Z0)∼ρsynth(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0})\sim\rho_{\rm synth}, we sample a random variable L∼πL(⋅|t0(ℒZ,Z0),N,τ)∈𝒫(ℕ)L\sim\pi_{L}\left(\cdot\,\middle|t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}),N,\tau\right)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{N}), and consider the objective
ℒL​[h;N,τ]=𝔼ρsynth​[𝔼L​[𝔼𝐙​[∑1≤k≤Lℍ⁡{Dk​τ​(ℒZ,Z0),hk​(F)}]]].\mathscr{L}_{L}[h;N,\tau]=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\rm synth}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{L}\left[\mathbb{E}_{{\bf Z}}\left[\sum_{1\leq k\leq L}\mathbb{H}\left\{D_{k\tau}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}),h_{k}(F)\right\}\right]\right]\right].
(160)
Then we have the following result.
Lemma 13.
For N≥1N\geq 1, τ>0\tau>0, and any causal h:𝒮⁡(ℝdhist)→𝒮⁡([0,1])h:\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm hist}})\to\mathcal{S}([0,1]), it holds that
ℒL​[h;N,τ]≥ℒL​[hL∗;N,τ],\mathscr{L}_{L}[h;N,\tau]\geq\mathscr{L}_{L}[h^{*}_{L};N,\tau],
(161)
where
hL,k∗(F)=ℙρsynth,L(τk>t0(ℒZ,Z0)|H1,…,Hk,k≤L).h^{*}_{L,k}(F)=\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{\rm synth},L}\left(\tau k>t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0})\,\middle|\,H_{1},\dots,H_{k},k\leq L\right).
(162)
Moreover, if LL is chosen such that τ​L>t0​(ℒZ,Z0)\tau L>t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) almost surely, then
hL,k∗​(F)≤hk∗​(F)ℙρsynth,L,𝐙​-almost surely.h_{L,k}^{*}(F)\leq h_{k}^{*}(F)\qquad\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{\rm synth},L,\mathbf{Z}}\text{-almost surely}.
(163)
Proof.
The inequality (161) is proven in the same way as Lemma 12, by writing ∑1≤k≤L=∑k≥1𝟙k≤L\sum_{1\leq k\leq L}=\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathbbm{1}_{k\leq L} and conditioning on σ⁡(H1,…,Hk,𝟙k≤L)\sigma(H_{1},\dots,H_{k},\mathbbm{1}_{k\leq L}) rather than ℱk\mathcal{F}_{k}.
For the inequality (163), write, with ρ=ρsynth\rho=\rho_{\rm synth},
1−hL,k∗​(F)\displaystyle 1-h_{L,k}^{*}(F)
=ℙρ,L(τk≤t0(ℒZ,Z0)|H1,…,Hk,k≤L)\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\rho,L}\left(\tau k\leq t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0})\,\middle|\,H_{1},\dots,H_{k},k\leq L\right)
(164)
=ℙρ,L(τk≤t0(ℒZ,Z0),τk≤τL|ℱk)ℙρ,L​(k≤L|ℱk)\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\rho,L}\left(\tau k\leq t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}),\tau k\leq\tau L\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right)}{\mathbb{P}_{\rho,L}\left(k\leq L\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right)}
=ℙρ​(τ​k≤t0​(ℒZ,Z0)|ℱk)ℙρ,L​(k≤L|ℱk)\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{P}_{\rho}\left(\tau k\leq t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0})\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right)}{\mathbb{P}_{\rho,L}\left(k\leq L\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right)}
≥ℙρ​(τ​k≤t0​(ℒZ,Z0)|ℱk)\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}_{\rho}\left(\tau k\leq t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0})\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right)
=1−hk∗​(F),\displaystyle=1-h_{k}^{*}(F),
using τ​L>t0​(ℒZ,Z0)\tau L>t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) in the third line, and 0≤ℙρsynth,L​(k≤L|ℱk)≤10\leq\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{\rm synth},L}\left(k\leq L\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right)\leq 1 in the fourth line.
∎
The inequality (163) means that the minimizer hL∗h_{L}^{*} is conservative, as it underestimates the posterior probabilities (147) of successful decorrelation.
In practice, we find that enforcing the condition τ​L>t0​(ℒZ,Z0)\tau L>t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) via the rule
L=⌈R​t0​(ℒZ,Z0)/τ⌉,R∼𝒰⁡(1,2)​ independentlyL=\lceil Rt_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0})/\tau\rceil,\qquad R\sim\mathcal{U}(1,2)\text{ independently}
(165)
is reasonable, as it results in a fairly balanced set of positive and negative observations, and naturally scales the simulation cost with the physical decorrelation time, thereby avoiding the cost of running long stationary simulations.
It is also simple to show that the ideal predictor hL∗h^{*}_{L} keeps the prior-averaged risk of false decorrelation below α\alpha, conditionally on a finite detection time.
Lemma 14.
Let hL∗h^{*}_{L} be defined as in (162) (with the randomized horizon LL satisfying the condition for (163)).
Define the decorrelation frame
K⁡(α)=inf{k≥1:  1−hL,k∗​(F)<α}K(\alpha)=\inf\left\{k\geq 1:\,\,1-h^{*}_{L,k}(F)<\alpha\right\}
(166)
and the false decorrelation event
A(α)={DK⁡(α)​τ(ℒZ,Z0)=0},A(\alpha)=\left\{D_{K(\alpha)\tau}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0})=0\right\},
(167)
recalling the definition (145). Then
ℙρsynth​(A⁡(α)|K⁡(α)<∞)≤α.\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{\rm synth}}\left(A(\alpha)\,\middle|\,K(\alpha)<\infty\right)\leq\alpha.
(168)
Proof.
We use the notation introduced in the proofs of Lemmas 12 and 13, and denote ρ=ρsynth\rho=\rho_{\rm synth}. Note that K⁡(α)K(\alpha) is an ℱk\mathcal{F}_{k}-stopping time, and write
ℙρ​(A⁡(α),K⁡(α)<∞)\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\rho}\left(A(\alpha),K(\alpha)<\infty\right)
=∑k≥1𝔼ρ​[𝟙K⁡(α)=k​𝟙A⁡(α)]\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{K(\alpha)=k}\mathbbm{1}_{A(\alpha)}\right]
(169)
=∑k≥1𝔼ρ​[𝟙K⁡(α)=k​𝔼ρ​[𝟙DK⁡(α)​τ​(ℒZ,Z0)=0|ℱk]]\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{K(\alpha)=k}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{D_{K(\alpha)\tau}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0})=0}\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right]\right]
=∑k≥1𝔼ρ​[𝟙K⁡(α)=k​ℙρ​(Dk​τ​(ℒZ,Z0)=0|ℱk)]\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{K(\alpha)=k}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}\left(D_{k\tau}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0})=0\,\middle|\,\mathcal{F}_{k}\right)\right]
=∑k≥1𝔼ρ​[𝟙K⁡(α)=k​(1−hk∗​(F))]\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{K(\alpha)=k}\left(1-h_{k}^{*}(F)\right)\right]
≤∑k≥1𝔼ρ​[𝟙K⁡(α)=k​(1−hL,k∗​(F))]\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathbb{E}_{\rho}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{K(\alpha)=k}\left(1-h_{L,k}^{*}(F)\right)\right]
≤α​∑k≥1ℙρ​(K⁡(α)=k)\displaystyle\leq\alpha\sum_{k\geq 1}\mathbb{P}_{\rho}\left(K(\alpha)=k\right)
≤α​ℙρ​(K⁡(α)<∞),\displaystyle\leq\alpha\mathbb{P}_{\rho}\left(K(\alpha)<\infty\right),
and dividing gives the result. We use the stopping-time property in the second line, the definition of h∗h^{*} in the fourth line, the inequality (163) in the fifth line, and the definition of K⁡(α)K(\alpha) in the sixth line.
∎
To make the predictions robust to the choice of lag time and number of replicas, we finally introduce an additional distribution over the design variables (N,τ)(N,\tau), and following the standard machine learning approach, we replace the function hh by the parametric ansatz of the previous subsection, which leads to the problem
minθ∈ℝp​𝔼N,τ​[ℒL​[hθ;N,τ]].\underset{\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}{\min}\,\mathbb{E}_{N,\tau}\left[\mathscr{L}_{L}[h_{\theta};N,\tau]\right].
(170)
Given an i.i.d. training batch B=(t0​(ℒiZ,Z0,i),Fi,Li,Ni,τi)1≤i≤bB=\left(t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z}_{i},Z_{0,i}),F_{i},L_{i},N_{i},\tau_{i}\right)_{1\leq i\leq b}, we approximate a descent direction for the problem (170) with
g^B​(θ)=1CB​∑1≤i≤b∑1≤k≤Li∇θℍ​{Dk​τi​(ℒiZ,Z0,i),hθ,k​(Fi)},\widehat{g}_{B}(\theta)=\frac{1}{C_{B}}\sum_{1\leq i\leq b}\sum_{1\leq k\leq L_{i}}\nabla_{\theta}\mathbb{H}\left\{D_{k\tau_{i}}(\mathcal{L}^{Z}_{i},Z_{0,i}),h_{\theta,k}(F_{i})\right\},
(171)
where CB=∑1≤i≤bLiC_{B}=\sum_{1\leq i\leq b}L_{i} is a consistent estimator of
1𝔼ρsynth​[L]​∇θ𝔼N,τ​[ℒL​[hθ;N,τ]]\frac{1}{\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{\rm synth}}\left[L\right]}\nabla_{\theta}\mathbb{E}_{N,\tau}\left[\mathscr{L}_{L}[h_{\theta};N,\tau]\right]
(172)
in the large batch limit b→∞b\to\infty. The estimator g^B​(θ)\widehat{g}_{B}(\theta) is then used in a stochastic gradient method (the Adam optimizer [35] in this work) to iteratively update the parameters.
3.2.5 On-the-fly diagnostic
Once the parameter θ∈ℝp\theta\in\mathbb{R}^{p} is chosen by the optimization procedure, the quasi-stationarity diagnostic is deployed as follows. The parameters N,τN,\tau are set by the practitioner, and therefore known.
We construct, as the simulation of Fleming–Viot process 𝐗\bf X proceeds, and at each multiple k​τk\tau (k≥1k\geq 1) of the lag time, the histogram
Hkξ=Hist⁡(1τ​∫(k−1)​τk​τξ⋆​η^t,NX​𝑑t)∈ℝdhist,H_{k}^{\xi}=\mathrm{Hist}\left(\frac{1}{\tau}\int_{(k-1)\tau}^{k\tau}\xi_{\star}\widehat{\eta}_{t,N}^{X}\,\mathrm{d}t\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\rm hist}},
(173)
where we recall η^t,NX\widehat{\eta}_{t,N}^{X} is the empirical measure of 𝐗\bf X.
This only requires accumulating bin counts for values of ξ\xi over the NN replicas of 𝐗\bf X and the time interval [(k−1)​τ,k​τ][(k-1)\tau,k\tau].
We then use this histogram to update the model’s latent state and predict, according to Equations (153) and (154),
hk,θξ=cθ​(Lk​(θ,N,τ)),Lk​(θ,N,τ)=rθ​(Hkξ,Lk−1,N,τ).h_{k,\theta}^{\xi}=c_{\theta}(L_{k}(\theta;N,\tau)),\qquad L_{k}(\theta;N,\tau)=r_{\theta}(H_{k}^{\xi},L_{k-1};N,\tau).
(174)
The RNN decorrelation time is finally defined as
tcorrRNN​(α)=τ×inf{k≥1:hk,θξ>1−α},t_{\rm corr}^{\rm RNN}(\alpha)=\tau\times\inf\,\left\{k\geq 1:\,h_{k,\theta}^{\xi}>1-\alpha\right\},
(175)
where α∈(0,1)\alpha\in(0,1) is a user-defined threshold. As in the Gelman–Rubin approach, one could conceivably apply the RNN to multiple collective variables ξ∈𝒪\xi\in\mathcal{O} (see Equation (139)).
This is however only heuristic if the boundary compatibility condition ξ(∂𝒳)=∂𝒵\xi(\partial^{\mathcal{X}})=\partial^{\mathcal{Z}} is not enforced simultaneously for all ξ∈𝒪\xi\in\mathcal{O} (we perform such an experiment involving multiple collective variables in Section 4.4 below).
We conclude this section by clearly stating the set of assumptions justifying the deployment of the synthetically-trained model on the non-linear, non-Markovian particle approximation ξ⋆​η^t,NX\xi_{\star}\widehat{\eta}_{t,N}^{X}.
3.3 Discussion of underlying assumptions
The transferability of the RNN diagnostic rests on several approximations, which we make explicit, as some are only empirically supported at this stage.
(B1) 
The collective variable ξ\xi is slow, i.e. the assumptions of Theorem 5 are satisfied with ‖AX‖/ζ\|A^{X}\|/\zeta small.
(B2) 
The histogram trajectories of the Fleming–Viot approximations (Hist⁡(ξ⋆​η^t,NX))t≥0\left(\rm{Hist}\left(\xi_{\star}\widehat{\eta}_{t,N}^{X}\right)\right)_{t\geq 0} converge to those of (ξ⋆​ηtX)t≥0(\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X})_{t\geq 0} as N→∞N\to\infty, uniformly in time. The same property holds for the ZZ-counterparts of these objects.
(B3) 
The decorrelation time t0​(ℒZ,Z0)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) is, on average over the prior ρsynth\rho_{\rm synth}, accurately predicted by the histogram trajectory (Hist⁡(ηtZ))t≥0\left(\rm{Hist}(\eta_{t}^{Z})\right)_{t\geq 0}.
(B4) 
The synthetic prior places sufficient mass near the canonical model (ℒZ,Z0)(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) of the system at deployment time.
Assumption (B1) justifies the approximation ξ⋆​ηtX≈ηtZ\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X}\approx\eta_{t}^{Z}, uniformly in t≥0t\geq 0, in the sense described by the first item in Theorem 5.
In a second step, (B2) then justifies the approximation Hist⁡(ξ⋆​η^t,NX)≈Hist⁡(ξ⋆​ηtX)≈Hist⁡(ηtZ){{\rm Hist}\left(\xi_{\star}\widehat{\eta}_{t,N}^{X}\right)\approx{\rm Hist}\left(\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X}\right)\approx{\rm Hist}\left(\eta_{t}^{Z}\right)}, again uniformly in time.
Assumptions (B3) and (B4) then imply that trajectories of Hist⁡(ξ⋆​η^t,NX){\rm Hist}\left(\xi_{\star}\widehat{\eta}_{t,N}^{X}\right) are highly informative about t0​(ℒZ,Z0)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}), and therefore about the sampling bias on the exit event, according to Proposition 11 and (B1).
The validity of the approximation warranted by Assumption (B1) is settled for the case of finite-state jump processes XX by the results of Section 2. The extension to the case of diffusion processes XX killed at the boundary of a domain (which is the relevant setting for MD) is still open, and will be studied in an upcoming work. The case of jump processes however already covers relevant physical models from atomistic simulation, such as kinetic Monte Carlo models.
Similarly, the justification of (B2) requires uniform-in-time propagation of chaos results for Fleming–Viot processes. While such results are known to hold when the underlying generator is related to a jump process (see for example [13]), the strongest results available for diffusion processes are for now restricted to particular regimes [33, 32, 11], and current mathematical understanding falls short of treating the case of general diffusions killed at a boundary.
We expect similar results to hold in very general situations.
The training procedure described in Section 3.2 is designed, to some degree, to address the finite-NN sampling approximation. Indeed, the recurrent model is trained to predict properties of ηtZ\eta_{t}^{Z} using observations of η^t,NZ\widehat{\eta}_{t,N}^{Z}, and can therefore be understood as a particle filter with a latent model of this error, fitted during training by observing a range of NN and τ\tau.
We observe empirically that the optimized RNN is quite robust to the choice of (N,τ)(N,\tau), and that the performance of the model improves with NN (see Figure 7).
This does not immediately dissolve the need for Assumption (B2), as it is unclear whether this finite NN-error model, fitted on η^t,NZ→ηtZ\widehat{\eta}_{t,N}^{Z}\to\eta_{t}^{Z} discrepancies, is well calibrated to the ξ⋆​η^t,NX→ξ⋆​ηtX\xi_{\star}\widehat{\eta}_{t,N}^{X}\to\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X} discrepancy faced at deployment. One way to address this is to prove coarse-graining results at the level of the Fleming–Viot process 𝐗\bf X itself, rather than in the mean-field description of Section 2.
Another way is to assume (B2) and take NN sufficiently large so that the mean-field description is accurate.
In Section 5, we discuss some strategies for relaxing some of these assumptions in future studies (in particular those pertaining to the choice of collective variable ξ\xi (B1) and prior ρsynth\rho_{\rm synth} in (B4)).
Regardless of whether (B1)–(B4) hold, the stopping times (139) and (175) are well-defined diagnostics: each one aggregates the history of the Fleming–Viot process into a decision to stop the simulation, and its usefulness can be assessed empirically.
For instance: what is the bias in sampled exit events, starting from a Fleming–Viot particle stopped at the diagnostic time, relative to a true sample from the QSD? How much does the diagnostic overestimate the honest decorrelation time, compared to some idealized “ground truth” estimate? How sensitive is the diagnostic to the choice of parameters (N,τ,α)(N,\tau,\alpha), and can these be chosen a priori over a wide physical range, without requiring per-system calibration?
We will explore these questions numerically in Section 4 below.
When Assumptions (B1)–(B4) do hold, the threshold α\alpha becomes interpretable in the RNN decision rule. As hk,θξh_{k,\theta}^{\xi} approximates a lower bound on the posterior probability (147) according to Lemma 13, the diagnostic rule (175) sets a prior-averaged risk of false convergence less than α\alpha (see Lemma 14 above). This in turn gives (through Proposition 11) control on the average bias in sampled exit events under this rule. In this ideal situation, α\alpha can therefore be selected a priori from a target error level rather than tuned empirically. This is in contrast to the threshold α\alpha in (138), where the variance ratio (137) has no comparable probabilistic interpretation, and may require system-by-system tuning.
We finally note that a caveat applies when using the RNN diagnostic to treat the decorrelated Fleming–Viot population as independent samples from the QSD (as one does in the generalized parallel replica algorithm), as the particles are correlated. However, these correlations are known to be weak if propagation of chaos holds. Besides, the same caveat applies to the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic (we refer to [4, Section 3.1] for various strategies addressing this concern).
4 Numerical experiments
We present various experiments to delineate the scope of the method developed in Section 3. In Section 4.1, we present direct numerical evidence supporting the theoretical findings of Section 2. In Section 4.2, we provide details on our implementation of the RNN diagnostic of Section 3. In Section 4.3, we benchmark the performance of our RNN diagnostic on synthetic examples, comparing it to the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic from [4].
We also explore the reliability of the diagnostic out-of-distribution, when the training prior is deliberately misspecified. We finally investigate in Section 4.4 how the diagnostic behaves in transfer, by applying it to a high-dimensional molecular system, contrasting again its behavior with the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic, in a case where ξ\xi is a slow variable of the system, and in a case where it is not.
All the code required to reproduce these results is available in a public repository [7], and public copies of processed simulation data will be made available in the short future.
4.1 Validation of theoretical predictions
We validate the theoretical claims on the simplest non-trivial example, where |𝒳|=4|\mathcal{X}|=4 and the fibers of ξ\xi (as defined in (119)) satisfy |𝒳1|=|𝒳2|=|𝒵|=2|\mathcal{X}_{1}|=|\mathcal{X}_{2}|=|\mathcal{Z}|=2. We consider the small-parameter chain whose generator is given by (120).
To investigate the effect of timescale separation on decorrelation, we randomly sample ℒmac,ℒmic\mathcal{L}_{\rm mac},\mathcal{L}_{\rm mic} and KK in (120), and let the scale-parameter ε\varepsilon vary.
Every jump rate (prior to any ε\varepsilon-rescaling) is chosen independently at random, uniformly in the interval [1,5][1,5].
The prefactors C∈{Cζ,CλY,CγY}C\in\{C_{\zeta},C_{\lambda}^{Y},C_{\gamma}^{Y}\}, associated respectively with the propagators
Pt∈{et​Π1​ℒX​Π1​Π1,et​ℒY,et​ℒY​PY}P_{t}\in\{\mathrm{e}^{t\Pi_{1}\mathcal{L}^{X}\Pi_{1}}\Pi_{1},\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}^{Y}},\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal{L}^{Y}}P^{Y}\}
(176)
are estimated as supt∈[0,Tmax]​eμ​t​‖Pt‖\underset{t\in[0,T_{\max}]}{\sup}\mathrm{e}^{\mu t}\|P_{t}\|, where each μ∈{ζ,λY,λY+γY}\mu\in\{\zeta,\lambda^{Y},\lambda^{Y}+\gamma^{Y}\} is computed with a numerical eigensolver (this example could conceivably be solved analytically).
For C∈{CλY,CγY}C\in\{C_{\lambda}^{Y},C_{\gamma}^{Y}\}, a discrete timestepping scheme with Δ​t=0.01\Delta t=0.01 is used. For CζC_{\zeta}, a finer timestep ε​Δ​t\varepsilon\Delta t is used instead to resolve fast transient effects. The value of TmaxT_{\max} is determined dynamically, using the criterion Tmax=inf{t>0:‖Pt‖<ωtol​sups<t‖Ps‖}T_{\max}=\inf\{t>0:\,\|P_{t}\|<\omega_{\mathrm{tol}}\sup_{s<t}\|P_{s}\|\}, where we take ωtol=10−12\omega_{\mathrm{tol}}=10^{-12}. We find this is necessary to avoid divergences associated with loss of numerical precision.
For propagation of the non-linear flow (11), we consider three types of initial conditions: i) drawing η0X\eta_{0}^{X} uniformly from the probability simplex 𝒫⁡(𝒳)\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{X}) (random initialization), ii) setting η0X=δP\eta_{0}^{X}=\delta_{P} where P∼𝒰⁡(𝒳)P\sim\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{X}) (Dirac initialization), and iii) η0X=∑z∈𝒵Wz​πz\eta_{0}^{X}=\sum_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}W_{z}\pi_{z} where WW is drawn uniformly from the simplex 𝒫⁡(𝒵)\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z}) (fiber-equilibrated initialization).
In each case, η0Z\eta_{0}^{Z} is determined by the compatibility condition η0Z=ξ⋆​η0X\eta_{0}^{Z}=\xi_{\star}\eta_{0}^{X} of Theorem 5.
In Figure 1, we show one realization of the various components appearing in the expression (99) for the prefactors C,C′C,C^{\prime} of (52) against the timescale separation parameter ζ\zeta. All terms converge to a finite value as ζ→∞\zeta\to\infty, showing that the prefactors are to leading order independent of ζ\zeta in this limit, in accordance with the conclusion of Theorem 7. The overall dependence of the constants on ζ\zeta is mild for this system.
In Figure 2, we show one realization of the supremum in tt of the total variation bias supt≥0​dTV​(ξ⋆​ηtX,ηtZ)\underset{t\geq 0}{\sup}\,\mathrm{d}_{\mathrm{TV}}(\xi_{\star}\eta_{t}^{X},\eta_{t}^{Z}) against ζ\zeta. The supremum scales as 1/ζ1/\zeta as predicted by the bound (52). Generally we observe, uniformly in time, excellent agreement between the coarse-grained canonical description ηtZ\eta_{t}^{Z} and the true flow. We also observe that fiber-equilibrated initial conditions lead to lower bias, which is not apparent in our analysis. This will be further investigated for the case of killed diffusion in future work.
In Figure 3, we show one realization of the long-time decay of the total variation bias, for various choices of initialization and parameter ε\varepsilon.
The scaling is consistent with the rate predicted by (52), while the prefactors CC and C′C^{\prime} from (52) are far from sharp.

Figure 2: Supremum in time of the total variation bias as a function of the timescale separation ζ\zeta. The theoretical scaling in 1/ζ1/\zeta expected from Theorem 5 is indicated with a dashed line.
