4.2 Implementation of the RNN diagnostic
We now investigate the behavior of the RNN on-the-fly diagnostic described in Section 3.2, and we compare it to the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic described in Section 3.1.
In view of applications to MD, we keep in mind that ξ\xi is related to a molecular collective variable ξ¯:Ω→ℝm\overline{\xi}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}^{m}, where Ω\Omega is an open and bounded fixed metastable state of the molecular system at hand. In fact we will assume in this work that ξ¯\overline{\xi} is one-dimensional (i.e. m=1m=1) and bounded (i.e. ξ¯​(Ω)\overline{\xi}(\Omega) is bounded), so that without loss of generality we can assume ξ¯​(Ω)=(0,1)\overline{\xi}(\Omega)=(0,1) after a change of gauge and unit. As the framework described in Section 3.2 does not rely on this assumption, the extension to higher-dimensional CVs is a natural next step, which we will pursue in future work.
The one-dimensional case covers many practical choices of molecular collective variables: bond lengths, dihedral angles, similarity measures, path collective variables, etc. It is also standard practice to define the metastable state in terms of the CV, so that Ω=ξ¯−1​(0,1)\Omega={\overline{\xi}}^{-1}(0,1) in our context.
When ξ¯\overline{\xi} is a slow variable of the molecular system, a natural ansatz class for low-dimensional surrogate dynamics is the set of Markovian diffusions over ℝm\mathbb{R}^{m}. Such processes have been extensively studied in the literature (see for example [39, 60, 38, 18]) as effective dynamics for ξ¯​(X¯)\overline{\xi}(\overline{X}), where the molecular dynamics X¯\overline{X} is defined as an elliptic diffusion process.
For one-dimensional CVs, these effective dynamics assume the form
d​Z¯t=−D⁡(Z¯t)​U′​(Z¯t)​d​t+1β​D′​(Z¯t)​d​t+2​D​(Z¯t)β​d​Bt,\mathrm{d}\overline{Z}_{t}=-D(\overline{Z}_{t})U^{\prime}(\overline{Z}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\frac{1}{\beta}D^{\prime}(\overline{Z}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\frac{2D(\overline{Z}_{t})}{\beta}}\,\mathrm{d}B_{t},
(177)
where the coefficients U:[0,1]→ℝU:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R} and D:[0,1]→(0,∞)D:[0,1]\to(0,\infty) are assumed to be smooth, and correspond physically to free energy and diffusivity profiles respectively. The parameter β=1/k​T>0\beta=1/kT>0 is proportional to the inverse temperature, and BB is a standard Brownian motion on ℝ\mathbb{R}.
In this work, our aim is to use a surrogate process for the decorrelation of X¯\overline{X} within Ω\Omega. We take as an ansatz the class of probability flows associated to dynamics of the form (177), killed at the boundary {0,1}\{0,1\}, and conditioned on non-absorption. Indeed, we expect this class to contain the canonical diffusion associated with the molecular dynamics within Ω\Omega, that is the analog of the canonical model ZZ from Definition 3.
Verifying this rigorously requires extending the coarse-graining results of Section 2 to killed diffusions, but this ansatz will nevertheless motivate our construction of the synthetic prior ρsynth\rho_{\rm synth}.
4.2.1 Construction of the prior ensemble
As the framework described in Section 3.2 only handles discrete surrogate models, we naturally define 𝒵⊂(0,1)\mathcal{Z}\subset(0,1) on a finite grid, and take ξ:𝒳→𝒵\xi:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Z} to be an associated quantization of ξ¯\overline{\xi}.
Correspondingly, the prior ensemble ρdyn\rho_{\rm dyn} is supported on generators of discrete dynamics, which are jump-process discretizations of the continuous dynamics (177). More precisely, ρdyn\rho_{\rm dyn} is supported on the set of generators of nearest-neighbor jump-processes, on a regular grid 𝒵⊂(0,1)\mathcal{Z}\subset(0,1), killed at the boundary ∂𝒵={0,1}\partial^{\mathcal{Z}}=\{0,1\}. The size of the grid |𝒵|=dgrid=100|\mathcal{Z}|=d_{\rm grid}=100 will be fixed throughout.
The relationship between ZZ and Z¯\overline{Z} is the following: the jump rates of ℒZ\mathcal{L}^{Z} are given, for all 1≤k≤dgrid1\leq k\leq d_{\rm grid}, by
ℒk,k±1Z=σdgrid,β​(D⁡(zk±1)+D⁡(zk)2)​e−β2​(U⁡(zk±1)−U⁡(zk)),\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{Z}_{k,k\pm 1}=\sigma_{d_{\rm grid},\beta}\left(\frac{D(z_{k\pm 1})+D(z_{k})}{2}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\beta}{2}(U(z_{k\pm 1})-U(z_{k}))},
(178)
ℒk,kZ=−(ℒk,k−1Z+ℒk,k+1Z),\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{Z}_{k,k}=-\left(\mathcal{L}^{Z}_{k,k-1}+\mathcal{L}^{Z}_{k,k+1}\right),
and zero otherwise, where the energy UU and diffusion DD are evaluated on the grid 𝒵={zk}k=1,…,dgrid\mathcal{Z}=\{z_{k}\}_{k=1,\dots,d_{\rm grid}}, as well as z0:=0z_{0}:=0 and zdgrid+1:=1z_{d_{\rm grid}+1}:=1. The constant σdgrid,β=(dgrid+1)2/β\sigma_{d_{\rm grid},\beta}=(d_{\rm grid}+1)^{2}/\beta is needed to get a consistent approximation of the generator ℒZ¯\mathcal{L}^{\overline{Z}} of the continuous dynamics (177) in the limit dgrid→∞d_{\rm grid}\to\infty.
The jump process ZZ would be mass-conserving were it not for the contributions −ℒ1,0Z-\mathcal{L}^{Z}_{1,0} and −ℒdgrid,dgrid+1Z-\mathcal{L}^{Z}_{d_{\rm grid},d_{\rm grid}+1} to the diagonal coefficients ℒ1,1Z\mathcal{L}^{Z}_{1,1} and ℒdgrid,dgridZ\mathcal{L}^{Z}_{d_{\rm grid},d_{\rm grid}} respectively, which make ℒZ\mathcal{L}^{Z} into the generator of a conditionally irreducible jump process on 𝒵\mathcal{Z}.
Given these definitions, the synthetic prior ρdyn\rho_{\rm dyn} is specified by a sampling procedure for the random physical parameters U,D,βU,D,\beta. The prior distributions of U,DU,D and β\beta are described in detail in Appendix B.1.
In Figure 4, we represent sixteen independent draws of ℒZ\mathcal{L}^{Z} from ρdyn\rho_{\rm dyn}, by plotting the corresponding physical parameters. The prior covers a broad range of metastable states, including ones containing multiple free energy wells at low temperature.
At low temperatures (β≫1\beta\gg 1), there exist initial configurations Z0Z_{0} and landscapes (U,D)(U,D) for which decorrelation inside 𝒵\mathcal{Z} is itself a metastable process. We therefore expect the distribution of t0​(ℒZ,Z0)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) under ρsynth\rho_{\rm synth} to be broad.
This is what we verify in Figure 5, where we plot the density per decade for this distribution, which is defined as
t↦dd⁡(log10⁡t)​ℙρsynth​(t0​(ℒZ,Z0)<t),t\mapsto\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}(\log_{10}\,t)}\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{\rm synth}}\left(t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0})<t\right),
(179)
where we recall ρsynth\rho_{\rm synth} is the total prior over (ℒZ,Z0)(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}). This function is estimated empirically by the black curve in Figure 5. The other lines correspond to density per decade curves of decorrelation times for various diagnostics. As expected, we observe decorrelation times span several orders of magnitude as (ℒZ,Z0)(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) ranges over the ensemble. The procedure to approximate t0​(ℒZ,Z0)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) is described in the next subsection.
Figure 4: Free energy profiles UU (solid lines) and diffusion profiles DD (dotted lines, in logarithmic scale) for sixteen independent samples from the training prior. The value of the inverse temperature β\beta is indicated with a color gradient.
Figure 5: Density per decade (179) of the honest decorrelation times t0​(ℒZ,Z0)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) (black), of the RNN decorrelation times (blue) and of the Gelman–Rubin decorrelation times (orange), for decision thresholds α=0.1\alpha=0.1 (solid lines) and α=0.05\alpha=0.05 (dashed lines).
4.2.2 Synthetic data generation
For each sample from the synthetic prior (ℒZ,Z0)∼ρsynth(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0})\sim\rho_{\rm synth}, we approximate the honest decorrelation time t0​(ℒZ,Z0)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) as follows. We represent the operator ℒZ\mathcal{L}^{Z} defined in (178) as a sparse matrix. This allows t0​(ℒZ,Z0)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) to be computed very quickly by i) solving the sparse eigenproblem defining (λZ,νZ)(\lambda^{Z},\nu^{Z}) using the Julia interface to Arpack and ii) directly computing the non-linear flow (11) in discrete steps of a fixed lag time τgt=0.01\tau_{\rm gt}=0.01, by iteratively applying the adjoint of Qk​τgtZ=ek​τgt​ℒZQ_{k\tau_{\rm gt}}^{Z}=\mathrm{e}^{k\tau_{\rm gt}\mathcal{L}^{Z}} to η0Z∈𝒫⁡(𝒵)\eta_{0}^{Z}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Z}), and normalizing the probability flow.
This makes it possible to compute, for η0Z=δZ0\eta_{0}^{Z}=\delta_{Z_{0}},
t0​(ℒZ,Z0)≈τgt​min⁡{k≥1:dTV​(ηk​τgtZ,νZ)<εcorr},t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0})\approx\tau_{\rm gt}\min\left\{k\geq 1:\,\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}\left(\eta_{k\tau_{\rm gt}}^{Z},\nu^{Z}\right)<\varepsilon_{\rm corr}\right\},
(180)
which amounts to counting the number of iterations to convergence of a power iteration algorithm. We fix εcorr=0.05\varepsilon_{\rm corr}=0.05 throughout our experiments.
For higher-sdimensional grids (|𝒵|∝dgridm|\mathcal{Z}|\propto d_{\rm grid}^{m}), which are needed to represent the dynamics of higher-dimensional collective variables, this approach remains tractable if the operations above are implemented with matrix-free solvers. In particular, the propagation of the semigroup could be implemented using exponential-Krylov methods [52]. For our value of dgridd_{\rm grid}, we found a dense representation of eτgt​ℒZ\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{\rm gt}\mathcal{L}^{Z}} to be more efficient.
The computation of t0​(ℒZ,Z0)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) is affordable enough that it can be performed online during training and evaluation. For this reason, prior samples are never used in more than one training batch (and never thereafter in testing or validation). This specificity of the one-dimensional case likely explains why our model successfully learns in the absence of any explicit regularization strategy.
To compute features, given a choice of number of replicas NN and lag time τ\tau, we sample histogram trajectories
HkZ,ξ=Hist⁡(1τ​∫(k−1)​τk​τη^t,NZ¯Δ​t​𝑑t),H_{k}^{Z,\xi}=\mathrm{Hist}\left(\frac{1}{\tau}\int_{(k-1)\tau}^{k\tau}\widehat{\eta}_{t,N}^{\overline{Z}^{\Delta t}}\,\mathrm{d}t\right),
(181)
where Z¯Δ​t\overline{Z}^{\Delta t} is an Euler–Maruyama discretization of the continuous diffusion (177) corresponding to ZZ, and η^t,NZ¯Δ​t\widehat{\eta}_{t,N}^{\overline{Z}^{\Delta t}} is the empirical measure (136) of an associated Fleming–Viot process (see Definition 9).
This introduces a slight discrepancy with respect to the procedure described in Section 3.2, as the Fleming–Viot processes in Section 3.2 are generated by the discrete dynamics ZZ.
The substitution Z←Z¯Δ​tZ\leftarrow\overline{Z}^{\Delta t} introduces a new discretization bias (which combines with the finite-NN sampling error motivating Assumption (B2)). We expect this discretization error to vanish in the limit dgrid→∞,Δ​t→0d_{\rm grid}\to\infty,\,\Delta t\to 0, and to be partially filtered out by the histogram featurization.
Controlling this discretization bias rigorously requires new results in the numerical analysis of discretized Fleming–Viot diffusions, which as far as we are aware are not yet available in the literature, and lie outside the scope of this work.
This is also consistent with deployment conditions for the RNN diagnostic, as these discretized Fleming–Viot trajectories are generally those available in a simulation setting.
The length of simulated Fleming–Viot trajectories is randomized in training, according to the rule (165), with the added constraint that L≥5L\geq 5, i.e. L=max⁡(5,⌈R​t0​(ℒZ,Z0)/τ⌉),R∼𝒰⁡(1,2)L=\max(5,\lceil Rt_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0})/\tau\rceil),\,R\sim\mathcal{U}(1,2). This rule still satisfies the conditions of Lemma 13. In accordance with the objective (170), we also randomize (N,τ)(N,\tau) in training, where N,τ/Δ​t∼𝒰⁡{10,…,200}N,\tau/\Delta t\sim\,\mathcal{U}\{10,\dots,200\} independently, fixing Δ​t=0.001\Delta t=0.001 throughout.
To amortize the computation of νZ\nu^{Z} and eτgt​ℒZ\mathrm{e}^{\tau_{\rm gt}\mathcal{L}^{Z}}, samples of physical parameters (U,D,β)(U,D,\beta) are used to produce more than one Fleming–Viot simulation. For each (U,D,β)(U,D,\beta) a number of samples of (Z0,N,τ)(Z_{0},N,\tau) are drawn, the QSD and semigroup are computed once, and each decorrelation time t0​(ℒZ,Z0)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) is computed according to (180). One Fleming–Viot simulation is then performed for each Z0Z_{0}, and the feature sequences are finally truncated using several samples of the time-horizon LL.
The values we used produced training batches of
5⏟(U,D,β)×5⏟(Z0,N,τ)×2⏟L=50\underbrace{5}_{(U,D,\beta)}\,\times\,\underbrace{5}_{(Z_{0},N,\tau)}\times\underbrace{2}_{L}=50
(182)
feature sequences. Note that the batch elements are not i.i.d. variables, however their use in the estimator (171) is still consistent in the limit of many independent samples of (U,D,β)(U,D,\beta).
4.2.3 Architecture and training details
We implement the RNN model described in Section 3.2 using the Julia machine learning library Flux [30].
All parameters were updated using the Adam optimizer with parameters (beta1,beta2,epsilon)=(0.9,0.999,10−8)=(0.9,0.999,10^{-8}).
Training proceeds in two steps. In a pretraining step, the architecture of the model is tuned using a Bayesian optimization algorithm implemented with the NePS [1] library.
This hyperparameter search uses a precomputed dataset of 10001000 feature sequences, with 800800 sequences used for training and 200200 sequences used to compute the objective, which consists in the empirical loss for (170) over the held-out set.
The search was specified over the following hyperparameters: input feature dimension dhistd_{\rm hist}, number of channels and depth (number of layers) in the CNN, dimension drnnd_{\rm rnn} and depth of the RNN, width and depth of the classifier, and learning rate in the optimizer.
The same data was used for different candidate architectures, producing a final model with p=195313p=195313 trainable parameters, most of which are in the recurrent layer. The architecture is described in detail in Appendix B.2 (see Table 3).
In a second step, the resulting model’s weights were retrained using the Adam optimizer (with a reinitialized state), using 3.75×1053.75\times 10^{5} feature sequences from 3.75×1043.75\times 10^{4} physical landscapes (U,D,β)(U,D,\beta), generated during training and assembled into batches according to (182).
A cosine learning-rate schedule (ηk=ηmin+12​(ηmax−ηmin)​(1+cos⁡(k​π/K))\eta_{k}=\eta_{\rm min}+\frac{1}{2}(\eta_{\rm max}-\eta_{\rm min})(1+\cos(k\pi/K)) with ηmax=5×10−4,ηmin=5×10−5\eta_{\rm max}=5\times 10^{-4},\eta_{\rm min}=5\times 10^{-5}) was applied, where each kk corresponds to an “epoch” of 5050 training batches, for a total of K=150K=150 epochs.
Training was performed on a laptop computer, using 55 CPU cores for approximately 1111 hours.
4.3 Performance on synthetic data
We first validate the RNN diagnostic (175) on synthetic data, comparing it to the Gelman–Rubin (139) diagnostic.
In this section, samples of (ℒZ,Z0,N,τ)(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0},N,\tau) are drawn from the training distribution, and the corresponding Fleming–Viot simulations are performed on a time-horizon of 10​t0​(ℒZ,Z0)10t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}).
A diagnostic which predicts a decorrelation time larger than this time horizon is declared to “fail”, as a ten-fold overestimation of the decorrelation time (140) roughly corresponds to a ten-fold decrease in the parallel efficiency of the parallel replica algorithm (see [46, Section 2.6] or [6, Appendix B]).
4.3.1 Binary classification performance
We first investigate the performance of each diagnostic as a decorrelation classifier in the training distribution. We sample uniformly at random a final observation frame KK such that τ≤K​τ≤2​t0​(ℒZ,Z0)\tau\leq K\tau\leq 2t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}), and compute the corresponding diagnostic statistics, namely hK,θ​(H1,…,HK)h_{K,\theta}(H_{1},\dots,H_{K}) for the RNN diagnostic, and Rτ​K,ξ⁡(z)=zZR_{\tau K,\xi(z)=z}^{Z} for the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic (see Equation (137)).
In this experiment, we will also compare this to the instantaneous total-variation distances
OkZ=dTV​(Hk,Hist⁡(νZ)),1≤k≤KO_{k}^{Z}=\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}\left(H_{k},{\rm Hist}\left(\nu^{Z}\right)\right),\qquad 1\leq k\leq K
(183)
between each Fleming–Viot histogram HkH_{k} and the histogram featurization of the QSD (using dhist=64d_{\rm hist}=64 bins). The associated diagnostic is given by
tcorrTV​(α)=τ×inf{k≥1:OkZ<α}.t_{\rm corr}^{\rm TV}(\alpha)=\tau\times\inf\,\left\{k\geq 1:\,\,O_{k}^{Z}<\alpha\right\}.
(184)
This diagnostic is non-recurrent, but directly informed of νZ\nu^{Z}, by contrast to the RNN and Gelman–Rubin diagnostics for which the opposite statement holds.
The Gelman–Rubin statistic (137) is computed at every multiple of the lag time τ\tau, and the reported Gelman–Rubin decorrelation time is therefore
tcorrGR​(α)=τ×inf{k≥1:Rk​τ,ξZ<1+α}t_{\rm corr}^{\rm GR}(\alpha)=\tau\times\inf\left\{k\geq 1:\,\,R_{k\tau,\xi}^{Z}<1+\alpha\right\}
(185)
rather than (139). Therefore τ\tau acts as a burn-in time and a decision period (which both have to be defined in practical applications of Gelman–Rubin [4, 28]).
Each of these diagnostics can be equivalently expressed in terms of an observable f:E→[0,∞){f:E\to[0,\infty)} over a set of sequences EE, where f→0f\to 0 as the diagnostic confidently predicts decorrelation.
Given a partition of a sequence dataset 𝒟=𝒟0∪𝒟1\mathcal{D}=\mathcal{D}_{0}\cup\mathcal{D}_{1} into negative and positive examples (corresponding to transient and quasi-stationary endpoints, respectively), the receiver operating characteristic curve (ROC curve, see for example [45, Section 5.7.2.1]) is a classical measure of classifier performance,
which we estimate as a parametric curve α↦(FPR⁡(α),TPR⁡(α))\alpha\mapsto({\rm FPR}(\alpha),{\rm TPR}(\alpha)), where FPR and TPR are empirical false positive and true positive rates
FPR⁡(α)=1|𝒟0|​∑s∈𝒟0𝟙f⁡(s)<α,TPR⁡(α)=1|𝒟1|​∑s∈𝒟1𝟙f⁡(s)<α.{\rm FPR}(\alpha)=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{0}|}\sum_{s\in\mathcal{D}_{0}}\mathbbm{1}_{f(s)<\alpha},\quad{\rm TPR}(\alpha)=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{1}|}\sum_{s\in\mathcal{D}_{1}}\mathbbm{1}_{f(s)<\alpha}.
(186)
The area under the ROC curve (AUC) corresponds to the probability that a random pair of negative and positive examples (s0,s1)∈𝒟0×𝒟1(s_{0},s_{1})\in\mathcal{D}_{0}\times\mathcal{D}_{1} is correctly ranked by the classifier statistic, i.e. f⁡(s0)>f⁡(s1)f(s_{0})>f(s_{1}).
We estimate it from the Wilcoxon–Mann–Whitney U statistic (see [27, 10])
AUC=1|𝒟0|​|𝒟1|​∑s0∈𝒟0∑s1∈𝒟1(𝟙f⁡(s0)>f⁡(s1)+12​𝟙f⁡(s0)=f⁡(s1)).{\rm AUC}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{D}_{0}||\mathcal{D}_{1}|}\sum_{s_{0}\in\mathcal{D}_{0}}\sum_{s_{1}\in\mathcal{D}_{1}}\left(\mathbbm{1}_{f(s_{0})>f(s_{1})}+\frac{1}{2}\mathbbm{1}_{f(s_{0})=f(s_{1})}\right).
(187)
The AUC score is a popular metric for assessing the overall discriminative quality of a binary classifier, as it is independent of the choice of decision threshold α\alpha.
The experiment was performed on a 6×66\times 6 grid of design parameters, N,τ/Δ​t∈{10,25,50,100,150,200}N,\tau/\Delta t\in\{10,25,50,100,150,200\}.
For each choice of design parameter, 200200 validation batches were assembled, consisting of 55 draws of physical parameters (U,D,β)(U,D,\beta) times 55 draws of initial points Z0Z_{0}, each giving one feature sequence (training batches, by contrast, assembled two feature sequences for each Z0Z_{0}).
In Figure 6, we show the ROC curves for the three considered diagnostics, aggregating samples over all choices of design parameters (N,τ)(N,\tau).
The RNN curve dominates the Gelman–Rubin curve, which itself dominates the total variation curve associated with (183), indicating a strict ordering in discriminative quality. This ordering is reflected in the computed AUC scores (0.96,0.820.96,0.82 and 0.720.72 respectively).
The poor performance of the total variation diagnostic reveals the usefulness of a recurrent diagnostic such as the RNN or Gelman–Rubin, since access to a reference QSD and HkH_{k} alone are insufficient to accurately predict decorrelation over a broad range of systems, due to finite-particle noise, even in toy one-dimensional systems.
In Figure 7, we report estimated AUC scores for each choice of (N,τ)(N,\tau) in the parameter grid. We observe that the RNN diagnostic uniformly outscores the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic, in the sense that the worst RNN AUC score is higher than the best Gelman–Rubin AUC score within the grid.
We also observe that both the RNN and Gelman–Rubin diagnostics are relatively robust to the choice of τ\tau on this task (recall that τ\tau plays the role of a burn-in time for the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic), and less so to the choice of NN, with performance improving monotonically for higher numbers of replicas, suggesting that the finite-NN Fleming–Viot particle approximation error plays a non-negligible role.
This is further confirmed by the total variation AUC scores, which show a high sensitivity to both the choice of τ\tau and NN: for low values of (N,τ)(N,\tau), the total-variation diagnostic is barely more discriminative than a random classifier, with AUC scores increasing monotonically as each frame HkH_{k} accumulates more samples.
For the higher values of (N,τ)(N,\tau) we consider, the total-variation diagnostic eventually outscores the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic. The sensitivity of the total variation diagnostic to the number of aggregated samples in each frame can be explained by the fact that the statistic (183) has no mechanism by which to filter out the finite-NN error.
We consider the total variation diagnostic to be impractical on the basis of these results, and therefore exclude it from further benchmarks.
Figure 6: ROC curves for the RNN, Gelman–Rubin and histogram TV (183) diagnostics, within the training prior. Samples are aggregated over a grid of design parameters (N,τ)(N,\tau). The ROC-AUC score of each diagnostic is indicated in the legend.
Figure 7: ROC-AUC scores of the RNN, Gelman–Rubin and histogram TV (183) diagnostics for the sub-classification tasks associated to each design setting (N,τ)(N,\tau), under the training prior.
4.3.2 On-the-fly quasi-stationarity detection
We next study the behavior of the Gelman–Rubin and RNN diagnostics as on-the-fly predictors of the decorrelation time, for fixed choices of the decision threshold α\alpha. For both diagnostics, we compare a lax decision threshold α=0.1\alpha=0.1 and the more conservative threshold α=0.05\alpha=0.05. For the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic, these values are those prescribed in [4, 28], and for the RNN, these are motivated by the average posterior risk estimates of Lemmas 13 and 14.
The Fleming–Viot simulations are the same as those used in the previous subsection (corresponding in particular to many values of (N,τ)(N,\tau)), which were simulated up to 10​t0​(ℒZ,Z0)10t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) to allow diagnostics to declare very late decorrelation times if necessary. If a diagnostic does not declare decorrelation within this time-frame, it is deemed to fail on this trajectory.
In Figure 8, we show empirical distributions of the diagnostic decorrelation times (175) and (185), in units of the honest decorrelation time t0​(ℒZ,Z0)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}). The area under each curve is given by the proportion of Fleming–Viot trajectories for which the diagnostic did not fail.
The distribution of tcorrRNN​(α)t^{\rm RNN}_{\rm corr}(\alpha) has a sharp mode around t0​(ℒZ,Z0)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) (for both choices of α\alpha), whereas tcorrGR​(α)/t0t^{\rm GR}_{\rm corr}(\alpha)/t_{0} has a much wider distribution with a mode around 44 at the lax setting α=0.1\alpha=0.1, and 88 at the conservative setting α=0.05\alpha=0.05.
The Gelman–Rubin diagnostic fails on a substantial proportion of trajectories (9.7%9.7\% for α=0.1\alpha=0.1 and 50%50\% at α=0.05\alpha=0.05), which biases the empirical distribution towards earlier Gelman–Rubin decorrelation times. By contrast, only 0.30.3 and 1%1\% of trajectories cause the RNN diagnostic to fail for the lax and conservative thresholds, respectively.
We note that the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic is much more sensitive to the choice of decision threshold, as indicated by the distribution of decorrelation times shifting by an approximate factor of two when α\alpha is halved (0.1→0.050.1\to 0.05). This behavior is consistent with an algebraic time-convergence of the variance ratio in (137) at a rate 1/t1/t.
Figure 8: Distributions of the declared decorrelation times tcorr​(α)t_{\rm corr}(\alpha) in the training prior, in units of t0​(ℒZ,Z0)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}), for the RNN (blue) and Gelman–Rubin (orange) diagnostics, for α=0.1\alpha=0.1 (solid lines) and α=0.05\alpha=0.05 (dashed lines). The proportion of failed trajectories for each diagnostic is indicated in the legend.
4.3.3 Posterior risk calibration and prior misspecifications
We next study the probabilistic interpretability of the decision threshold α\alpha in the RNN diagnostic, as introduced in Lemmas 13 and 14.
We investigate the calibration of our learned approximation hθh_{\theta} to the posterior-averaged risk of false decorrelation.
A total of 600600 batches were drawn from the training distribution, representing 1500015000 Fleming–Viot trajectories, each simulation lasting for 2​t0​(ℒZ,Z0)2t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}). At every frame, the output of the RNN hθh_{\theta} is recorded, giving 1.24×1061.24\times 10^{6} predictions in total. In Figure 9, we plot the model’s predicted probabilities against the empirical proportion of decorrelated Fleming–Viot trajectories, according to the reference definition (180). Predictions are grouped into 2020 bins of equal width (the smallest bin containing 1626216262 predictions).
We observe that the model underestimates decorrelation probabilities, on average over the prior (the curve lies above the diagonal), as expected from Lemma 13. Generally, this under-confidence is mild, and the model appears well-calibrated, particularly for high predicted probabilities (corresponding to low values of α\alpha).
Similarly, and consistently with Lemma 14, the risk of false decorrelation appears well-calibrated on average over the training prior, as the empirical proportion of falsely-decorrelated trajectories is below α\alpha (as indicated by the “early” columns in the first row of Table 1(a)).
We next study the behavior of these empirical risks on purposefully misspecified priors, obtained by shifting the distributions of physical parameters (U,D,β)(U,D,\beta) away from the training distribution. We experiment with the following transformations.
∘\circ 
high DD: diffusion coefficients are larger than those observed in training.
∘\circ 
low TT: temperatures are lower (higher β\beta parameters) than those observed in training.
∘\circ 
low TT, high DD: a combination of the two previous transformations.
∘\circ 
low TT/one-well: temperatures are lower, but UU has only shallow energy barriers within the state, so decorrelation is not a metastable process.
Precise definitions for these shifts are provided in Appendix B.1.
In Figure 10, we repeat the calibration study on each of these shifted priors. The least populated bins contained respectively 18991899, 4063740637, 38243824 and 2769327693 samples for each of the transformations listed above.
Beyond what could be expected from our methodology, the model remains reliable on average in terms of false-decorrelation risk (see the “early” columns in Table 1(a)), and generally conservative on average, as the predicted risk typically overestimates the empirical risk.
A mild miscalibration occurs at low temperatures (the “low TT” ensemble). We can speculate that this is due to overconfident predictions in cases where there are metastable substates, within which the particle approximation becomes quasi-stationary, and is mistakenly labeled as stationary.
Any nonconvergence diagnostic may fail this way. If such metastable substates are excluded from the ensemble (low TT, one well), this overconfidence appears to be largely corrected.
In the first lines of Table 1, we collect some additional summary statistics associated with Fleming–Viot trajectories sampled under these prior shifts, reporting, for both the RNN and Gelman–Rubin diagnostics: AUC scores (for samples aggregated over time and parameters N,τN,\tau), median normalized decorrelation time tcorr​(α)/t0​(ℒZ,Z0)t_{\rm corr}(\alpha)/t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) and 25%,75%25\%,75\% quantiles, empirical false decorrelation risks, and empirical failure risks for each of the decision thresholds α∈{0.1,0.05}\alpha\in\{0.1,0.05\}.
The RNN diagnostic outscores the Gelman–Rubin in AUC under all prior shifts. We observe, in line with Figure 8, that the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic generally overestimates the reference decorrelation time, with a larger interquartile range than for the RNN. The Gelman–Rubin diagnostic fails on a significant number of trajectories, sometimes on a majority of them.
On the other hand, the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic has a lower empirical risk of false decorrelation compared to the RNN diagnostic, in line with a general conservativeness at these choices of α\alpha. Beyond what Lemma 14 allows, the empirical risk of false decorrelation is controlled by α\alpha for the RNN diagnostic, over all the synthetic prior misspecifications we considered.
Figure 9: Empirical decorrelation frequency as a function of empirical RNN predictions under the training prior. Perfect calibration is indicated with a dotted line. Confidence bands are computed with a bootstrap estimator, treating different Fleming–Viot trajectories on the same landscape (U,D,β)(U,D,\beta) as independent samples.
Figure 10: Empirical frequency of decorrelation as a function of the probability predicted by the RNN diagnostic, under each prior shift (blue lines) and under the training prior (grey lines). Perfect calibration is indicated with a dotted line.
4.4 Transfer to a solvated dimer
We now compare the behavior of the RNN diagnostic to the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic on a solvated dimer, a toy model of biochemical systems.
We describe the system in Section 4.4.1, the dynamics and reference data in Sections 4.4.2 and 4.4.3, and the results of our three experiments in Sections 4.4.4, 4.4.5 and 4.4.6.
4.4.1 Dimer system and collective variables
The system is composed of Nat=512N_{\rm at}=512 atoms placed in a cubic periodic domain 𝒬=L​𝕋3​Nat\mathcal{Q}=L\mathbb{T}^{3N_{\rm at}} (𝕋=ℝ/ℤ\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z} is the one-dimensional torus), with L=(Nat/ρ)1/3L=(N_{\rm at}/\rho)^{1/3} enforcing a particle density of ρ=0.693\rho=0.693, and is simulated at an inverse temperature β=1/(kB​T)=1\beta=1/(k_{\rm B}T)=1. All quantities in this experiment are given in reduced Lennard–Jones units, see [2, Appendix B].
To model the liquid phase typical of biochemistry, the solvent particles interact via the WCA pair potential
vWCA(r)={4​ϵ​[(rσ)−12−(rσ)−6]if ​0<r<21/6​σ,−ϵif ​r≥21/6​σ.v_{\rm{WCA}}(r)=\left\{\begin{aligned} &4\epsilon\left[\left(\frac{r}{\sigma}\right)^{-12}-\left(\frac{r}{\sigma}\right)^{-6}\right]&\text{if }0<r<2^{1/6}\sigma,\\
&-\epsilon&\text{if }r\geq 2^{1/6}\sigma.\end{aligned}\right.
(188)
This potential is simply the repulsive component of the classical Lennard–Jones potential, where we take ϵ=σ=1.0\epsilon=\sigma=1.0.
A selected pair of atoms (say atoms 11 and 22) acts as a solvated dimer. Between these atoms, the WCA interaction is turned off, and replaced by a potential of their interatomic distance. This potential is defined for r∈(0.68,2.22)r\in(0.68,2.22) by
W⁡(r)=KW​(s​(r)66−(1+CW2)​s​(r)44+CW2​s​(r)22)+EW​s​(r),W(r)=K_{W}\left(\frac{s(r)^{6}}{6}-(1+C_{W}^{2})\frac{s(r)^{4}}{4}+C_{W}^{2}\frac{s(r)^{2}}{2}\right)+E_{W}s(r),
(189)
where s⁡(r)=(r−1.45)/0.7s(r)=(r-1.45)/0.7 and (KW,CW,EW)=(78,0.6,0.49)(K_{W},C_{W},E_{W})=(78,0.6,0.49), and is extended linearly beyond this range.
The total interaction potential of the system is therefore
V⁡(q)=∑1≤i<j≤Nat(i,j)≠(1,2)vWCA​(ri​j)+W⁡(r12),V(q)=\sum_{\underset{(i,j)\neq(1,2)}{1\leq i<j\leq N_{\rm at}}}v_{\rm{WCA}}(r_{ij})+W(r_{12}),
(190)
where q=(q1,…,qNat)q=(q_{1},\dots,q_{N_{\rm at}}) is the vector of atomic positions and ri​jr_{ij} denotes the periodic distance between atoms ii and jj, which is their geodesic distance in L​𝕋3L\mathbb{T}^{3}.
The system’s equilibrium distribution is the canonical Gibbs measure
μ⁡(d​q)=e−β​V​(q)∫𝒬e−β​V​d​q∈𝒫⁡(𝒬).\mu(\mathrm{d}q)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V(q)}}{\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{Q}}\mathrm{e}^{-\beta V}}\,\mathrm{d}q\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Q}).
(191)
The potential WW induces three metastable states in the system, corresponding to three conformations of the dimer molecule: a compact conformation, an intermediate conformation, and a stretched conformation.
In this experiment, we consider decorrelations of the system within the intermediate state, defined by
Ω={rL<r12<rR},(rL,rR)=(1.0396,1.8795).\Omega=\left\{r_{\rm L}<r_{12}<r_{\rm R}\right\},\qquad(r_{\rm L},r_{\rm R})=(1.0396,1.8795).
(192)
To monitor the system, we consider two collective variables:
ξ¯​(q)=r12−rLrR−rL,ncoord​(q)=∑j=3Nat𝒮⁡(min⁡(r1​j,r2​j)),𝒮⁡(r)=11+er−rcw,\overline{\xi}(q)=\frac{r_{12}-r_{\rm L}}{r_{\rm R}-r_{\rm L}},\qquad n_{\rm coord}(q)=\sum_{j=3}^{N_{\rm at}}\mathscr{S}(\min(r_{1j},r_{2j})),\qquad{\mathscr{S}}(r)=\frac{1}{1+e^{\frac{r-r_{c}}{w}}},
(193)
where rc=21/6​σr_{c}=2^{1/6}\sigma is the interaction radius of the WCA potential and w=0.2w=0.2.
The variable ξ¯\overline{\xi} is simply the dimer length, normalized so that Ω=ξ¯−1​(0,1)\Omega=\overline{\xi}^{-1}(0,1). The variable ncoordn_{\rm coord} is a soft solvent coordination number.
Atoms at a distance of at least 1,1.5,2​rc1,1.5,2r_{c} from the dimer contribute respectively at most 0.5,0.060.5,0.06 and 0.0040.004 to the sum in (193), so that ncoordn_{\rm coord} roughly counts the number of atoms in the first solvation shell (the value 1.5​rc1.5r_{c} being a standard shell radius for Lennard–Jones fluids, see [2, Figure 2.2]).
In Figure 11, we represent three sample configurations of the solvated dimer system, within each one of its metastable states. The dimer is represented as a pair of bonded atoms in black, while the solvent molecules at a distance at most 1.5​rc1.5r_{c} from the dimer are shaded in green.
In Figure 12, we show free energy profiles for the system in the interatomic distance r12r_{12}. The dashed line in the left panel corresponds to the unsolvated free energy (the case Nat=2N_{\rm at}=2) which can be computed in closed form from WW and a polar change of variables, expressing μ\mu in the coordinate r12r_{12}. This change of variables gives an explicit contribution from the Jacobian, which is the entropic correction in the Helmholtz free energy, reflecting the fact that low-r12r_{12} configurations have low entropy.
The solid curve corresponds to the solvated free energy FF, computed using a simple histogram estimator (see [40, Section 2.5.1.1.]) from a reference trajectory of the equilibrium dynamics (see Equation (194) below). The shaded area corresponds to the metastable state Ω\Omega, and the blue points 1,2,3,4,5,5′1,2,3,4,5,5^{\prime} correspond to reference initial configurations to be used in our experiments. On the right panel, we represent the restriction to Ω\Omega of the free energy in the coordinate ξ¯\overline{\xi} (solid black line), superimposed with several samples of β​U\beta U from the training prior (grey lines). This suggests that the distribution of (β,U)(\beta,U) is reasonably calibrated to this system, but this is only a heuristic interpretation, as FF has no simple relationship to the canonical model ZZ.
Figure 11: Configurations of the solvated dimer in each of its three metastable conformations. The dimer atoms are shown in black, and the solvent atoms within 1.5​rc1.5r_{c}, contributing the bulk of ncoordn_{\rm coord} in green.
Figure 12: (a) Free energy of the dimer length r12r_{12} (solid line) and unsolvated free energy (dashed line). The state Ω\Omega is shaded, and the initial points are indicated in blue. (b) Free energy as a function of ξ¯\overline{\xi} in Ω\Omega, with draws β​U\beta U from the training prior (grey lines).
4.4.2 Molecular dynamics
The system evolves under the overdamped Langevin dynamics, which is defined by the SDE
dX¯t=−D∇V(X¯t)dt+2βD1/2dWt,\mathrm{d}\overline{X}_{t}=-D\nabla V(\overline{X}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\frac{2}{\beta}}D^{1/2}\,\mathrm{d}W_{t},
(194)
where WW is a standard 3​Nat3N_{\rm at}-dimensional Brownian motion, and the diffusion matrix is a diagonal matrix
D=Dd​(I1+I2)+∑i≥3Ii,Dd>0,D=D_{d}(\mathrm{I}_{1}+\mathrm{I}_{2})+\sum_{i\geq 3}\mathrm{I}_{i},\qquad D_{d}>0,
(195)
with IiI_{i} acting as the identity on the ii-th atom’s degrees of freedom, and zero otherwise. The coefficient Dd>0D_{d}>0 is used to control the mobility of the dimer, which will be useful to control whether ξ¯\overline{\xi} is a slow (low DdD_{d}) or a fast (high DdD_{d}) variable, and therefore whether the system lies in the physical regime described by the assumptions of Theorem 5.
Broadly speaking, the case Dd=0.05D_{d}=0.05 is a toy model for a heavy molecule in water solution, with the dimer length representing some global geometric feature of the solute, while the case Dd=1D_{d}=1 is rather representative of light solutes.
In all simulations, the trajectories of (194) (and any derived Fleming–Viot process) are discretized using an Euler–Maruyama scheme with a timestep Δ​t=10−4\Delta t=10^{-4}.
Molecular dynamics simulations were implemented in Julia, using functionalities of the package Molly [24].
4.4.3 Reference decorrelation times and scale separation
As the honest decorrelation time (140) is sensitive to the initial condition η0X\eta_{0}^{X}, we perform our experiments for six deterministic initial conditions q0∈Ωq_{0}\in\Omega, constructed as follows.
First, the dimer is frozen at a length r0∈{1.1,1.25,1.45,1.65,1.82}r_{0}\in\{1.1,1.25,1.45,1.65,1.82\}, corresponding to the five blue points depicted in Figure 12. Then the solvent molecules are added to the system, and relaxed under overdamped Langevin dynamics for tth=20t_{\rm th}=20, where the dimer-solvent WCA interaction is treated as an external potential, keeping the dimer configuration fixed.
For the most stretched initial point r0=1.82r_{0}=1.82, two initial configurations were sampled in this way. These are labeled points 55 and 5′5^{\prime} in the results, while the others are labeled 1,2,31,2,3 and 44.
For each initial configuration, M=3000M=3000 independent Fleming–Viot simulations for the one-particle dynamics (194) killed at ∂Ω\partial\Omega were performed, using N=72N=72 replicas each.
Estimators η^tX¯\widehat{\eta}_{t}^{\overline{X}} of ηtX¯\eta_{t}^{\overline{X}} are constructed by aggregating over independent simulations
η^tX¯=1M​∑m=1Mη^t,NX¯,(m),\widehat{\eta}_{t}^{\overline{X}}=\frac{1}{M}\sum_{m=1}^{M}\widehat{\eta}^{\overline{X},(m)}_{t,N},
(196)
where η^t,NX¯,(m)\widehat{\eta}^{\overline{X},(m)}_{t,N} denotes the empirical measure of the mm-th Fleming–Viot simulation. Traces of (ξ¯,ncoord)⋆​η^tX¯(\overline{\xi},n_{\rm coord})_{\star}\widehat{\eta}_{t}^{\overline{X}} were recorded during the simulations.
Simulations were run for tref=3t_{\rm ref}=3, and the second half of these η^tX¯\widehat{\eta}_{t}^{\overline{X}} for t∈[1.5,3]t\in[1.5,3] was averaged to estimate the QSD
ν^X¯=2tref​∫tref/2trefη^tX¯​𝑑t,\widehat{\nu}^{\overline{X}}=\frac{2}{t_{\rm ref}}\int_{t_{\rm ref}/2}^{t_{\rm ref}}\widehat{\eta}_{t}^{\overline{X}}\,\mathrm{d}t,
(197)
with time-averages replaced by averages over the numerical trajectory.
Reference decorrelation times do not rely on direct estimators dTV​(ηtX¯,νX¯)\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}(\eta_{t}^{\overline{X}},\nu^{\overline{X}}), which are computationally intractable in this setting.
Instead, we estimate dTV​(f⋆​ηtX¯,f⋆​νX¯)\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}(f_{\star}\eta_{t}^{\overline{X}},f_{\star}\nu^{\overline{X}}) for f∈{ξ¯,ncoord,(ξ¯,ncoord)}f\in\{\overline{\xi},n_{\rm coord},(\overline{\xi},n_{\rm coord})\}. When f=ξ¯f=\overline{\xi} is a slow variable (in the sense of Theorem 5), the generalization of (56) to dynamics such as (194) would imply that quasi-stationarity in ξ¯\overline{\xi} predicts (approximate) quasi-stationarity in the full system.
In our first two experiments, the role of both on-the-fly diagnostics is to detect quasi-stationarity of ξ¯⋆​ηtX¯\overline{\xi}_{\star}\eta_{t}^{\overline{X}}.
The cases f∈{ncoord,(ξ¯,ncoord)}f\in\{n_{\rm coord},(\overline{\xi},n_{\rm coord})\} are included at this point to monitor failures in the assumption that ξ¯\overline{\xi} is a slow variable, and therefore failures of our framework to justify the use of a marginal diagnostic in ξ¯\overline{\xi}.
Total variation distances between marginals are estimated using histograms. For each ff, we fix a regular partition of the range of ff: dhist=64d_{\rm hist}=64 equal bins for f=ξ¯,ncoordf=\overline{\xi},n_{\rm coord} (where the range of ncoordn_{\rm coord} is clamped to [4,10][4,10]), and a partition of (0,1)×(4,10)(0,1)\times(4,10) into 8×88\times 8 regular bins for f=(ξ¯,ncoord)f=(\overline{\xi},n_{\rm coord}).
In each case, we denote by Histf{\rm Hist}_{f} the corresponding histogram map, which sends a point probability measure to a probability measure with piecewise-constant Lebesgue-density.
We then construct an estimate for dTV​(f⋆​ηtX¯,f⋆​νX¯)\mathrm{d}_{\rm TV}(f_{\star}\eta_{t}^{\overline{X}},f_{\star}\nu^{\overline{X}}) via
D^f​(t)=‖Histf​(f⋆​η^tX¯)−Histf​(f⋆​ν^X¯)‖L1,\widehat{D}_{f}(t)=\left\|{\rm Hist}_{f}\left(f_{\star}\widehat{\eta}_{t}^{\overline{X}}\right)-{\rm Hist}_{f}\left(f_{\star}\widehat{\nu}^{\overline{X}}\right)\right\|_{L^{1}},
(198)
and define reference decorrelation times in ff as
t0​(f)=inf{t≥0:D^f​(s)<εcorr​∀s∈[t,t+th]},t_{0}(f)=\inf\left\{t\geq 0:\,\,\widehat{D}_{f}(s)<\varepsilon_{\rm corr}\,\forall\,s\in[t,t+t_{\rm h}]\right\},
(199)
where th=0.05t_{\rm h}=0.05 for Dd=1D_{d}=1 and th=0.2t_{\rm h}=0.2 for Dd=0.05D_{d}=0.05.
In Figure 13, we show decorrelation plots of D^f​(t)\widehat{D}_{f}(t) for each of the three observables ff we consider, each of the six initial configurations and both values of the diffusion coefficient Dd∈{0.05,1}D_{d}\in\{0.05,1\}. We confirm that the decorrelation time is generally sensitive to the choice of initial conditions, with configuration 33, located near the free-energy minimum (see Figure 12) leading to significantly shorter decorrelation times in all cases.
Moreover, we observe that histograms in ξ¯\overline{\xi} routinely converge before those in ncoordn_{\rm coord} in the case Dd=1D_{d}=1 (and therefore ξ¯\overline{\xi} fails to be a slow variable), whereas in the case Dd=0.05D_{d}=0.05, decorrelation times (in our marginal definition (199), indicated by vertical lines) are always predicted by ξ¯\overline{\xi}. Therefore we informally expect the use of the RNN diagnostic in ξ¯\overline{\xi} to be sound in the case Dd=0.05D_{d}=0.05, but not Dd=1D_{d}=1, as in this latter case, ξ¯⋆​ηtX¯\overline{\xi}_{\star}\eta_{t}^{\overline{X}} may not be approximable by a non-linear Markovian probability flow. However we note that scale separation between ncoordn_{\rm coord} and ξ¯\overline{\xi} is at most mild even in the case Dd=0.05D_{d}=0.05. Therefore, the system is not deep inside the adiabatic regime of Section 2.6.
The choice of th>0t_{\rm h}>0 in (199) only has a significant effect when Dd=1D_{d}=1 in initial configuration 55.
Figure 13: Histogram distances D^f​(t)\widehat{D}_{f}(t) (198) to their quasi-stationary state as a function of time, for f=ξ¯,ncoordf=\overline{\xi},n_{\rm coord} and (ξ¯,ncoord)(\overline{\xi},n_{\rm coord}). Columns correspond to different initial conditions, and rows to different values of the parameter DdD_{d}. Reference decorrelation times t0​(f)t_{0}(f) defined in (199) are indicated with vertical lines (solid gray: f=ξ¯f=\overline{\xi}, dashed pink: OPENf=(ξ¯,ncoord))f=(\overline{\xi},n_{\rm coord})). The tolerance threshold εcorr\varepsilon_{\rm corr} is indicated with dotted horizontal lines.
4.4.4 On-the-fly decorrelation detection
For each value Dd∈{0.05,1}D_{d}\in\{0.05,1\}, each initial point and each design parameter (N,τ)(N,\tau), with N∈{25,50,100}N\in\{25,50,100\} and τ/Δ​t∈{100,200,400}\tau/\Delta t\in\{100,200,400\}, 2020 independent Fleming–Viot simulations are performed. Each simulation is observed up to 10​max⁡(t0​(ξ¯),t0​(ξ¯,ncoord))10\max(t_{0}(\overline{\xi}),t_{0}(\overline{\xi},n_{\rm coord})).
The RNN model from Section 4.2, trained on synthetic trajectories, is applied without retraining. Fleming–Viot histogram features in ξ¯\overline{\xi} are aggregated on-the-fly, and the scalar m⁡(N,τ)=N​τm(N,\tau)=\sqrt{N\tau} is used as input in the recurrent layer without rescaling (the lag time τ\tau, in Lennard–Jones units, is therefore directly compared to the synthetic time units used in training). The Gelman–Rubin diagnostic is computed using the collective variable ξ¯\overline{\xi} only (i.e. 𝒪={ξ¯}\mathcal{O}=\{\overline{\xi}\} in (139)).
We note that adding more observables to 𝒪\mathcal{O} can only make the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic more conservative, as we shall see in Section 4.4.6 below.
In Figure 14, we plot distributions of detected decorrelation times for the solvated dimer, in units of t0​(ξ¯)t_{0}(\overline{\xi}). The nonconvergence of ncoordn_{\rm coord} in the case Dd=1D_{d}=1 is hidden from both diagnostics in this experiment, and therefore not taken into account for evaluation.
For Dd=0.05D_{d}=0.05, the RNN is well-calibrated with respect to this reference in terms of empirical false decorrelation risk, and both diagnostics display the same overall behavior as the one observed in Figure 8. Namely, the RNN decorrelation times have a sharp mode around t0​(ξ¯)t_{0}(\overline{\xi}), while the Gelman–Rubin decorrelation times have a spread-out distribution, are highly sensitive to α\alpha, overestimate the reference, and exceed 10​t0​(ξ¯)10t_{0}(\overline{\xi}) on a significant portion of trajectories. The RNN never fails to detect decorrelation within this time horizon.
For Dd=1D_{d}=1, we observe a significant miscalibration in the RNN, with an empirical false decorrelation risk of 57%57\% for α=0.1\alpha=0.1 and 41%41\% for α=0.05\alpha=0.05. For Dd=1D_{d}=1, the distribution of decorrelation times is also shifted to the left for Gelman–Rubin, leading to an overall better diagnostic for Dd=1D_{d}=1. The empirical risks of false decorrelation for the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic are 11%11\% for α=0.1\alpha=0.1 and 0%0\% for α=0.05\alpha=0.05, while the corresponding failure rates are 0%0\% and 2%2\%.
Since the false decorrelation risk is defined with respect to the approximate definition (199), these values should not be interpreted quantitatively. It nevertheless appears that the RNN diagnostic is qualitatively miscalibrated on this system, which is expected from the failure of Assumption (B1). We explore the impact of this miscalibration on exit distribution biases in our next experiment.
The summary statistics reported in Section 4.3 are also reported for this experiment, in the last lines of Tables 1(a) and 1(b), along with the ROC AUC scores for both diagnostics, where the RNN again outscores the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic for both Dd=1D_{d}=1 and Dd=0.05D_{d}=0.05.
We speculate that the risk miscalibration of the RNN could be explained by two factors. A first explanation could be that a higher effective diffusion in ξ¯\overline{\xi} for Dd=1D_{d}=1 lies outside the range of the training prior. Such a failure could be addressed directly by changing the prior. However, the results of Table 1(a) indicate that high DD prior misspecifications do not induce early detections in the synthetic setting.
We therefore believe that the main cause is more fundamental: the fact that ξ¯\overline{\xi} is not slow for Dd=1D_{d}=1 (according to Figure 13) may cause non-Markovian features to appear in the ξ¯\overline{\xi} time-marginals of the non-linear probability flow.
Since such features were never observed in training, the recurrent model’s inputs are out of distribution, its outputs extrapolative, and its predictions are therefore unreliable.
We believe this failure mode can also be addressed in several ways, to be explored in future work: we will come back to this in Section 5 below.
It is interesting to note that the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic performs better where its assumptions (A1)–(A3) suggest it should fail. We doubt whether this is a systematic effect, but rather interpret it as an indication that the relationship between the decision threshold α\alpha and the risk of false decorrelation is system-dependent, which prevents α\alpha from being selected a priori for the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic over a broad range of systems.
Figure 14: Distributions of the declared decorrelation times tcorr​(α)t_{\rm corr}(\alpha), in units of t0​(ξ¯)t_{0}(\overline{\xi}), for the RNN (blue) and Gelman–Rubin (orange) diagnostics on the solvated dimer, for α=0.1\alpha=0.1 (solid lines) and α=0.05\alpha=0.05 (dashed lines). The proportion of trajectories on which each diagnostic fails is indicated in each panel.
4.4.5 Bias on the exit event
We now investigate the effect of the choice of on-the-fly diagnostic on the exit distribution (τΩ,X¯τΩ)(\tau^{\Omega},\overline{X}_{\tau^{\Omega}}) from Ω\Omega (see (2)), starting the dynamics (194) from a replica from the Fleming–Viot state at the decorrelation time of each diagnostic.
When the dynamics starts from an ideal sample from the QSD, exit points and exit times from Ω\Omega are independent, and exit times are exponentially distributed. Moreover, the exit point is independent of the starting configuration. Checking these properties empirically provides a way to indirectly validate decorrelation time definitions (on-the-fly or not) without resorting to an approximate reference such as (199).
At the decorrelation time of each diagnostic (or at time 10​t0​(ξ¯)10t_{0}(\overline{\xi}) when the diagnostic fails) in the experiment of Section 4.4.4, each of the NN replicas of its Fleming–Viot process is taken as the initial point of an independent trajectory of the dynamics (194), which is simulated until it exits Ω\Omega. For each replica, we record the exit time and exit point in this way. As the exit point can only take two values in the continuum limit, we only record whether the dimer exits to the left (to the compact state) or to the right (to the stretched state). Samples are then aggregated over replicas and trajectories.
We also compute a reference exit event distribution in the same way, starting from the final state (after tref=3t_{\rm ref}=3 Lennard–Jones time units, when we assume every system is decorrelated, see Figure 13) of 4848 reference Fleming–Viot simulations per initial configuration and value of DdD_{d}, using 7272 replicas each, giving a total of 34563456 reference exit events per (q0,Dd)(q_{0},D_{d}), and 4147241472 exit events in total.
In Figure 15, we compare empirical exit distributions for the reference initial points and the decorrelated samples according to each diagnostic of the previous subsection.
The left panel compares survival probabilities t↦ℙ⁡(τΩ>t)t\mapsto\mathbb{P}\left(\tau^{\Omega}>t\right), while the right panel compares probabilities of exiting through the left point (r12​(X¯τΩ)=rLCLOSE(r_{12}({\overline{X}}_{\tau^{\Omega}})=r_{\rm L}), which corresponds to a transition to the compact state. In both panels, grey dotted lines correspond to heuristic ±εcorr/2\pm\varepsilon_{\rm corr}/2 error bars around the reference mean, corresponding to the method’s target error level from Proposition 11. On the right panel, the grey shaded region corresponds to a ±1.96​σ\pm 1.96\sigma confidence interval for the reference probabilities.
The choice of diagnostic has no discernible effect on the distribution of exit times at our statistical resolution, and is in every case indistinguishable from the reference distribution, and from one another.
In the case Dd=0.05D_{d}=0.05, we observe no bias or dependence on the initial point in the exit distribution, at our statistical resolution. This suggests that all four diagnostics are valid decorrelation times in this case. As the RNN diagnostic is less conservative (Figure 14), it is the overall better diagnostic for Dd=0.05D_{d}=0.05.
In the case Dd=1D_{d}=1, we observe more dispersion in the exit probabilities for both the RNN and Gelman–Rubin diagnostics. From initial configurations 11 and 44 at α=0.1\alpha=0.1, the RNN exit points appear biased: the empirical probability of a compact transition lies slightly outside the target error range of εcorr/2\varepsilon_{\rm corr}/2. This is consistent with the presence of false decorrelations suggested by the experiment of Section 4.4.4. For other choices of initial configuration or threshold α\alpha, any bias is not clearly beyond the target error, at our level of statistical resolution.
The Gelman–Rubin exit distribution has no discernible bias.
In Figure 16, we investigate the dependence of the distribution of exit times on the exit point, by conditioning the empirical survival probability on the exit point. For the reference population and both diagnostics, for both values of DdD_{d}, the exit times show no dependence on the exit point, consistently with the property (2) of the QSD.
The plots only show these distributions for the lax setting α=0.1\alpha=0.1, as a conservative setting α=0.05\alpha=0.05 is expected to decrease any bias.
Figure 15: Exit distribution from diagnosed samples, for Dd=0.05D_{d}=0.05 (top) and Dd=1D_{d}=1 (bottom). The left panels show empirical survival functions for the exit time, with the short-time behavior represented in the insets. Right panels show the empirical probability of exiting through ξ¯=0\overline{\xi}=0 for each initial point, with 95%95\% confidence intervals, treating Fleming–Viot particles as independent. The reference values and its confidence interval are indicated by the black line and grey band, and the heuristic error level ±εcorr/2\pm\varepsilon_{\rm corr}/2 by dotted lines.
Figure 16: Empirical survival functions of the exit time conditioned on the exit point, through ξ¯=0\overline{\xi}=0 (red) or ξ¯=1\overline{\xi}=1 (blue), starting from a Fleming–Viot population. Left panel: reference decorrelated population. Middle panel: population decorrelated according to the RNN. Right panel: population decorrelated according to the Gelman–Rubin diagnostics.
4.4.6 Using multiple collective variables
We finally address the failure of the RNN diagnostic in the previous section using a simple mitigation strategy. Just as the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic can be defined using multiple collective variables (see (139)), we propose applying the RNN diagnostic to multiple CVs at once.
The feature trajectories can be batched to compute predictions efficiently. Here, we apply both diagnostics with 𝒪={ξ¯,n~coord}\mathcal{O}=\{\overline{\xi},\widetilde{n}_{\rm coord}\}, where the coordination number is normalized
n~coord=16​clamp​(ncoord−4,[0,6])∈[0,1].\widetilde{n}_{\rm coord}=\frac{1}{6}\texttt{clamp}(n_{\rm coord}-4,[0,6])\in[0,1].
(200)
Histogram trajectories Hkn~coordH_{k}^{\widetilde{n}_{\rm coord}} (see (173)) are then recorded, and model predictions hθ,kn~coordh_{\theta,k}^{\widetilde{n}_{\rm coord}} (see (174)) are used to define
t^corrRNN​(α)=max⁡(tcorrRNN​(α),τ×inf{k≥1:hk,θn~coord>1−α}),\widehat{t}_{\rm corr}^{\rm RNN}(\alpha)=\max\left(t_{\rm corr}^{\rm RNN}(\alpha),\tau\times\inf\,\left\{k\geq 1:\,h_{k,\theta}^{\widetilde{n}_{\rm coord}}>1-\alpha\right\}\right),
(201)
where tcorrRNN​(α)t_{\rm corr}^{\rm RNN}(\alpha) is the ξ¯\overline{\xi}-marginal RNN decorrelation time. The reference decorrelation time we use is now the joint decorrelation time t0​(ξ¯,ncoord)t_{0}(\overline{\xi},n_{\rm coord}) defined in (199).
We repeat the experiments of Sections 4.4.4 and 4.4.5 using these new definitions. Summary statistics are presented in Table 1.
For Dd=0.05D_{d}=0.05, the behavior of both the RNN and Gelman–Rubin diagnostics shows little difference compared to the single collective-variable experiment.
For Dd=1D_{d}=1, the RNN diagnostic becomes more conservative, with an empirical false decorrelation risk of zero compared to the reference, yet still very few failures (the RNN diagnostic shows an empirical failure rate of 0.1%0.1\% at α=0.05\alpha=0.05 in this case).
The Gelman–Rubin diagnostic is again more conservative than the RNN, fails more often, and has a lower ROC-AUC, though only slightly in this case.
In Figure 17, we show the equivalents of Figures 14, 15 and 16 using these new definitions of decorrelation times.
The same qualitative behavior as in well-calibrated situations can be observed at the level of decorrelation time distributions. No bias is detectable in the exit distribution.
We stress that this mitigation strategy is heuristic, as marginals of the Fleming–Viot process in n~coord\widetilde{n}_{\rm coord} are not well-represented by the training prior.
In particular, replicas may be killed at the center of the n~coord\widetilde{n}_{\rm coord} domain, rather than only at the boundary for ξ¯\overline{\xi}. It is also possible to include a soft killing rate in the training prior to represent such dynamics.
Figure 17: Behavior of the two-variable diagnostics for α=0.1\alpha=0.1. Top left: distribution of decorrelation times in units of t0​(ξ¯,ncoord)t_{0}(\overline{\xi},n_{\rm coord}) (see Figure 14). Top right: exit-side distribution per initial point (see right panels of Figure 15). Bottom: survival functions conditioned on the exit side, for the RNN (left) and Gelman–Rubin (right, see Figure 16).
4.4.7 Computational cost and summary statistics
The cost of one RNN inference (on a single histogram feature) is comparable to the cost of one MD step for the dimer system on a laptop.
While we have not performed systematic benchmarks for an optimized implementation, this anecdotal evidence suggests that the RNN represents a negligible portion of the total computational cost, while the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic is cheaper still.
As the cost of RNN inference is independent of the number of atoms in the system and its relative cost decreases as the lag time τ\tau increases, we therefore expect the approach to be scalable to much larger systems with negligible computational cost in deployment.

5 Discussion and perspectives
We conclude by summarizing the main features and limitations of our proposed method, and outlining some perspectives for future work addressing these limitations.
∘\circ 
Deployability in accelerated molecular dynamics algorithms. The RNN we propose only requires periodic observations of a Fleming–Viot process in the collective variable ξ¯\overline{\xi}. As this is precisely the operational setting of the generalized parallel replica algorithm [4, 28], an RNN diagnostic could replace the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic in these algorithms with minor implementation difficulties. Even on the small system considered in Section 4.4, the computational cost of inference will likely remain negligible compared to the cost of evaluating the interatomic forces.
∘\circ 
Physical information as a modeling input. Our method uses prior knowledge of the system, which is encoded in the definition of the prior ρsynth\rho_{\rm synth} over plausible surrogate processes (Assumption (B4)). Our method therefore occupies a middle ground between MCMC-inspired diagnostics (which require no prior information, but appear conservative and difficult to calibrate) and deterministic definitions of the decorrelation time (which only apply in specific physical regimes). The experiments of Section 4.3 show empirically that this prior can be defined rather broadly (see Figures 5 and 4), with the diagnostic nevertheless retaining high discriminative power (see Figure 6) and robustness with respect to the parameters (N,τ)(N,\tau) (see Figure 7). As a broad range of effective models is often known in practice, this middle ground may be ideal for certain applications.
∘\circ 
Interpretability. The decision threshold α\alpha can be interpreted probabilistically, if the prior is well-calibrated to the deployment conditions: it controls the average risk of false decorrelation over the prior (Lemma 14), which in turn controls the bias on the sampled exit distribution, via Proposition 11. It can therefore be chosen a priori by setting a target error level, rather than calibrated system-by-system as the Gelman–Rubin α\alpha seems to require. We stress that this interpretation is heuristic in practice, and this guarantee is lost under prior misspecification (see Figure 10).
∘\circ 
Failure under misspecification. When the RNN diagnostic’s assumptions do not hold, it may fail optimistically, falsely and confidently declaring decorrelation (see the case Dd=1D_{d}=1 in Figure 14, where ξ¯\overline{\xi} is not a slow variable of the system). At present, the diagnostic has no mechanism to detect that inputs lie outside the support of its training distribution. If such a mechanism existed, a simple mitigation would be to apply the RNN diagnostic in several collective variables concurrently (just as the Gelman–Rubin diagnostic does, see (139)), and discard predictions from out-of-distribution trajectories. As demonstrated in Section 4.4.6, a simpler heuristic mitigation consists in taking the most conservative prediction across multiple CVs.
∘\circ 
Choice of collective variables. In our experiments, we restricted ourselves to one-dimensional collective variables. The same method should nevertheless remain practical as long as the honest decorrelation times t0​(ℒZ,Z0)t_{0}(\mathcal{L}^{Z},Z_{0}) can be computed cheaply for ℒZ\mathcal{L}^{Z} in the training distribution. For grid-based quantizations of an mm-dimensional CV ξ¯\overline{\xi}, the size of 𝒵\mathcal{Z} scales as dgridmd_{\rm grid}^{m}, which should make training a model possible for m∈{2,3,4}m\in\{2,3,4\} at most, if matrix-free methods are used for the computation of reference decorrelation times and coarser values of dgridd_{\rm grid} are considered. Many complex systems require higher-dimensional CVs to accurately resolve the global landscape of metastable states. However, the CVs used in this method are only required to describe decorrelation within a single, metastable state. In other words, the collective variable ξ\xi may change from state to state. We therefore expect in many cases this local description to involve fewer effective degrees of freedom than the global landscape. Statistical methods for the automatic detection of slow variables have been developed (in particular TICA [43] and its variants), which may be combined with our approach in future work.
Several directions are left open for future work.
∘\circ 
Coarse-graining for killed diffusions. The analysis of Section 2 is at present restricted to finite-state jump processes. A natural extension, relevant to the MD context, would be to extend the result to elliptic diffusions killed at the boundary of a domain, possibly in a small-parameter adiabatic regime analogous to the setting of Section 2.6. We expect this to be a mathematically interesting problem. A subsequent step would be the analogous result for hypoelliptic diffusions, whose spectral properties are much more delicate to analyze.
∘\circ 
Accelerated MD simulations on large systems. We plan to implement the RNN diagnostic for higher-dimensional CVs, and to deploy it in the generalized parallel replica algorithm to test its usefulness for the accelerated simulation of larger glassy materials and biomolecular systems.
∘\circ 
Relaxing Assumption (B1). The purpose of Assumption (B1) is to justify the use of a prior over Markovian dynamics. It is however possible to enrich the training prior with non-Markovian effective dynamics, in the form of low-dimensional projections ξ⁡(Y)\xi(Y) of some higher-dimensional process YY. If YY is not very high-dimensional (say of dimension 33 at most), honest decorrelation times for YY should remain computable. A few examples from genuinely high-dimensional systems could also be incorporated, where computationally intractable honest decorrelation times could be replaced with expensive approximations, as in Section 4.4.
∘\circ 
Relaxing Assumption (B2). The finite-NN implicit error model learned by the RNN is fitted on discrepancies between η^t,NZ\widehat{\eta}^{Z}_{t,N} and ηtZ\eta^{Z}_{t} within the training distribution. Its deployment to ξ⋆​η^t,NX\xi_{\star}\widehat{\eta}^{X}_{t,N} is at present not justified by the mean-field analysis of Section 2. Proving effective dynamics results at the level of paths of the Fleming–Viot process would address this gap.
∘\circ 
Relaxing Assumption (B4). We plan to explore automatic methods to define the prior ρdyn\rho_{\rm dyn}, for instance by calibrating it so that synthetically generated features are indistinguishable from traces ξ⋆​η^t,NX\xi_{\star}\widehat{\eta}^{X}_{t,N} observed on the target system, using classification-based methods (see [25]). In an accelerated simulation setting, this procedure could be deployed online, recalibrating the prior periodically as new metastable states are visited.
